# AshenOath The Road Between Crowns


A novel written for Rafa



# Prologue


## The Painted Crow


The first thing Oren stole that morning was his mother's good black paint.

Sella found the open pot beneath the wagon, next to a brush made from three of her own hairs and a feather he had almost certainly taken from the shrine. The boy lay on his stomach in the dust, tongue between his teeth, painting a bird on a piece of brass.

"That paint is for the cart."

"There's enough."

"You haven't seen the cart."

"It's right above me."

She crouched. The brass thing was no bigger than the bottom of a cup, pierced at one edge. Its face had gone green in the hollows. The bird was good. That annoyed her more than if it had been bad, because she had meant to be thoroughly angry and now would have to be accurate.

"Come out of there. Bram will run over you."

"Bram's eating."

Across the yard Bram Kett lifted the heel of his breakfast loaf in confirmation. Crumbs caught in his beard. He wore the expression of a man who had survived sixty years by staying out of arguments between women and their children.

"That's not yours either," Sella said, pointing at the brass.

"The road men said I could have it."

The road men had said no such thing. They had spent yesterday afternoon standing beside a hole with their spades lowered, calling to one another in voices that had made Sella stop loading the wagon. Then the foreman had put the little metal things in a meal sack and paid Bram to take them to Greyfen's keeper. Oren had been sent away. He had the uncommon talent of looking obedient while remaining very close.

Sella put out her hand.

He looked at the bird, then at her.

"I'll give it to her myself."

She almost took it. The sound in the hole had troubled her, and the extra coin, and the foreman's refusal to put his mark in Bram's book. Now Oren had paint on his nose. Her right shoe pinched. They were late.

"You will," she said. "And you'll tell her you painted on it."

"It's a crow."

"I can see it's a crow."

He smiled then, quick and pleased, because he had heard what she had tried to leave out.

She pulled him out by an ankle and made him wash. The water in the basin darkened. A little black remained beneath his thumbnail, where he had scratched something through the paint.

"What's that?"

"There was writing. I couldn't see it properly."

He showed her. Four letters lay below the bird's belly, newly cleared with a nail.

AVEL.

"A person's name?" he asked.

"Likely."

"Why would they put it in the road?"

She had no answer worth giving. "Ask Anwen."

He threaded red cord through the hole and tied it badly. She untied it and made a knot that would hold.

They took the north road with a pair of salt barrels, a crate of iron fittings, six bundles of shrine feathers Sella had bound herself, and the meal sack of brass tokens tucked beside Bram's feet. The wagon made one long complaint descending the yard. Oren waved to the empty window of the room they rented. He always waved, though there was no one left at home to wave back.

Sella sat on the tailboard with her boots clear of the wheel. The cart lettering needed doing before winter. So did the room's shutters. There was a crack in the washing jug, and Oren had grown out of his good shirt while somehow wearing through both elbows. If the salt sold well she could buy cloth and a second blanket. If it sold badly she would turn the shirt cuffs again and pretend it was a fashion he had invented.

He had begun asking about apprenticeships. He wanted a place where there were books, but also a forge, and perhaps a boat. She had told him that was three professions. He had said he would try them before deciding.

In the first mile he described the boat.

In the second he changed his mind about the forge.

By the third Bram had offered him a profession which involved walking ahead and being quiet.

"You'd miss me," Oren said.

"I'd learn to bear it."

At Greyfen's threshold they waited while a woman with two water pails argued with a gate worker. A board had been put across the safer footpath. The woman said she could either climb over it with the pails or pour his water into the ditch, and he ought to choose while it was still his water. Bram laughed. The gate worker moved the board.

Sella bought an onion pastry with a bent coin. She broke it unevenly and gave Oren the larger piece, then regretted it when he ate only the middle.

"The crust is the part I paid for."

He offered it back, damp with the generosity of children.

She ate it. Warm onion stung the cut at the corner of her mouth.

Anwen was changing a dressing when they reached the shrine. She made them wait beneath the porch while she finished. Oren looked up at the bell tower. No rope hung in the porch where a rope ought to have been.

"Did somebody steal it?"

"Probably a boy who wanted to make a boat," Bram said.

Anwen came out carrying a basin. She knew Bram, and asked after the axle he had been promising to replace since spring. Sella gave her the bundles of feathers. Oren began his confession before anyone could introduce him.

"I painted on one. But I didn't paint over the name. I made the name better."

He held up the token.

The basin struck the porch post. Water washed across Anwen's shoes.

For a moment Sella thought the woman had hurt herself. Then Anwen set the basin down very carefully.

"Where did you find it?"

Bram told her about the road works. He spoke more slowly as he noticed her face. The pastry had left a shining patch of grease on Oren's chin. Sella wiped it away with her thumb. His skin was warm and entirely ordinary beneath her hand.

Anwen took them inside.

The room smelled of boiled linen and old stone. A row of little shoes stood near the hearth, drying, none of them paired correctly. Oren went to put two together. Anwen caught his wrist before he could touch them.

"Please. Sit here."

He sat. Sella moved closer to him.

Anwen opened the sack and looked at the tokens without counting them. She held Oren's up beside the lamp.

"Avel," she said, so quietly that Sella almost missed it.

The flame went out. Farther back in the shrine, a door struck its frame once. Sella turned toward the sound. She could see no open window, but old houses had drafts in unexpected places. Anwen put the token down and relit the lamp with a shaking taper.

She asked Bram the location of the hole, whether bones had been found, whether the foreman had read any names aloud. When he could not answer the last question, she asked it again.

"He paid us to bring them," Sella said. "That's all we know."

Anwen nodded. She went to a cupboard and took out a spool of gray thread.

"Is this worth something?" Bram asked.

"I wish it weren't."

Oren watched her winding the thread around his token. "You can still see the crow."

Anwen's fingers stopped.

"Yes," she said. "I can."

She told Sella to remove the boy's damp coat. There was a place near the fire where it would dry. She gave them hot water with something bitter in it and asked them to drink. Sella tasted willow and a sweetness that did not belong. Oren made a face. Anwen smiled at him with her mouth alone.

Outside, someone knocked a stick against a gate. Three blows. A pause. Three again.

Anwen went to the window. Sella could see only the side of her face.

"We can stay," Sella said.

The words surprised her. They had deliveries to make. Bram would complain about the time, and the room rent was due at the turn of the month. But she wanted her son's coat back. She wanted to be outside. She wanted, contradictorily, to remain where the woman with the thread seemed to know what was happening.

"The road will be easier before rain," Anwen said.

"You haven't told us what these are."

Anwen looked at the cup in Sella's hands. "People's names."

"I can see that."

The keeper flinched. It was a small movement, as if Sella had reached across the table.

"I need to find a safer way of handling them. I'll send word. Keep the sack closed. And the thread. Don't let him untie the thread."

"Then keep the sack."

For the first time Anwen appeared to consider an answer she could not bring herself to give.

"Take it as far as the old stones. I will come after you."

She said it with enough urgency that Bram stood. Sella did not. The keeper put a hand on her forearm.

"I am trying to keep him safe."

Sella looked down at the hand. There was a crescent of dried blood beneath the thumbnail. It might have come from the dressing. It might have been Anwen's own.

"Then tell me what to do if it isn't safe."

Anwen opened her mouth. The stick struck the gate again.

"Stay together."

They left with the sack. Oren's coat remained beside the fire. Anwen lent him a shawl, saying she would bring the coat when she followed. He complained that the shawl smelled of turnips, which made Bram laugh so suddenly that he caught himself coughing.

At the wagon Sella checked the knot she had made in the red cord. Gray thread crossed it now, tight as a small wound. She thought of cutting it and leaving the brass on the porch. But Anwen had told her not to untie it. The woman knew something. That was the worst of it: Sella had no knowledge with which to disagree.

The sky lowered over the road. Bram coaxed the mare along the rutted shoulder. Oren sat beside his mother, no longer talking about his boat. Through the shawl she could feel him shivering.

"Are we in trouble?"

"No."

"You say it like when we're in trouble."

She put an arm around him. "I'm cross about the paint."

He leaned against her, relieved to be guilty of something he understood.

They had gone perhaps a mile into the trees when the mare stopped.

Bram clicked his tongue. The animal's ears flattened.

Somewhere off the road a branch moved against the wind.

Oren lifted the corner of the shawl to look at the crow. He had been careful with the eye, a tiny point of unpainted brass. Even in that light it shone.

"Mam," he said, "do you think it'll dry?"

She pulled the shawl over his hand.

"Keep it covered."

Under the wagon, something dragged slowly through the leaves.


# Part One The Road of Crows



# Chapter 1


## A Hunter's Price


The cart had lost a wheel in the gate, and everyone in Greyfen had an opinion about whose fault it was.

Kael stopped on the road with his pack pulling at the seam of his shoulder. The carter blamed the stone threshold. A gate worker blamed the carter. A young man in a green coat blamed the original inventor of wheels, who had, he said, clearly failed to consider mud.

A woman stood beyond them with two full water pails. She had the patient, dangerous expression of someone whose work had already been delayed once that morning.

"You could help," she told the man in green.

"I've been helping. I've made him less lonely."

"He's about to be a lonely corpse if I can't get through."

Kael put down his pack. "Where's the jack?"

They looked at him.

"At the smith's," the carter said. "Broken."

Kael examined the wheel. Its hub had cracked along an old repair; the rim rested in a clot of clay. The cart carried root vegetables, a bed frame, and what appeared to be the entire contents of somebody's marriage tied beneath a faded rug.

"Unload that end."

"I only just loaded it."

Kael looked at the man until he began untying the rug.

They used a fence rail and the edge of the threshold. The young man took the long end of the rail with Kael. On the other side, the woman set down her pails and braced the wheel. It took two attempts. On the first, the carter tried to save a rolling cabbage instead of the pin, and Kael acquired a bruise against his thigh. On the second, a square-shouldered man arrived carrying a wooden block and told them they were all holding the damned thing wrong.

He was correct.

With the block beneath the axle, the cart steadied. The water carrier pulled her hands free and stood examining a torn nail.

"Siv," the young man said, "your assistance has been a comfort to us all."

"You can carry a pail."

His smile thinned, but he took one.

The man with the block crouched at the damaged hub. Metal filings shone in his hair. "That repair was meant to get you home."

"It did," the carter said.

"Then you were meant to bring it to me."

"I did. Look where we are."

Kael picked up his pack before he became a party to the argument.

The young man intercepted him with Siv's pail hanging from his hand. "You're the hunter."

"There may be more than one."

"There's one with a sword, a bow, and no wish to tell me his name. Fits the description. I'm Rook. That's Tor, who will charge him twice now. You've met Siv. She thinks very highly of me."

Siv, already several steps ahead, did not turn around. "Bring it."

"Anwen's expecting you," Rook said. "Shrine by the square. Don't go to the tower. The door bites."

"Doors do that here?"

"Only the expensive ones."

He hurried after the water carrier.

Greyfen climbed a low rise between wet fields. Its houses leaned toward one another, their upper rooms projecting over the lanes as if exchanging secrets the people below were tired of hearing. Kael smelled yeast, damp wool, and a drainage ditch that needed clearing. Someone was singing badly behind a shutter. Two children had cornered a hen beneath a bench and were bargaining with it.

He counted three exits from the square. One led to the gate behind him, one to the mill road, one north beneath the trees. A broken sign swung over a baker's door. He stopped there before the shrine.

The woman behind the counter looked at his boots, then at the muddy print he had already made on her floor.

"Wipe them on the way out as well."

"What have you got?"

"Bread. It's the large clue."

He bought a heel and a bowl of thin stew. She watched him count his coins. Her apron was dusted white along the hips where she had wiped her hands, and one sleeve had been singed close to the wrist.

"Anwen pays," she said.

"Not yet."

"She'd better. I'm not feeding another man who has come to be mysterious in our square."

He sat near the door. The stew contained beans, a little onion, and a piece of meat whose history he decided not to investigate. He ate slowly at first because he had been hungry long enough to know what would happen otherwise.

An older woman came in with a street broom. She leaned it beside his chair and asked the baker for the bread that had stuck to the trays.

"There isn't any," the baker said.

"Nessa."

"There isn't any for free. There is some in exchange for telling me why you haven't mended that boot."

The woman lifted her heel. A flap opened at the sole.

"Ventilation."

Nessa fetched a bundle from beneath the counter. She added a small roll while pretending to look for string.

"For your house, Bera. Not the pigeons."

"The pigeons are cleaner."

Bera took the bundle and reclaimed her broom. Her fingers were knotted at the joints. As she passed, she looked at the sword resting against Kael's leg.

"Can you do anything about rats?"

"With this? It would be expensive."

"Everything is."

She went out. Kael finished his stew.

The shrine was warmer than he expected. He had known keepers who confused holiness with a cold room. Here a kettle worked on the hearth and a row of mugs stood ready, some chipped, one with a handle repaired by wire. Anwen was kneeling in front of a little girl, tying a strip of linen around her wrist.

"It won't stop itching just because you look offended," she said.

The girl looked more offended.

Anwen sent her away with a piece of apple. Only then did she turn to Kael.

He had expected an old woman from the hand in the letter. The keeper was perhaps in her middle forties. She had a long face, dark hair caught untidily back, and an abrasion on one knuckle that had opened again. A gray thread stuck to the hem of her sleeve.

"You came."

"Your letter offered twelve silver."

"Eight."

"Twelve if there was proof."

She nodded, as though the difference had escaped her and she was relieved to have it made clear.

He disliked that. People who wanted something frightened them seldom needed the price repeated.

"Sit."

He set his pack beside the door, within reach. Anwen noticed but said nothing. She gave him hot water, then took three objects from a drawer: a folded bill, a red feather, and a child's coat.

The coat had been brushed clean. One cuff had been let down, leaving a pale band where the fabric had not weathered. A new button waited beside it on the table, unsewn.

"Bram Kett," she said. "Carter. Sella Vey. She makes shrine bindings, does small trading. Her son Oren. Twelve. They came through six days ago. They should have been back the next afternoon."

"Where were they going?"

"North. The old stones, then the woodland road."

"Who saw them last?"

She smoothed the coat with her palm.

"The gate worker saw the wagon leave."

"And before the gate worker?"

"They were here."

"What for?"

"Bram had things from the road repairs. Old things. Names. He was bringing them to be identified."

Kael waited.

"Tokens," she said. "People once carried them when a clerk had to record them. Some were brass. Some iron."

"Were they valuable?"

"To somebody."

"I mean valuable enough to kill for."

She folded the coat, then unfolded it. "I don't know."

He asked about the road. Anwen described shapes beside the trees, a patrol that had gone in and returned without its pack mule, the sound of a cart moving at night when no cart arrived. She did not call the shapes ghosts. He was grateful for that much.

"Anyone searched?"

"Rook went as far as the turning. Tor went with him. They found the mare."

"Alive?"

"Yes. Cut about the legs. It wouldn't let them lead it north."

"The patrol?"

"Vargan men. They told us the road was closed."

Kael touched the folded bill. A freight list: salt, fittings, binding feathers. A small addition in a different hand named a sack of recovered metal. No signature accompanied the addition.

"You want them found."

"I want to know what happened to them."

"Those may be different contracts."

Anwen looked at him properly then. For a moment the tired politeness left her face.

"I know."

He drank. The water tasted faintly of apples, as if the mug had held something better earlier.

"Four now. Four when I return. Another four for proof you can put a name to. I won't promise they're alive. I won't drag a whole cart out of a marsh for the same money. If there's a living person to bring back, the cart waits."

"Agreed."

She said it too quickly. He thought about raising the price, then despised himself for thinking about it and was irritated with her for having made him notice.

Anwen counted four silver onto the table. They came from separate little bags. One was labeled in a school hand. He did not read the label.

"The boy's coat," he said. "Why is it here?"

"It was wet. I lent him a shawl."

"What color?"

"Brown. With a yellow line near the edge."

"Does he answer to anything besides Oren?"

Her mouth moved before the sound came. "His mother called him Trouble. When she wasn't angry."

Kael wrote that down as well.

Outside, Rook was waiting with a loaf under one arm and a short coil of rope under the other.

"She told you about the mare?"

Kael nodded.

"It was Bram's father's mare before it was his. Or so he said. By his account that animal should have been older than the gate."

"Where is it?"

"Toma's fold. Wouldn't settle anywhere else."

They walked toward the north lane. Rook seemed incapable of walking without looking at what other people carried. A basket of eggs, a torn sack, a parcel tied in blue. He greeted some of their owners. Others he allowed to pass in peace.

"Bram bought my first bad purchase off me," he said. "Thirty-seven left gloves. Told me he knew a place where people only worked with one hand."

"Did he?"

"No. He sold them as polishing cloths. Made a profit. I was furious when I found out."

At the turning, Rook handed over the rope.

"There's a break in the old wall after the second beech. The ground's better there. Don't follow the fresh cart ruts unless you've tested them. And if you see something with boards strapped over its face, put the weapon down before you make the carters' sign."

He raised empty hands, crossed them at the wrists, then opened them slowly.

Kael watched. "You tried that?"

"Once. It stopped. Tor thought it was deciding which part of me to eat. I didn't stay to ask."

"Why would it know a carters' sign?"

Rook looked past him toward the woods.

"I don't know."

It was the first answer that morning Kael entirely trusted.

He checked the bowstring under his cloak. He had six good arrows, two he would use only if he had to, and a knife with a loose rivet. The sword needed sharpening close to the hilt. His boots were still damp from yesterday.

Rook offered the loaf.

"Nessa said you'd paid too much for the stew."

"She didn't."

"No. But I like to imagine a better world."

Kael took it. Past the last house, the road narrowed beneath the trees. He turned once to ask whether Bram had been carrying a lantern.

Rook was already gone. A customer had called his name, and ordinary business had reclaimed him.

Kael stood for a moment with the bread warm against his palm. Then he broke off a piece, put the rest away, and went north.


# Chapter 2


## The Broken Cart


The first arrow missed because Kael was watching the wrong shadow.

He heard the leaves move behind the wall and turned toward them. Something came out of the ditch on his other side. He loosed too quickly. The shaft struck the old beech at shoulder height. Then he had the bow across a mouth full of stones and human teeth, and the weight of the thing drove him backward into the wall.

Its breath smelled of stagnant water.

He let his knees fold. As it fell over him, he drove the knife beneath its jaw and rolled clear. The knife stayed in the creature. One long hand struck the earth beside his face. It had too many joints in two of its fingers and a band of rusted metal where its wrist ought to have narrowed.

Kael got the sword free.

He did not rise at once. The thing dragged itself around, worrying at the knife with a broken nail. Its other hand opened and closed, palm upward, with a clerk's small, impatient gesture.

"Two," it said.

The word came through a throat that had no business producing words.

Kael moved left, keeping the beech behind his shoulder. The creature followed the noise of his boots. He tossed a loose stone against the wall to his right. Its head snapped toward the sound. He cut behind the knee, stepped clear of the sweeping arm, and brought the sword down before it could turn again.

The body collapsed into the ditch. For several seconds its hand continued that little demand.

Then it stopped.

He waited, breathing through his nose, listening beyond his own pulse. Water moved somewhere below the road. A crow called once. There were no footsteps.

He retrieved the knife. Dark matter clung to the blade, fibrous rather than flesh. Beneath the dead creature's ribs he could see a piece of animal bone fitted where no animal would have carried it. Ghoulkin. He had seen one outside a burned hospice years ago. That one had tried to rock a stone to sleep.

He cleaned the knife on moss and made himself look around before he looked down at his shaking hand.

The wall ended at a washout. Beyond it, the cart stood across the road with its rear wheels in the ditch. One shaft had broken. The other pointed into the trees as if the wagon had been trying to turn. There were no birds on it.

Kael studied the ground from the shelter of the beech.

The mare's tracks went south, uneven and deep. Human footprints tangled beside the wagon. Some were booted. Some were bare and too long. A pair of narrow grooves led away from the tailboard into the undergrowth. Several yards farther on, those grooves divided around a stone and joined again. Something had been dragged by more than one carrier, or had been moved twice.

He did not follow them yet.

A long shape stood between two trees beyond the cart. It was so still he might have mistaken it for a split trunk if its shoulder had not lifted when he looked away.

He looked back. It stopped.

The Stalker had a pale seam where an eye should have been. Its forelimbs hung almost to the ground, bent backward at the wrist. Kael kept his face toward it and reached slowly for the fallen arrow.

The shaft was lodged too firmly in the beech.

"Of course," he said.

He left it.

For the next ten paces he walked sideways. When he glanced at his footing, the creature moved. He heard the rush, brought his eyes up, and found it twice as close, limbs locked in the middle of a motion it had not finished.

He had fought things that hunted scent. Things that followed heat. This was worse because his own tiredness had become part of the ground he had to cross.

The cart's rear wheel was higher than his waist. He backed toward it, keeping the Stalker in view. There was a loose salt barrel beside the wheel. He nudged it with his heel until it rolled across the road.

The creature's head followed the barrel.

Kael stepped behind the cart.

He heard the rush again, fast and low. It struck the spokes where he had stood. The wheel shuddered. He thrust through the gap above the hub and felt the blade meet resistance. The creature wrenched sideways, pulling the sword against the wood. He released one hand before it broke his wrist, braced a foot against the rim, and dragged the blade free.

It withdrew into the trees with no cry at all.

Kael stayed crouched, waiting for another attack. A drop of sweat ran into his eye. He wiped it away with the back of his hand and counted his breaths until they slowed.

The cart smelled of spilled salt and spoiled onions. A smear of black paint crossed the tailboard. He found the pot beneath it, almost empty, its lid pierced by a small nail. Nearby lay a brush made from hair tied to a feather.

He took the brush.

Under a collapsed sack he found Bram's ledger. The leather cover was swollen with rain. Several pages had stuck together. He separated the first with the tip of his knife and recognized the freight list Anwen had shown him. Farther in were records of ordinary journeys: lamp oil, barley, a borrowed mallet returned late. Beside one debt someone had drawn an exceptionally ugly face.

A wet boot protruded beneath the front axle.

Kael set the ledger down. He crouched until he could see past the broken shaft.

Bram lay on his side beneath the wagon. His beard was full of mud. One hand still held the end of a leather rein. The injuries left no question, but Kael checked the neck because habits were worth keeping when thoughts became unpleasant.

He found the carter's small purse tucked inside his shirt. Three coins and a button. Whatever had happened here had not begun as robbery.

Something knocked against the other side of the cart.

Kael went still.

Through the spokes he saw a foot wrapped in strips of hide. Above it rose a bent body carrying two boards strapped across its chest. A third board covered part of its face. The wood had old freight marks on it. Nails protruded from the edges.

The creature dragged a length of chain.

Kael had the sword raised before he remembered Rook's hands.

He lowered the blade onto the ground. He was careful to make no scraping sound. Then he came slowly upright, showing both palms.

The chain stopped moving.

He crossed his wrists and opened his hands.

For a long moment the creature did nothing. Through the broken face board he could see a wet, moving darkness. It lifted its own hand a little, then let it fall.

The Stalker shifted in the trees beyond it.

Kael fought the need to snatch up his sword. He held the gesture. A muscle in his shoulder began to twitch.

The board-wearing creature turned its body sideways. The movement opened a narrow space between it and the cart.

Kael did not mistake the space for friendship.

He picked up the ledger with one hand and the sword with the other, keeping the blade low. He moved through the opening. The creature followed him with its head, but the chain stayed on the ground.

At the far edge of the washout he stopped to listen. He could still hear the Stalker. He could not hear the thing that had begun moving behind the salt barrels.

The first warning was the cart rising.

A great shoulder lifted the broken shaft. The wagon tilted toward Kael, and he jumped for the wall as the barrel rolled down the slope. Wood smashed against stone. He landed badly, caught his left forearm on a broken iron fitting, and felt warmth run into his sleeve.

The Brute came around the cart wearing a yoke across its upper body. Straps had sunk into the material of its neck. It carried the wagon's broken shaft in one hand. Where the other arm ended, something like a root gripped a lump of stone.

Kael ran along the wall. The shaft struck behind him and burst against the stones. He heard the board-wearing creature retreat through the leaves, its chain rattling now. The truce had bought a passage, nothing more.

At the end of the wall the ground fell toward a narrow watercourse. Kael could not outrun the Brute on the road. He could not climb the far bank quickly enough with his arm wet. He turned toward it instead.

The thing charged.

He waited until the yoke filled his sight, then stepped down into the watercourse. The wooden crosspiece struck the gap in the wall and caught. The Brute's momentum drove its chest against it. For a moment it could not raise its arm.

Kael climbed the lower stone beside it and cut where the straps entered the back of its neck.

It twisted. The sword struck the yoke. His injured arm nearly failed him.

He let himself fall into the ditch before the stone hand swept over his head. Mud filled his mouth. The Brute heaved backward, dragging the yoke loose. He crawled beneath the collapsed bank, where the roots gave him a moment's shelter.

His right hand had begun to ache in the old scars.

He pressed that hand against the exposed root. He held one small, exact intention in his mind: the space between himself and the thing that would kill him.

Heat gathered in his palm. It hurt before it lit.

The Oathfire caught on the dry heart of the root and ran outward in a thin blue line. The Brute recoiled. Kael shoved through the opening beneath its arm and came up on its unprotected side. This time he had room for the stroke.

The creature sagged against the bank. The root hand gripped and released the stone. He struck again before it could recover.

When it fell, it took part of the bank with it.

Kael stood in the water, bent over his sword. His palm had blistered. The cut on his forearm was bleeding steadily. He had used fire to earn perhaps three seconds, and his body would collect payment for the rest of the day.

He tore a strip from the cleanest part of his undershirt and bound the arm with his teeth. Then he went back to the cart.

There was no victory in the clearing. There were damaged things and the work he had agreed to do.

He found Sella farther along the drag marks, half hidden by bracken. The red feathers lay scattered beside her. One hand was closed around the yellow-edged shawl. He covered her face with the unbloodied part and sat on his heels until he could stand without swaying.

Oren was not beside her.

Kael searched the ground slowly. A smaller set of marks led toward the marsh, broken by the prints of creatures. The boy's red cord had caught on a thorn. Its brass token hung below it, wrapped with gray thread. Flattened wire crossed the thread in two hard turns.

He cut the thorn rather than the cord.

On the brass, beneath a painted crow, someone had scraped four letters clean.

He wrapped the token, thread and wire together. He took feathers, the ledger, Bram's purse, and the small brush. He marked the places of the bodies on a page torn from his own book. It was all he could carry safely.

Before leaving, he laid a stone beside Bram's head. He knew it did nothing for the dead man. He needed the ground to look a little less accidental.

He had almost reached the beech when the Stalker moved again.

Kael turned his face toward it and walked backward, one careful step at a time, until the road bent between them.


# Chapter 3


## What We Brought Back


Mira would not let him tell the story until she had cut his sleeve open.

"You can talk while you do it," Kael said.

"You can faint while I do it. I'd rather know which you're planning."

She had taken one look at the blood on the baker's threshold and brought him through a side door into a room full of drying plants. Nessa followed with a basin, complaining about the floor. A broad man rose from a stool beside the hearth. A little girl slept on the pallet behind him with one fist tucked beneath her cheek.

"Toma, put the lamp here," Mira said.

The man obeyed. His gaze moved from Kael's sword to the bundle of red feathers in his hand.

"The road?"

Kael nodded.

Toma opened his mouth, looked at the sleeping child, and closed it again.

Mira worked the strip of cloth free of Kael's arm. She had thick dark hair twisted up with a wooden pin and a narrow burn across the back of one hand. There was a little flour on her collar. He wondered whether she knew it was there.

"You're fortunate," she said.

"It doesn't feel like fortune."

"The fitting missed the tendon. Feel ungrateful after I've cleaned it."

He watched her lay out a needle, a small bottle, and two folded squares of linen. Everything went in the place her hand expected to find it. She did not waste motion, but neither did she hurry when there was no use in hurrying.

"This will sting."

It did more than that. He held the edge of the stool with his good hand.

"You could have been more precise."

"You'd have moved."

Nessa set a cup in front of him. "Drink. If you put your blood in my stew, I'll put my stew in your wound."

He drank. It was watered beer, cold and sour enough to make his eyes ache. Mira's mouth curved without interrupting her work.

When she reached for the needle, he looked at the sleeping girl again. Toma stood beside the pallet as though his body could prevent something from reaching it. The child's breathing was too quick.

"How old?" Kael asked him.

"Ten."

"Name?"

"Pell."

Mira glanced up, then back at the wound. "She's cooler than she was this morning. Let her sleep, Toma."

He had not touched the child. His hand had only begun to move.

Kael knew that sort of watchfulness. He looked away before it made him unkind.

Anwen arrived while the last stitch was going in. Rook came behind her. Tor stopped in the doorway, filling it with his shoulders.

"Not here," Mira said.

"I only need—" Anwen began.

"Not beside Pell."

The keeper looked at the pallet. Her face changed. "Of course. I'm sorry."

She waited outside with the others while Mira finished. Kael let her bind his arm. When she took his right hand to examine the blistered palm, he tried to pull it away.

"That as well," she said.

"It'll settle."

"Perhaps it will settle beautifully if we clean it first."

She turned his wrist toward the lamp. The old scars formed pale, uneven lines above the new burn. She did not ask what his oath was or whether he had kept it. Most people asked one of those things, believing either question entitled them to the answer.

Instead she said, "Next time, use your left hand for the hot things."

"You just stitched it."

"Then you'll have to stop finding hot things."

He almost laughed. It hurt him unexpectedly that he almost laughed.

***

In the shrine, Anwen had cleared the table. Oren's coat was no longer on it.

Kael unwrapped the ledger first. He laid Bram's purse beside it, then the feathers, then his own rough map. Rook reached for the ledger and stopped before touching it.

"Bram?"

"Under the cart."

Rook nodded once. His face seemed to be waiting for another version of the answer.

"Sella was farther from the road. I marked where. I couldn't bring them out alone."

Tor bent over the map. "We can take a hurdle. Two men at each end."

"Not until morning. There's a Stalker near the turning. And things following sound."

"Then you come."

"Yes."

Rook sat down hard enough to scrape the chair across the floor.

"The boy?" Anwen asked.

Kael put the oilskin bundle on the table.

The keeper did not touch it. She knew what it was before he opened it. That settled one question.

"I found this by the marsh track. Small drag marks. No body I could identify."

Anwen closed her eyes.

"Don't make that into a promise," he said. "I haven't found him. That's all it means."

She opened them again. "I understand."

Rook rubbed both palms against his knees. "He kept changing the sign on Bram's wagon. Put a beard on the mare once. Bram drove through three villages before somebody told him."

No one answered. Rook looked furious with them, then with himself.

Kael showed Tor the wire.

The smith held it close to the window. "Drawn, then flattened. Two passes. I've seen this in Vargan stores. They use it on sealed parcels, when they want to know if a box has been opened."

"Sold elsewhere?"

"Sometimes. Stolen, too. Wire doesn't stay loyal to its buyer."

"There's more around the broken cart."

Rook looked up. "The patrol."

"Possibly," Kael said.

"They went there."

"And we need to know when."

"Does it matter when they tied a child up?"

The room tightened. Anwen made a sound and covered it with a cough.

Kael kept his voice level. "I found wire around an object. I haven't found it around the boy. I won't tell you a thing I haven't seen because it would give us somebody to hate this afternoon."

Rook's ears reddened. He stood, walked to the hearth, and leaned one hand against the stone. The other was shaking.

Tor put the wire down very gently.

"The thread?" Kael asked Anwen.

She looked at the gray strands beneath it.

"Mine."

"Why?"

"To quiet what was in the token."

"What was in it?"

"Memory. A name that had been covered. I thought I could hold it long enough to find a safer rite."

"You sent it out on the road."

Her hand flattened on the table. "Yes."

"Did you tell them what they were carrying?"

"I told Sella to keep the thread tied."

"That's not what I asked."

Anwen stared at the little black bird. "No."

There was the answer, ugly and insufficient, among all the objects he had brought back. Kael found he preferred it to the explanation he could see her wanting to give.

"We'll come back to that," he said.

"You think I—"

"I think you know more than you've told me. I also think you're frightened enough to make a poor witness to your own choices. I want the boy's trail before the rain ruins it, and I want to know why a dead thing in those woods held out its hand for two of something."

Tor glanced toward the cemetery hill.

Anwen saw the glance. "There are older fragments near the graves. Sometimes they catch on an object. A sound. The bell used to help keep them where they could be heard safely."

"Used to?"

She pulled her sleeve down over the abrasion on her knuckle.

"The rope was cut."

Rook laughed once without amusement. "The rope was cut. Funny, how it cut itself."

She looked at him. He held her gaze for a moment, then looked back into the hearth.

Kael gathered the token and its bindings. Anwen put out a hand.

"Leave it here."

"I'll keep it wrapped. You can examine it with me present."

Her hand stayed in the space between them, then withdrew.

"The families need the account," Tor said.

"They'll have it. Names of the dead, what I saw, where we can bring them from. The rest waits until we know what it means. I'm not putting a patrol murder on the square's board on the strength of a piece of wire."

"Nor what she did?" Rook asked.

Kael looked at Anwen. "She can tell people what she did. I won't do her lying for her."

The keeper swallowed. "I'll tell Sella's neighbors. And Bram's family. Today."

"I'll go with you," Tor said.

It was impossible to tell whether he offered company or supervision. Perhaps he did not know either.

Anwen opened the drawer beneath the table. She brought out eight more silver coins.

"Take them."

Kael counted them with his eyes. Twelve in all, with the morning's payment. Enough to replace his knife and buy a week of food if he walked south tomorrow.

He could still do that. The contract had asked for an account, not a cure. Bram and Sella were dead. Oren's absence had a likely explanation. He had seen men charge twice as much for less certainty and sleep soundly afterward.

Through the window he could hear somebody arguing over onions. A child shouted that the hen had escaped again. Tor's thumb rubbed at a black mark on the ledger, trying to decide whether it was paint or dirt.

Kael knew exactly how far he could walk before nightfall. He pictured the bridge south of the fields. There was dry shelter beneath its far arch if the water had stayed low. No one there would know his name.

He put four coins into his purse. He left the other four on the table.

"I haven't accounted for Oren."

"You don't owe us—"

"Don't tell me what I owe you."

Anwen fell silent.

The sharpness embarrassed him. He rested his good hand on the back of the chair until it passed.

"I'll stay," he said. "I'll find what I can. And while we sort out this account, I'll help keep the people carrying it alive. That isn't a promise that nothing bad will happen. It's what I'm agreeing to do."

Rook turned from the hearth.

Outside, the cemetery bell rang.

One stroke, so low that Kael felt it in the legs of the chair before he heard it. The mugs along the shelf trembled. A spoon fell from the lip of the basin.

Tor was already at the door. Rook followed him.

Anwen remained beside the table, her face drained of color.

"There's no rope," she said.

***

Nessa gave him the room above the flour store. It contained a bed, a basin, and a window that could be shut only by moving the bed.

"You can have fresh air or privacy," she said. "We don't charge extra for the choice."

He moved the bed.

She set a bowl down on the chest. There was a whole onion in the stew this time, and more meat. He looked at it, then at her.

"Tor says you're going back tomorrow."

"Yes."

"Then eat. And don't bleed on the pillow. It isn't mine."

After she left, Kael took off his boots. The cut throbbed under Mira's dressing. He set the wrapped token on the chest beside the bowl, a hand's reach from the bed.

He ate half the stew before he realized he was waiting to hear a child's voice from inside the oilskin.

There was no voice. Downstairs, Nessa dropped a tray and swore. Someone laughed. He finished eating while the room was still warm.


# Chapter 4


## A Widow's Bell


They brought Bram and Sella home on doors.

Tor had wanted hurdles, but the willow frames were at the mill and nobody wished to wait. He took the back door off an empty house, then the door of his own shed. He complained about the hinges until Rook told him he was allowed to be unhappy without pretending it was about the hinges.

After that they worked quietly.

Kael led them through the safer ground beside the wall. He had slept badly and eaten because Mira had stood over him until he did. His arm held. Rook carried the rear of Bram's door, his coat folded away to keep it clean, a precaution he abandoned before they reached the turning.

The Stalker watched from a distance. Kael never took his eyes from it for long. Once Rook began to turn his head toward the trees and Kael said, "Keep walking," so sharply that nobody spoke again until they saw the first roof.

People came out as they passed. They knew before anyone told them. A woman put a hand to her mouth. The carter from the gate took off his cap, realized he had nowhere to put it, and stood with it clutched against his stomach.

There was no third door.

Anwen met them at the square. She had laid clean cloths in the room behind the shrine. Tor and Rook carried Bram inside. Kael remained outside while two women took Sella. One of them touched the red feathers he had returned, then held them so tightly that the shafts broke.

He heard Anwen begin to speak.

He did not stay to listen. She had said she would tell them, and there were ways of watching another person confess that made the watching a cruelty of its own.

At the pump he washed mud from his boots. The water was so cold it hurt his teeth to look at it.

"You've brought my brother's door back," a woman said behind him.

He turned. She wore a dark wool dress with a patch at one elbow and carried a bell small enough to fit in both hands.

"Was it his house?"

"Before he married someone who disliked the roof. He lives south now. They both dislike that roof as well."

She looked at the water running down the channel.

"Elna," she said. "Tor told me you'd need somebody who knows the cemetery before the keeper improved it."

"Improved it how?"

"New names on old stones. Old names on no stones at all. A very tidy arrangement if you don't happen to be under it."

She handed him the bell. Its clapper had been removed.

"Harl's. My husband's. He wore it on his cart when the road was crowded. He thought people ought to get out of his way before he reached them."

"Were they wise to?"

"He was a terrible driver."

The sudden fondness in her voice made the next words difficult. He waited for her to choose them.

"He died last winter. I wanted the bell put beside him. Anwen said iron near those graves could trouble the fragments. I thought she meant people might steal it. Now our great bell rings without a rope, and I would like someone to explain the difference."

Kael turned the little bell in his hand. Beneath the patina, close to its lip, a row of marks had been filed almost smooth.

"Who made this?"

"It was old when Harl bought it. Everything he bought was old. He claimed that meant somebody else had already tested it."

"Will you show me his grave?"

She took the bell back. "If you don't make a solemn face the whole way. I've had enough solemn faces to last me."

The cemetery lay above Greyfen, behind a low chapel whose stones had darkened with damp. New graves occupied the sunniest ground. Older ones leaned toward the marsh. A yew had pushed one wall outward, leaving a gap wide enough for a child or a determined man.

Tor waited at the gate with a coil of chain. He looked uncomfortable in clean clothes.

"For the bell," he said before anyone asked. "If we need to stop it moving."

Elna raised an eyebrow. "You could ask it politely."

"I tried that with your husband."

She made a short sound that was almost a laugh.

Kael let them go ahead while he studied the ground. There were prints between the graves, recent and irregular. Some ended without turning. Small chips had been knocked from three headstones. Near the chapel door, the grass lay flat in a circle.

"Don't step there," he said.

Tor stopped with one boot raised.

"Why?"

"I don't know yet."

The smith put his foot down somewhere else.

Elna led them to a grave with a plain stone and a surprisingly neat border of winter herbs. Someone had laid a pair of worn boot laces across it. She saw Kael notice them.

"He'd put my shoes by the hearth before I woke. One morning he put them too close. Burned a hole through the left heel and spent the rest of the week insisting that my feet were different temperatures."

She knelt to move a fallen leaf from the herbs.

"That's what I remember first. It bothers me that it isn't something grander."

"It doesn't have to be."

"People say that. Then they wait for the grander thing."

Kael crouched beside the neighboring stone. Its name had been cut shallowly over an older inscription. The newer letters said that the man beneath it had died in a late winter seven years ago. The older date was thirty-two years past.

"Was this moved?"

Elna leaned closer. "It wasn't here when we married. There used to be a timber marker."

"Whose?"

"Len. He kept the lantern at the north gate. I remember my mother sending food to his wife."

Tor's fingers tightened around the chain.

A sound came from the far side of the stone: a small scrape, followed by the hiss of a lantern being lowered into wet grass.

There was no lantern.

The air above the grave clouded. A bent figure appeared in pieces, sleeve first, then hand, then the suggestion of a face that would not stay arranged. It held an arm out at shoulder height. The other hand kept searching its belt.

Elna stepped backward. Tor caught her elbow, and she shook him off without taking her eyes from the grave.

"Hold it higher," the figure said.

Kael stayed crouched. "Len?"

The searching hand stopped.

"Hold it higher. They're still out there."

A sensation moved through Kael's body, brief and sickening: cold iron beneath his palms, the pressure of other bodies behind him. He was at a gate and above a grave at the same time. Someone was pushing a lantern toward him. There was a sound beyond the timber that might have been rain if he had not heard it for so long.

He took his hand off the stone.

The cemetery returned. Tor was asking him something. Kael had to listen twice before he understood.

"Do we strike it?"

"No. Stay back."

The figure began its motion again, reaching for a belt that was no longer there.

"Can it hear us?" Elna asked.

"It caught the name. I don't know what else it catches."

"Ask him about Harl."

Kael looked at her.

She pressed her lips together. "They worked at the same gate."

He could have asked. He could have treated the fragment's repeated words as an answer to whatever question troubled her most. He had seen people do that and come away convinced that the dead had judged them.

"This isn't all of Len," he said. "It may never have been."

"I didn't ask for a lecture."

"I know."

She turned her face away. Tor studied the little bell in her hands.

They found a second marker beside the yew. Its base had been turned so the inscription faced the wall. Tor levered it upright while Kael kept watch. Beneath the dirt lay a short list of household names and the word RECEIVED. Below that, where another line should have been, a chisel had taken a bite from the stone.

The same four letters appeared halfway down the list.

AVEL.

Kael opened his pouch without unwrapping the token completely. The painted crow looked absurdly cheerful above the grave names.

Tor glanced from one to the other. "The boy copied it."

"He uncovered it."

"Does that matter?"

"It mattered to something."

Elna had gone back to Harl's grave. She stood with her hands around the bell, rubbing its rim with one thumb. When Kael approached, she spoke before he could.

"He was young."

"Who?"

"My husband. At the gate."

Kael waited.

"He did useful things afterward," she said. "That ought to be allowed to remain true."

"I haven't said otherwise."

"No. You haven't said anything. It's a habit men have when they want somebody else to do the difficult part."

She was right often enough that he did not answer immediately.

"I need to know what happened here," he said. "You don't have to tell me everything you remember about him."

"I couldn't if I wanted to. Some days I can't remember the sound he made when he cleared his throat. Then a stranger does it in the lane and I hate him for an hour."

She looked down at the bell. "Take it. Find out why they filed the marks off. Bring it back when you can."

He accepted it without promising a time.

The chapel door moved.

Tor turned toward it. "Wind?"

The air was still. A pebble rose from the flattened grass and struck the wood from the outside. Then another. The sound thickened as if something below the ground were clearing a passage.

Kael pushed Elna behind the yew.

From within the chapel came the deep beginning of a bell stroke. The tower answered a moment later. Beneath it, among the graves, something unfolded from the shadow of a stone.

It was larger than the opening from which it seemed to have come. Its back rose in a curve of plates and old metal. A hollow in its chest narrowed and widened. With each movement the air pressed against Kael's ears.

Tor lifted the chain.

"Back," Kael said. "Not through the center. Along the wall."

Harl's little bell was vibrating against Kael's palm, answering the tower with a faint singing of its own despite its missing clapper. The thing turned toward it.

Kael wrapped the bell in his cloak. The creature stopped. He could see it listening.

They moved along the wall one at a time. Tor went first, then Elna. Kael kept the covered bell against his body and placed his feet carefully between the stones.

At the gate, the smith's chain struck the latch.

The creature opened its chest.

The sound knocked Kael to one knee. Elna fell against the gatepost. Tor dragged it open, and Kael pushed them through before another stroke could come.

They stumbled down the path. At the first bend, Elna bent over with her hands on her knees. She was breathing hard, furious and frightened, her hair coming loose.

"I wanted," she said between breaths, "to leave a bell beside my husband."

Tor looked back at the hill. "We'll need to change how we do that."

She began to laugh. It sounded dreadful for a moment, then like a woman laughing. Tor gave a startled answering snort.

Kael stood with the bell wrapped against his ribs and waited until they were ready to walk.


# Chapter 5


## The Rope


Tor did not sleep. By dawn he had made three iron wedges, repaired a length of chain, and fitted a temporary striker inside Harl's clapperless bell. He had also decided that Kael's plan was foolish.

"You didn't have to spend all night on it to decide that," Kael said.

"I hoped it would improve."

They stood in the smithy while rain tapped the roof. Tor had drawn the cemetery on a plank with charcoal. He had made the chapel too large and the yew too small. Kael corrected neither. The distances between the gate, the broken wall, and the flattened circle mattered more.

"It turns toward a struck bell," Kael said. "It stopped when I covered Elna's. It needs to gather itself before it makes the sound."

"So we give it a sound to turn toward."

"On the far side of the wall. You pull the chain from here. I approach behind it."

"And when it turns the other way?"

"You stop pulling."

Tor looked at him. "That's the foolish part."

Mira arrived carrying folded cloth, wax, and a basket of bottles. She listened to the plan while wrapping waxed wool around the handle of the little bell.

"Put this in your ears," she said, handing them each a piece of soft wax. "You still need to hear one another. Don't push it all the way in."

"Will it help?" Tor asked.

"Some."

"How much?"

"More than asking me again."

Anwen waited outside. She carried a lantern with no flame in it and had tied her sleeves tightly above the wrists. Kael saw the fresh dressing on her scraped knuckle. She had mended the boy's coat during the night. When he had called at the shrine that morning, the new button had been sewn in place.

He wondered what else she did when she could not sleep.

At the cemetery they set the chain through a hole in the wall. Tor fixed the wedges between two stones so the little bell could swing without being seen from the center. Mira stayed near the gate with a clear path back toward the village. Anwen went with her.

"What happens to what is inside it?" Kael asked the keeper.

"If its shape is broken, the memory spreads. Into earth, roots, anything that can hold it."

"Can you gather it?"

"Some. Not all."

"Then keep people out afterward. Don't tell them it's safe because we've finished fighting."

Anwen nodded. She did not say that she already knew.

Kael went in through the gap by the yew.

The thing stood beside the chapel, folded low over itself. In the gray morning he could see objects caught between its plates: buckles, scraps of wire, the crushed bowl of a handbell. A dull bronze key hung from a loop near its throat. There was nothing there that resembled an eye.

He moved behind the larger headstone. His bandaged arm throbbed where the sword's weight pulled at it. He shifted the blade to his right hand and raised two fingers toward Tor.

The little bell rang beyond the wall.

The creature lifted.

It crossed the grass with a dragging, uneven gait. Its chest narrowed. The loose pieces of metal along its body began to tremble.

Kael came around the stone and drove the sword beneath a lifted plate.

The blade went in farther than he expected. The creature folded around it. He pulled, felt it catch, and let go just before the plate closed on his hand.

The little bell stopped.

For a moment there was only rain.

Then the hollow chest turned toward him.

Kael threw himself behind the stone. The stroke hit the other side. Soil jumped from the grave. Something struck his shoulder, and he realized the top of the headstone had broken away.

He spat grit and reached for his knife.

From the wall came Tor's muffled shout. Kael could not make out the words. The creature moved again. It had his sword lodged in its side, bright metal shifting among the old dark pieces.

He had been wrong about the gap. It opened to take things in.

The bell rang a second time. The creature began to turn, then stopped. It had learned where Kael was.

"Keep it ringing!" he shouted.

He ran toward the chapel, away from the cover he knew. The creature followed. Its body dragged across the graves and left furrows in the wet grass.

The chapel steps were narrow. He took them two at a time, hit the locked door with his shoulder, and turned as the thing reached the foot of the steps. Its plates lifted to gather another stroke.

He jumped down beside it.

The sound struck the door above him. The wood cracked from latch to hinge. He landed hard on one knee and drove the knife into the exposed inner seam of its chest. This time he did not try to cut through. He used the knife as a lever and reached for the hilt of his sword with the injured hand.

Something deep inside the creature took a breath through several human throats.

Kael planted his boot against the plate and pulled.

Pain tore up his forearm. The sword came free. He fell backward onto a grave and rolled as the hollow swept over him.

Tor's chain caught across the creature's shoulders.

The smith had come through the gate.

"Pull it down!" Kael shouted.

Tor braced the chain around a stone. The creature dragged him half a pace. His boots cut trenches through the mud. Kael reached him, caught the free length with his good hand, and hauled sideways.

One of the wedges held. The other tore out of the wall.

The creature tipped. The great hollow struck the edge of Harl's neighboring stone with a sound like a dropped pot.

Kael scrambled up its lower side. The lifted plates could not close while its weight rested on them. He drove the sword into the seam and shoved with both hands until it struck something solid beneath.

The creature convulsed.

He did not hear its last stroke. For several seconds he heard nothing at all.

When sound returned, Mira was calling his name. He lay on his back in the mud, Tor beside him. The smith still held the chain. His teeth were clenched and his eyes wet.

"Let go," Kael said.

Tor stared at his hand as if it belonged to someone else. Then his fingers opened.

Across the grass, small pale impressions moved and vanished: a bare foot, the edge of a coat, a hand held out with two fingers raised. Anwen knelt beside the damaged grave, speaking names from the old marker. She did not pretend to know the ones she could not read.

Kael sat up. His sleeve had gone red again.

"I told you to stop finding hot things," Mira said, pressing cloth against the arm.

"This one wasn't hot."

"A useful distinction. Hold that."

Tor began to laugh. It came out as a cough, then a laugh again. Kael looked at him and found himself doing the same. Mira waited until they had finished, which took longer than either man wanted.

***

The bronze key opened the lower lock. The upper part of the chapel door had already split.

Inside, the air was cold enough to turn breath white. The bell rope lay coiled on the floor. Its cut end was clean. Shelves along the wall held books wrapped in cloth, each bundle tied shut. Near the steps to the tower, someone had scrubbed a dark mark from the stone and failed to remove its outline.

Anwen stopped there.

"Evin," she said.

Kael waited beside her.

"He was seventeen. He could mend a sleeve so you couldn't see where it had torn. He couldn't keep a plant alive if you stood over him with a watering can."

She looked at a narrow shelf beside the door. A dead plant stood on it, its stalk still tied to a little support.

"He heard a name from one of the books. A name we'd covered. I had told him the chapel was closed, but he had the key. Of course he had the key. He worked here."

"Last winter?"

"Yes."

The keeper rubbed her knuckle against her skirt. "I found him under the rope. He'd tried to ring the bell. I don't know if he was calling for help or answering something. He was gone before I got to him. The bell kept moving. I cut the rope because I couldn't make it stop."

Kael looked up into the tower. The great bell hung motionless. He could see where the old mechanism had been repaired with newer iron.

"What did you tell people?"

"That he fell."

"Did he?"

"Yes. That was how I managed to say it."

She picked up the rope. It had left a cleaner curve on the floor beneath it. "I put the books back. Changed the bindings. Made the room quiet. People needed the shrine, and I knew how to give them a room they could come into. I didn't know how to give them the rest."

Kael thought of the letter that had brought him here, its careful hand and specific sum. She had been able to ask a stranger to look for three people. She had not been able to say what looking might find.

"And the boy's token?"

Anwen closed her fingers around the rope.

"I thought I'd learned enough."

Mira came through the doorway. She had heard that much. Kael saw the keeper notice her, and the shame of being heard by a person whose respect mattered more than his.

"We'll need the old bindings," Mira said. "The ones you replaced. If we're going to understand what happened on the road."

"I burned some."

Mira said nothing.

"There may be copies in the woodland register," Anwen added. "Household fragments. I don't know how much survives."

She looked as though she wanted the suggestion to count for something. Mira let it remain a useful suggestion, without making it forgiveness.

They spent the afternoon securing what could be secured. Tor braced the broken door and made a temporary catch. Anwen separated the books with readable names from those that still gave off pressure when touched. Kael carried the latter into the lower stone room one at a time, with the keeper beside him. He stopped whenever either of them heard anything.

There were quicker methods. Anwen described them. Opening the bindings might release the pressure at once, but would spread what they could not gather. Redirecting it could quiet the chapel at another place's expense. For now they chose a temporary containment around the damaged room, marked its limits, and left a way to release it deliberately when they understood the debt.

Anwen made the small promise of maintenance herself. Kael promised to help inspect the failing boundary. Neither spoke for the village.

When they finished, Tor placed Evin's little dead plant outside in the light.

"Won't bring it back," he said, catching Anwen watching him.

"I know."

He looked embarrassed. "The pot's sound. Somebody might use it."

Anwen sat on the step and began to cry. It was an inconvenient, ordinary crying, the kind that made her nose run and prevented her from saying what she meant. Mira sat beside her without touching her at first. After a while the keeper leaned against her shoulder.

Kael carried the cut rope outside. He laid it where it could dry, away from the footprints, and went to help Tor close the gate.


# Chapter 6


## The One Who Returned


Nera came into Greyfen on foot beside a wagon that had one wheel wrapped in rope.

She was holding the rim away from the axle with a hooked bar. Every few steps she leaned her weight against it, swore softly, and told the driver to move again. Two passengers walked behind the wagon carrying a third between them on a chair. The third passenger objected to being carried. His objections grew more elaborate as they approached the square.

"Put me down and I'll show you," he said.

"You'll show us a broken hip," Nera replied. "We've seen one."

Kael stood outside Nessa's with a cup in his hand. He had been ordered to keep the arm still for an hour. He watched the wagon, looked down at the cup, and put it on the windowsill.

"I can take that end."

The woman did not look at his face. "Can you hold it without slipping?"

"Yes."

"Then do."

He took the bar in his right hand and braced it against his hip. She moved to the wheel, checked the rope, and called to the driver. The wagon creaked forward. Kael felt the strain through his whole body. Nera kept her fingers clear of the rim with a practiced little curl of the hand.

"Stop."

The driver stopped. She wedged a block under the wheel and let out a breath.

Only then did she look at Kael.

He saw recognition arrive. He knew it because he had seen it before in less expected places: the moment a face ceased to be a face and became something remembered against its owner's wishes.

He searched her features for the earlier woman.

Her hair was pulled back tightly. A pale scar crossed the edge of her lower lip. There was mud on one cheek and a strip of blue cloth knotted around her belt. She had the sun-darkened skin of someone who worked outdoors whether she wanted to or not.

"You," she said.

The bar felt heavier.

"Have we met?"

She looked at his hands. The old marks on his right wrist were visible where the sleeve had pulled back.

"Yes."

Before he could ask more, something shrieked beneath the wagon.

Nera dropped to a knee. Kael set the bar against the block and crouched beside her. A small animal had wedged itself between the undercarriage and a hanging basket. Its flat back was plated in overlapping scales, copper-dark along the ridge. One hind foot was caught in the basket's leather loop.

It opened its mouth at them. There were plenty of teeth, all small, none reassuring.

"That's new," the driver said.

"You've dragged a creature halfway across the parish and only now noticed?" Nera asked.

"It wasn't there when I loaded."

"Perhaps it bought passage. Kael, don't touch the foot. Lift the basket."

She knew his name.

He lifted. The animal thrashed, scraping the wood with its forefeet. Nera slid a folded cloth over its head and took the trapped foot between two fingers. She waited for it to stop struggling before she loosened the loop.

It bit through the cloth.

"Bad bargain," she told it. "You need me."

Kael held the basket until his shoulder shook. At last the loop came free. The creature dropped into Nera's arms, twisted, and landed on his boot.

Heat soaked through the leather.

"Hearthscale," Nera said. "Young."

"How young?"

"Young enough to make poor choices about wagons."

It climbed his trouser leg.

Kael caught it before it reached the bandaged arm. Its underside was softer than the plates suggested, warm and smooth, with tiny muscles working beneath the skin. It tucked its head toward his blistered palm and made a low, vibrating sound.

Nera watched him hold it. Whatever had come into her face at the sight of him did not leave, but something else had to make room beside it.

"You can't carry it while we unload," she said.

Nessa appeared with an empty flour basket. "Put it here. If it eats the basket, you owe me for the basket."

The hearthscale climbed out immediately.

"Expensive passenger," Nessa said.

They spent the next hour unloading. Kael carried sacks with his good arm until Mira appeared and took one out of his hand without a word. He protested. She opened the top of his dressing, showed him the new red patch, and held his gaze until he moved to work he could do seated.

Nera gave him the passenger list.

"Names against the things they actually arrived with. Don't mark a blanket because one was promised. Mark it when somebody puts it in their hands."

He looked at the columns. Person. Household. Place of arrival. Goods received.

At the far right was a narrow space for the return.

His thumb rested on it too long.

"Can you read it?" Nera asked.

"Yes."

"Then start with him. He's been telling us he can walk for six miles. He can prove it by walking to your table."

The old man in the chair came over between his two companions, cursing Nera with considerable affection. Kael recorded a blanket, a change of linen, and a small pot which the man refused to let anyone else touch.

"My wife's," he said.

Kael left it at that.

Nera checked every fastening before the empty wagon was moved to Tor's yard. She inspected a strap the driver assured her was sound, found the tear beneath the buckle, and handed it to him without comment. He looked more ashamed than if she had shouted.

When the work was finished, she washed her hands at the pump. Kael waited nearby with the list. The hearthscale had settled against a warm stone at his feet, one injured hind leg held slightly clear of the ground.

"Nera," she said, without looking up. "Since you don't remember."

The name reached him before the face did.

A different road. A wagon on its side. Rain bouncing from the rim of a shield. Someone with a blue strip tied around a belt, hauling at a wheel while other people shouted behind her.

He looked at the cloth she wore now. It was not the same piece. Of course it was not the same piece.

"The eastern crossing," he said.

She wrung water from her hands. "Eight years ago."

He set the list on the pump ledge. He wanted to put something between them and hated himself for noticing the wish.

"I remember."

"You remember a road."

He had no answer he trusted.

She dried her hands slowly. Her scar whitened as she pressed her lips together.

"What did your report say?"

He looked past her toward the yard, where Tor and the driver were arguing over which of them owned the failed repair. Ordinary noise carried clearly in the cold air.

"That the road couldn't be held."

"It couldn't."

The agreement took him by surprise. He almost began to explain. Then he saw her expression and stopped.

"What did it say about afterward?" she asked.

The memory changed. No rain now. A low room. His own hand cramped around a pen. Somebody telling him the casualty list could be amended when better figures came in. He had written neatly. That remained one of the things he remembered most clearly.

"Nera—"

"I'm asking about the report."

"I wrote what I knew then."

She looked at him for several seconds.

"No," she said. "You wrote where you stopped knowing. There's a difference."

He felt anger arrive, quick and useful, offering him a way to avoid the rest. He gripped the edge of the pump until it passed.

"There were orders."

Nera turned away and picked up the list.

"I didn't ask you for them."

She checked the entries. At one name she frowned, went back to the old man, and asked him whether the second blanket was for him or the person sharing his room. When he answered, she corrected the column. Kael stood where she had left him.

The hearthscale climbed onto his boot again.

***

That evening the animal stole a piece of fish from Nessa's kitchen and hid beneath Kael's bed to eat it.

"Your friend," Nessa said, standing in the doorway.

"I haven't agreed to that."

"Neither have I. Yet here we are."

He knelt to retrieve it. Two bright eyes watched him from behind his pack. When he reached under the bed, the creature moved just beyond his fingers.

"It can stay by the ovens," Nessa said. "There's warmth. I'll put a board over the flour."

"You said it was expensive."

"I say many things. Most of them are true."

He looked at the small foot, still held awkwardly. It would be easier to leave the creature here. Nessa would feed it. It would learn the kitchen and become somebody else's nuisance.

He put down a strip of clean cloth. After a while it stepped onto it, cautiously, then climbed into the warmth of his palm.

"It came out of the bracken," he said.

"So did you. I'm not naming you after it."

"Bracken."

Nessa rolled her eyes. "Well, he'll have no doubts about the effort you put in."

He made a place for the animal beside the hearth with a folded sack and a warm brick. It circled three times and settled with its head tucked beneath one plate. The vibrating sound began again.

Later Kael went down to the yard. Nera sat on an upturned bucket, mending a buckle by lamplight. He stopped at the edge of the light.

"Do you need a hand?"

"I need two sound hands. You're down to half of one."

"I can hold the lamp."

She considered him, then nodded toward the hook. He took the lamp and held it where she could see.

For a while there was only the needle moving through leather.

"I didn't come here for you," she said.

"I know."

"I'm going north when the load is ready. People are waiting for it. Some of them have already been moved twice. If you're going to keep this road useful, we can use the same stretch. If you're going to ask me to make you feel better, find another yard."

He kept the lamp steady.

"The woods are worse off the cart track," he said. "But the old walking path is passable. I can look at it before you take a load."

She drew the thread tight. "Look with me. I need to know what you call passable."

He agreed.

When the buckle was mended she took the lamp from him. She did not invite him to sit. He went back upstairs, where Bracken had left the warm brick and occupied the middle of his pillow.

Kael moved the creature onto the sack, lay down, and stared at the dark ceiling. Below him, Nera was still working. He could hear the small, regular sound of her hammer long after he had run out of things to tell himself.


# Chapter 7


## Under Green Water


Pell had drawn a picture of the thing that lived beyond her father's fold.

It had six long arms, a smiling face, and a hat.

"It doesn't wear a hat," Toma said.

"I know. I thought it might like one."

Kael looked at the drawing. Pell had used the back of a feed bill, filling the empty space around the creature with flowers considerably larger than the sheep. She lay propped on pillows in the kitchen, wrapped in a blanket that kept sliding off one shoulder. Her fever had eased enough for her to be cross with everyone.

"Does it have that many arms?" he asked.

"Sometimes."

Toma reached for the paper. Pell held it away from him.

"You said I could draw."

"I said you could rest."

"Drawing is resting."

Mira set a cup beside the pallet. "Arguing isn't. Drink half, then you can tell us what else it does."

Pell smelled the cup and looked betrayed.

"Half," Mira repeated.

The girl drank with elaborate disgust. Bracken raised his head from a patch of sunlight near the hearth and watched her. She extended a foot beneath the blanket. He settled against it, which improved her opinion of the morning.

Outside, Toma shut the kitchen door quietly.

"It's never hurt her."

Kael looked toward the fold. Two sheep stood as far from the lower fence as their enclosure permitted. Beyond the fence the ground dipped into reeds.

"What has it hurt?"

Toma picked at a flake of paint beside the latch.

"Foxes. Something bigger last spring. A ewe went missing. I don't know if that was it."

"When did Pell meet it?"

"Three years ago. She followed a lamb down to the water. The bank gave way. I heard her calling, but I couldn't get to her from the path. There was something in the reeds. It brought her up."

"Brought her how?"

Toma held out his arms, then lowered them. "I didn't ask it."

His embarrassment became anger before it could become shame.

"I was glad she was alive. That's what I knew. She was coughing water into my coat, and the thing went back where it came from. What would you have done?"

Kael glanced at the kitchen window. Pell had pressed the drawing against the glass to show Bracken, who could not see it from inside.

"Probably been glad. Then I would have looked at the bank."

"I did."

"And?"

"I kept the sheep away from it."

Mira folded her arms. "You didn't tell me."

"You'd have sent somebody."

"You don't know what I'd have done."

Toma looked at her. "Wouldn't you?"

She did not answer quickly enough to satisfy him.

The farmer led them to the lower fence. He had reinforced it with scraps of timber, leaving one narrow opening at the far end where a person could pass sideways. Beside the opening stood a chipped bowl with the remains of bread in it.

Kael looked at the bowl.

"Pell leaves things," Toma said.

"How often?"

"I leave them for her now."

From the reeds came a soft snapping sound. Something rose above the sedge, a confusion of branches with patches of old cloth caught in it. For a moment Kael could almost make a human shape. Then it shifted and the likeness failed.

It extended one limb toward the bowl.

Toma took a step forward. Kael held out an arm.

The limb brushed the fence. The wood darkened where it touched. A sheep bleated behind them. The shape turned toward the sound, and all the branches moved at once.

Kael pulled Toma back. The reaching limb struck the fence hard enough to break a rail. It did not pursue them. It dragged the bowl into the reeds and disappeared.

Mira picked up the broken rail. Its inner grain had gone damp and gray.

"You can't leave that opening," she said.

Toma stared at the gap. "It saved her."

"I believe you."

"You say that like it doesn't matter."

Mira put the rail down. "It matters that it saved her. It matters that it nearly took your hand. I need you to help me with both."

He rubbed his face with his palms. For a long while no one spoke.

They marked a new boundary farther up the slope, where the ground was dry. Toma would move the fold before dusk. Tor could help make a gate with no feeding gap. The reeds and their inhabitant would remain for now, disclosed to the people using the path and watched from a distance. No child would be sent to thank it. No bread would be left to encourage it closer.

It was an arrangement none of them entirely liked. Toma agreed to it because he had seen the rail break, not because Kael had told him what gratitude ought to mean.

When they returned to the kitchen, Pell asked whether the thing could keep her drawing.

Toma sat beside her. "We'll keep it here."

"You think it's bad."

"I think I don't know it as well as I thought."

She considered this, then slid the paper beneath her pillow as if the creature might be offended to find it on the table.

***

Mira's herb beds lay beyond the fold, on a strip of ground sheltered by willow. She went there to gather supplies for the marsh. Kael followed carrying the empty basket, under strict instructions not to lift anything that would require another stitch.

The dark soil was unusually rich. The plants nearest the old stone boundary had thick stems and glossy leaves. In a separate bed farther upslope, smaller roots struggled in paler earth.

"Those are the clean ones," Mira said, seeing where he looked.

"Clean of what?"

She knelt beside the lower bed. "Ash."

He waited.

"The old deposits run beneath this ground. I knew there had been burning here. I knew some of the ash came from funeral work. I didn't know whose."

"Did the people you treated know?"

Her hand stopped above a leaf.

"Not all of them."

From the fold came Pell's voice, raised in an argument about whether she could sit outside. Mira listened to it until the girl laughed at something Toma had said.
