"The roots work quickly," she said. "With some fevers that matters. The upper bed takes longer to grow and the preparation is slower. When there's a child burning through the blankets, I've chosen the thing I know will lower the heat."

"And chosen what to tell the father."

Mira looked up at him. "Yes."

He had expected defensiveness. The plain answer left him with fewer useful places to put his own discomfort.

"Did you put it on my arm?"

"No. Willow, spirits, clean linen. You were lucky enough to need things I could explain without losing my nerve."

He turned the basket in his hands. He liked her. He liked the steadiness of her fingers, the impatient intelligence, the way she had spoken to Pell without turning the child into a frightened little saint. He wanted the answer to have been kinder to that liking.

Mira pulled two roots from the upper bed. They came free with too little earth clinging to them.

"I will tell Toma," she said. "This morning. He can choose the slower preparation if that's what he wants. We can keep watching her."

"Can you do that for everyone?"

"Not with this bed."

She stood, brushing dirt from her knees. "That's the work. More clean soil, more plants, someone to tend them while I'm elsewhere. I haven't done enough of it."

He handed her the basket.

"Don't look relieved," she said.

"I wasn't."

"You were beginning to. Confession isn't the same as having the roots ready."

It stung because she had seen him accurately. He followed her back to the house and waited outside while she spoke to Toma.

He heard the farmer raise his voice. Then he heard it lower. When Mira emerged, her face was pale.

"The upper bed," she said. "He wants to try it. I told him what to watch for and when to call me."

"And Pell?"

"Asked if the medicine would taste better."

Kael smiled despite himself.

Mira looked away, then laughed once, tiredly. "It won't."

***

They left for the marsh after midday. Bracken stayed at the fold with strict instructions for Pell not to put him in the bed. Kael suspected that instruction would last until they reached the lane.

The old stones stood beyond a belt of alder. Anwen had shown them the place on a map and had wanted to come. Mira told her to remain with the people arriving at the shrine and to find the woodland register references. It was not a punishment, though Kael could see Anwen hearing it as one.

He carried the token in its oilskin. Mira carried Moon Oil in a stoppered bottle, wound cloth, two coils of light line, and a lunch that Nessa had described as unsuitable for people determined to die hungry.

At the first water channel they stopped to oil the exposed metal of Kael's blade. The liquid smelled of crushed leaves and bitter rind.

"It shows certain bindings more clearly," Mira said. "It doesn't tell you what to do with them."

"Nothing ever does."

"Some things do. Rotten bridges, for example."

He looked at the plank across the water. One end had sunk into the bank.

They went around.

Beyond the alders, the marsh opened into low green pools divided by narrow ridges. The surface of the nearest pool shivered although no wind crossed it. A line of pale shapes rose beneath the water, then sank before Kael could decide whether they had been fish.

Mira touched his sleeve and pointed to the bank. Small depressions marked the mud: one, then another, then a longer drag. Beside them lay a torn scrap of brown wool with a yellow line near its edge.

He crouched. This was the continuation of the trail from the wagon. The water had damaged it, but the direction was clear.

They followed it to a stand of reeds taller than either of them.

A voice said something on the far side.

Kael held up a hand. Mira stopped.

The voice came again, high and thin, with a questioning rise at the end. He could not make out a word. It was followed by a heavy movement through shallow water.

He thought of the paper on which he had written the boy's name. Twelve. Brown shawl. Mother calls him Trouble when she isn't angry.

He wanted very badly for the voice to be a living child's.

That wanting was dangerous. He knew it, and it did not lessen.

Mira lowered herself beside a hummock. Her hand found his uninjured wrist.

"Look first," she whispered.

Through a gap in the reeds, he saw the shape standing beside the old stones. Its lower body belonged to the marsh: weed, black water, a swelling mass that shifted as if something underneath were trying to breathe. Above it, a head turned slowly toward a low branch.

Caught on the branch was a small shoe.

Kael stayed where he was until the urge to rush forward had passed. Then he began looking for a way across the ground that would bear his weight.


# Chapter 8


## The Name in the Reeds


The first stone sank when he put his weight on it.

Kael stopped with one foot above the water. Mira caught the back of his belt and pulled him toward the hummock. They waited while mud rose around the stone and covered the place where it had been.

"That one doesn't count," he whispered.

She let go of his belt. "Try not to discover the others with your whole body."

They worked their way along the firmer ridge, testing each patch with a branch. The creature near the stones turned whenever the branch struck water. When they stood still, it settled back toward the low bough and the small shoe.

Kael could see a pale line beneath its outer weed. The Moon Oil on his blade caught a similar light. A binding ran through the thing's upper body and disappeared into the roots behind it.

He pointed. Mira nodded.

Beyond the creature, something brown lay beneath the willow. He could not look at it properly without showing himself.

He took the token from the pouch. The gray thread had loosened where he had cut the thorn. Beneath it, the painted crow's brass eye caught the flat light.

"Oren," he said.

The creature stopped moving.

Kael waited. His voice sounded too loud in the open marsh.

"Oren Vey. Your mother was Sella. You were traveling with Bram Kett."

A small, thin sound came from beneath the weed. It was not an answer. It was the beginning of a question repeated from somewhere that had ended.

The roots behind the creature tightened. Water drew away from the nearest stone, exposing a strip of dark earth.

Mira touched Kael's elbow. They moved together onto the revealed ground.

For a moment he could see the boy. Not standing there, not whole, but in the accidental arrangement of light against the water: a bent head, a hand held close under a shawl, someone concentrating fiercely on a little piece of work. Kael had never seen Oren alive. He could not know which parts of the impression belonged to the child and which his own mind supplied.

He wanted to say something comforting.

Instead he said the name again.

The Wretch's lower body surged toward them.

Kael pushed Mira behind the stone and drew. A limb made of weed and packed silt struck the place where she had stood. He cut across it. The blade passed through the outer growth and caught briefly on something harder beneath.

The creature pulled back, dragging the water with it.

"The left side," Mira called. "Where it joins the root."

He saw it: a narrower strand, bright beneath the oil's sheen, stretched between the Wretch and the willow. He could not reach it without stepping into the deeper pool.

Mira cast a line around the standing stone and held the free end toward him. He passed it around his waist without tying himself fast.

"If I go under, don't follow."

"Then don't go under."

He moved left. The Wretch shifted to block him. He struck low, not to finish it but to make it gather its weight. As the great body sagged toward the blow, he stepped onto a submerged root and cut at the shining strand.

The strand held.

The root rolled beneath his foot.

Cold water reached his ribs before he understood he was falling. He caught the line with his good hand. The sword vanished below the surface. He kept hold of it because the alternative was worse, then felt something close around his boot.

Mira pulled. The stone took the weight. Kael dragged himself upward until he could breathe and saw the Wretch folding toward him.

He thrust the sword between himself and its hollow body. The point met the hard knot beneath the weed. The pressure on his boot eased. He kicked free and hauled himself onto the root, coughing marsh water.

"Kael!"

He looked up.

Mira had climbed onto the lower shoulder of the stone. In one hand she held the opened bottle. She threw the last of the oil across the strand.

It shone clearly now, a thin cord buried in the living mess.

Kael cut once with both hands.

The Wretch came apart unevenly. The upper shape lost its hold; weed dropped into the pool, and the mass below it spread through the water with a heavy sigh. Something hard struck the root beside Kael's knee.

He stayed very still until nothing else moved.

Mira climbed down. Her hands were shaking, and she was angry enough to conceal it badly.

"You were meant to step on the root."

"I did."

"You were meant to stay on it."

He laughed once, coughed, and bent over until the coughing stopped.

Between them lay a small knot of pale material threaded with gray fibers. It gave off no heat. When Kael put his hand near it, the marsh receded for a heartbeat. He smelled onion pastry and wet wool.

He took his hand away.

Mira knelt on the other side. "That's what's left holding the fragment."

"Can we carry it?"

"We can. I don't know what carrying it would do to the next thing it touched."

He looked toward the willow.

The brown shape lay on its side beneath the roots. A bare heel showed at the edge of the cloth. The other foot wore the mate of the shoe caught on the branch.

Kael crossed the ground slowly.

Oren was smaller than the coat had made him seem.

He knelt, touched the boy's neck, and remained there with his hand in place long after there was any reason to keep it there. He had found children alive in worse-looking places. Once, years ago, he had carried a girl out of a collapsed charcoal shed while everyone behind him said to leave her. That memory offered itself now with the cruelty of something true and useless.

Mira came beside him. She did not touch the boy until Kael moved his hand.

"We'll take him home," she said.

He nodded.

They found a place beneath the older stones where the pale knot could rest without being carried farther. Mira explained what she knew of returning a bounded memory. She was careful about the parts she did not know. The knot might settle into the roots. It would not make the roots harmless, nor bring back the mind that had supplied the fragment.

Kael looked at the token in his palm. A child had taken an old name and made a bird over it. He had then scraped the name clear because something in the work had interested him. That small curiosity had mattered more than anyone had understood.

"Here," he said. "Privately."

They set the knot in the hollow and covered it with wet earth. Kael spoke Oren's name. Mira spoke Sella's and Bram's. They did not claim to speak for the older name under the paint, whose story they had not yet learned.

For an instant a question trembled in the air, too faint to make into words. Then the roots loosened. Ordinary water moved between the stones.

The brass token remained in Kael's hand. He wrapped it again. It was an object a boy had made, and he wanted there to remain at least one thing in this place that did not have to perform another task.

***

They made a carrying frame from two alder poles and Mira's spare cloak.

The walk back took most of the afternoon. Kael carried the front with the load against his good shoulder. Mira took the rear. When the narrow path forced them to turn sideways, they spoke before they moved. At a fallen tree they put the frame down, climbed over, and lifted it again. Each small difficulty seemed indecently practical.

Once Mira stopped and said she had to eat.

Kael almost said they could wait until the village. Then he looked at her face and put down his end.

They sat on a log with the frame out of the mud. Nessa had packed cheese, bread, and two hard pears. Mira bit into one and began to cry so suddenly that she tried to hide it by bending over her basket.

Kael looked away. After a moment she said, "Don't do that."

He looked back.

"I don't need you to pretend you haven't seen."

"I wasn't sure."

"Neither am I. Just stay there."

He stayed. She wiped her face with the heel of her hand, irritated with herself, and took another bite of the pear.

"I was hungry," she said. "I've been thinking about lunch for the last mile."

"So was I."

She gave him a disbelieving look. He broke a piece from the bread and ate it. That seemed to help more than anything he could have said.

After a while she asked, "What did you do before this?"

"Before the road?"

"Before people paid you to go where they couldn't."

He watched a beetle climb a blade of grass beside his boot.

"I worked with wood. Poorly. My uncle made chests. I made parts of chests he could put at the back."

"Why did you stop?"

"I was young enough to think there must be better things than getting a lid to fit."

"Were there?"

"There were other things."

Mira turned the pear in her hand. "I've never seen the sea."

He looked at her.

"You asked no question," she said. "I know. I thought I'd answer one anyway. There are plants that live in salt. Whole stretches of them. I keep reading descriptions and deciding the person who wrote them must have left something out."

"They usually have."

"I'd like to see what."

He imagined her on an unfamiliar shore, impatient with a book, hair pulled loose by a wind neither of them knew. The image had no place in what they were doing, which was why he wanted to keep it.

They finished eating and lifted the frame.

Anwen met them beyond the first house. She saw the size of the burden. For a moment she did not move.

Then she walked toward them and took Mira's end.

No one thanked her. Mira let go because her hands had begun to fail.

***

The three graves were dug in the upper ground, away from the damaged chapel and the old markers. Anwen had written out the names in advance and asked the people who knew them to check the spelling.

Nessa brought food nobody wanted until the work was done. Rook told the story of the left gloves. He managed to finish it this time. Someone remembered Sella bargaining over red thread until a trader begged her to take it and leave him his afternoon. Tor said Bram had returned every tool he borrowed, although seldom on the day he promised.

When it came to Oren, there were too many small things. People began at once, apologized, and began again. A drawing on a wall. A question that had lasted an entire market morning. A hen caught successfully and then released because the boy had changed sides in the dispute.

Kael stood at the back. Mira came beside him. Her fingers touched his uninjured hand for a moment, lightly enough that he could have moved away without either of them having to explain it.

He did not move away.

Anwen laid the mended coat where Sella's neighbor could take it to keep with the boy's other things. The new button was slightly larger than the others. Kael noticed it and wished he hadn't.

At the shrine, Anwen counted the remaining four silver onto the table. He took them. Nessa had fed him since he arrived, and the rope they had used had not come from nowhere.

That night, when he reached for his pack, he found the small brush still wrapped inside Bram's ledger. He had forgotten it among the evidence.

He set it beside the token. In the lamplight the hair was ordinary hair, the feather an ordinary feather. The boy had made a tool because he wanted to make a bird.

Kael sat with it until the lamp began to smoke. Then he trimmed the wick and wrote the third name into his account.


# Chapter 9


## No Footsteps Back


Nera put three stones in his pack before they left.

Kael took them out.

She put them back.

"We're testing a carrying route," she said. "People carry things."

"I've got things."

"You've got a spare shirt, a knife, and a creature that believes your pack is a house. That's not a load."

Bracken pushed his head out through the loosened flap and made an offended sound. His hind foot had improved. He used it now to kick one of the stones toward Kael's back.

Nera smiled at the animal. The smile faded when she noticed Kael watching.

He put the pack on.

At the north gate they met Rook, who had bought a pair of boots too fine for the weather and was looking for somebody to admire his sacrifice in wearing them anyway.

"The old footpath runs above the marsh," he said. "Then across the cutting to the tally house. If you reach the fallen ash, you've gone too far."

"Which fallen ash?" Nera asked.

Rook considered the woods. "The distinctive one."

She looked at his boots.

"I'll show you," he said.

They took the narrower way beside Toma's fold. The sheep had been moved uphill. New rails stood where the old feeding gap had been. Toma lifted a hand from the fence and called that Pell was asking for her hearthscale back.

"Tell her he's working," Kael said.

Bracken had gone to sleep.

Beyond the fold, the ground rose through alder and thin birch. Nera stopped often, making marks on a board with a stub of charcoal. She measured the narrowest spaces with the hooked bar she had used on the wagon. When Kael pointed out a shortcut, she examined the slope below it before looking at the path itself.

"Fine going out empty," she said. "Bad coming back with somebody between you."

He looked again and saw the drop at the far edge, half concealed by leaves. She was right.

At the first fork, Rook took them toward a line of cut branches. Nera caught his sleeve.

"Those aren't old."

"Somebody's improved the path."

She crouched beside the nearest cut. The exposed wood was pale. Beneath it, leaves had been pressed into the mud by several boots.

"Or made it look used."

Kael went a little farther without stepping on the track. The branches led downhill toward a patch of ground where water lay just beneath the surface. Someone had thrown brush across it. He tested the brush with his sword. It shifted under almost no pressure.

"Could be hunters," Rook said.

"Could be," Kael agreed. "We still aren't using it."

They returned to the fork. Nera marked the fresh trail on her board with a cross. Below it she wrote a short explanation, not a warning symbol alone.

"For the next person?" Kael asked.

"For me when I'm tired. I won't remember what the cross meant."

The admission was so plain that he found himself thinking about it for the next hundred paces.

Rook left them at the ridge to fetch his grandmother's book from a household near the lower road. Anwen had asked for it after comparing the cemetery names with surviving shrine pages. He disliked carrying it openly. He disliked, more visibly, admitting that he disliked it.

"She used it for accounts," he said. "It's not a sacred relic. There's jam on some of the pages."

"Bring it wrapped," Nera said. "And tell Tor we may need a lever at the house."

"How large a lever?"

"Tell him what we're moving. He'll decide."

Rook went down the slope, swearing when the fine boots slipped. They heard him for some distance after he disappeared.

***

At the cutting, the footbridge had lost its middle rail. A stream ran twenty feet below it between black stones. The bridge deck was wide enough for two people who liked each other and one person who did not.

Kael tested the first plank.

Nera caught his pack strap.

"What?"

"Look at the underside."

He knelt near the bank and leaned out. The deck was newer than the beams beneath it. One beam had split where it rested on the far stone support.

"It'll take one," he said.

"One what?"

He looked at the stones in his pack.

She did not smile this time.

They unloaded beside the bridge. Bracken climbed out and found a warm hollow in the roots of a birch. Kael took off his sword belt. Nera tested the anchoring tree on the near bank, then passed a line around it. They sent the packs over separately, using the line to keep their weight close to the sounder beam. Kael crossed first without a load and placed each foot where he could see the plank meet the beam.

Halfway across, a board gave beneath his heel.

He dropped to one knee. The bridge swung. Behind him Nera tightened the line, but did not shout.

He reached for the next board with his right hand, tested it, and pulled himself forward. A splinter pierced the skin beside his thumb. He ignored it until he reached the far bank.

Nera came across on hands and knees, carrying the hooked bar rather than trusting it to the rope. When she reached him, she sat down abruptly and let her feet hang over the edge.

"That needs replacing," she said.

"The plank?"

"The bridge."

"We can brace it."

"For today's people. Write that down too."

They ate on the far bank while waiting for the others. Kael removed the splinter. Nera took out a piece of bread folded around cheese and inspected it with suspicion.

"Nessa puts onion in everything," she said.

"You don't like onion?"

"I like knowing it's coming."

She ate it anyway. Kael watched the stream through the gap in the bridge.

"Were you always doing this?" he asked.

"Eating things I was promised didn't contain onion? Most of my life."

"Moving people. Finding paths."

She looked at him, measuring whether the question was really about eight years ago.

"No. I used to move cloth. Cloth doesn't argue if you put it on the wrong wagon. It simply goes somewhere else and makes your life expensive."

"Why stop?"

"I didn't entirely. People needing help still need shirts. Someone has to work out whether a cart full of blankets will fit through the place a man on a horse calls a road."

She took a small book from her coat. Kael expected accounts. Inside were drawings of landings, bridges, hull shapes. On one page, between two measurements, someone had sketched a man with a nose so large it occupied most of his face.

"Your driver?"

"He said I couldn't draw him. I said accuracy was beyond me, but enthusiasm wasn't."

Kael laughed. She let the page remain open long enough for him to see the small angry horse beside the man.

"I'd like a boat," she said. "A proper working ferry. Wide enough for a cart, with a landing that doesn't turn into a broken leg every winter. Pay a crew a wage they can actually live on. Put someone else in charge for a day and go fishing without being fetched back because I know where the spare nails are."

"Where?"

"I haven't found the place yet."

She closed the book.

Kael rested his hands on his knees. He wanted to tell her there might be a place. He knew almost nothing about the crossings north of Greyfen, and the wish to say it was more about how he would sound than what he could offer.

He said nothing.

Nera watched the stream for a while.

"There was a bridge after the eastern road," she said.

His stomach tightened.

"I remember."

"No. After. Farther on. You never saw it."

He looked down at the bandage on his arm.

"We had to cross it at night. There was no rail. We tied sleeves together because we hadn't enough rope. A woman kept singing to let the people behind her know where she was. I hated the song by morning. I can still remember every word."

Kael listened to the water below them. He could picture the sleeves, joined at cuffs, wet cloth pulling against skin. He had not pictured them before. His account of that road had ended in the rain where he left it.

"Did you—"

"Don't ask whether we got across. I'm here. You can see that part."

He closed his mouth.

"I need to know what you do when the way ahead is worse than the way you described," she said. "Today. On this road."

He looked back at the bridge.

"We change the description. Then we change the way, if we can. We don't send a load over it because I've already said it would hold."

"Good."

It was not forgiveness. It was approval of a usable answer. He tried to let it be enough for the question she had asked.

They spent an hour bracing the damaged beam from the bank. Kael cut supports while Nera fitted the lashings. They used the stones from his pack to test the repaired deck before trusting it with another person. When Bracken refused to cross, Nera went back and carried him tucked inside her coat. He emerged on the far side with his head beside her collar, pleased with the arrangement.

By the time Rook returned, both of them were wet to the knee.

"I see you found the bridge," he said.

"You're about to make a generous contribution to replacing it," Nera told him.

"Am I?"

"You sell rope."

"My own mistakes are beginning to trouble my business."

He had the book wrapped in a flour sack. Anwen came behind him with pages sewn between stiffer covers. She had walked quickly. Mud reached the hem of her coat and her breath came hard, but she did not ask them to wait while she recovered.

"There's something you should see," she said.

Kael looked at the packet.

"Another thing?"

She accepted the edge in his voice. "Yes."

She opened the pages on a dry flat stone. A list of wagon marks ran down the first, beside dates in the old calendar. On the facing sheet a different hand had copied the same marks into a column headed RETURNED.

Kael recognized the word before he began to understand the dates.

"These people arrived on the same day they supposedly left," he said.

"Some. Others were entered as returned before the receiving clerk had counted them."

Rook shifted his flour sack under his arm. "My grandmother had households for those marks. Actual people. She kept names where the clerks kept wagon numbers."

"Where are the signatures?" Nera asked.

Anwen shook her head.

Beyond the bridge stood the tally house, its roof lifted at one corner by roots. A stone chimney leaned over the approach. The place looked abandoned until Kael saw the warmth rising from the soil beneath it.

He gathered the pages before the damp could reach them.

"We look at what survives," he said. "Then you tell me everything about the day they brought you the tokens. Even the parts you think will make us stop listening."

Anwen met his eyes.

"All right."

Tor's voice came from the other side of the bridge, asking why nobody had warned him about the beam. Rook turned to answer. Nera went to show him where to put his feet.

Kael stood beside the old house with the unsigned return column in his hands. Somewhere in a room eight years behind him, his younger self was still writing neatly at a table.

He folded the pages along their existing crease and went to help them bring the lever across.


# Part Two The Names They Burned



# Chapter 10


## The Names They Burned


Kael took the iron bar from Tor before letting him onto the bridge. They drew it across on the line, low over the sounder beam. Nera watched the new brace while Tor came over alone, placing his boots where she told him.

“Next time you send for a man with a lever,” Tor told Rook, “tell him how far he's carrying it.”

“I said it was near the old house.”

“There are thirty old houses.”

“And I chose the most distinctive.”

Tor put his bag of wedges beside the bar and took off his coat. His shirt was dark with sweat between the shoulders. Kael gave the shrine pages back to Anwen. The unsigned column would be waiting when they had opened the tally house.

Rook loosened the flour sack around his grandmother's book. A row of small figures had been squeezed between two household accounts.

“Turnips,” he said, following Kael's glance. “She kept everything together. I haven't altered it.”

Beyond him, the tally house chimney leaned over the cutting. The roots that had broken its base were holding it upright. Bracken had found a warm patch of earth beside it and lowered himself onto it with the deliberation of a much larger animal.

“The warmth isn’t right,” Kael said.

Nera turned. “From the chimney?”

“Below it.”

She collected Bracken before Kael could ask, bracing the little plated body against her hip. He objected with a dry grinding chirr.

Anwen drew out her own pages. They were sewn along one edge with gray thread, the oldest sheet protected between two newer ones. “The shrine’s register names the wagons. Rook’s names the households. Tor’s marks give us the freight.”

“And that house?” Kael asked.

“Where arrivals were counted.”

“You’ve been here.”

She looked up at the chimney. “Years ago. The lower room was still sound.”

The tally house door stood crooked in a frame of blackened oak. Its lintel bore a weathered Vargan seal. Somebody had chipped away the crown and left the deer beneath it. Kael left the heavy bar outside until they knew what needed shifting, then stepped over the threshold.

Inside, one wall had fallen outward. The other three enclosed a room smelling of wet bark and iron. A stone shelf ran along the back, beneath roots as thick as Kael’s thigh. On the shelf sat a wooden box faced in lead. Its lock had burst under the growth of a root that passed through it.

“There,” Anwen said from the doorway.

Kael put his sword aside to lift the box. It would not move. A warm drop fell on his wrist. Sap, black in the room’s poor light.

The floor rose under his feet.

He caught the shelf, felt it slide, and pushed away as the back wall opened. Stones went past him into the room. Something immense unfolded behind them, dragging a lattice of roots from the bank. The iron corners of old chests were caught in its body. An axle projected where a shoulder should have been.

Nera seized Anwen by the collar and pulled her clear of the doorway. Kael snatched his sword. The thing’s first movement tore the shelf loose; its second swept the doorway with an arm of earth and timber. Kael dropped beneath it. A stone struck his back through his coat and drove the breath from him.

He crawled after the light.

Outside, Tor had planted the bar against the braced bridge beam. Rook was already on the farther bank with the books. Nera had set Bracken behind a fallen trunk and was trying to make Anwen leave the bridge.

“Go!” Kael shouted.

Anwen went. He followed as the tally house roof collapsed onto the colossus. It carried the weight for a moment, head bowed beneath slate and rotten beams. Then it straightened. The chimney came down across the narrow cutting with a sound that frightened the birds out of half the wood. Its upper courses broke against the opposite bank. The lower shaft lodged between the old house foundations and a beech, held together by the roots that threaded its mortar.

Kael reached the far bank. A root punched through the bridge behind his heel. Planks flew upward. The creature’s lower body remained embedded in the tally house mound, but the roots were crossing the ditch, feeling through the water.

“Its chest,” Tor said.

An iron band encircled the mass beneath the axle. Old cart straps had been riveted together, making a cage around a pulsing knot of pale wood. The tally box hung inside it, tilted against the straps.

“Can you cut it?” Nera asked.

“The iron, no.”

“Then what can you cut?”

Kael watched a root emerge from the water. It passed over bare stone, shuddered and went searching through mud again. Every new extension pulled the band tighter. A rivet near the axle had begun to lift.

“That joint.” He pointed with his sword. “If I get close.”

“You’ll be under the arm,” Tor said.

“Yes.”

Tor swore, picked up his wedges and crossed the ditch on the fallen chimney. Nera followed him, rope in hand. Neither waited for Kael to explain the rest.

The colossus dragged its arm across the mound. Tor flattened himself against the chimney stones. Nera flung the rope over the projecting axle and pulled the free end back toward the trees. It caught on a broken spoke. She tested it once, then took the line around a beech trunk.

Kael climbed the ruined wall on the creature’s right. Roots turned toward the vibration of his boots. He put a hand against the cold stone, called the small heat he could bear to hold, and thrust it into the tangle barring his way.

Oathfire ran along the wet bark with a sound like fat dropped into a pan. The roots recoiled. A breath of ash rose into his face. For an instant he smelled a crowded room: damp wool, sour milk, somebody’s cooking onions. Then it was gone.

He climbed.

The arm rose to strike. Nera hauled on the rope, putting her weight into it. The axle turned a few inches. Tor drove a wedge beneath the lifted rivet with the heel of his bar.

“Again!” Kael shouted.

Tor struck. The rivet jumped. Kael hooked his sword beneath the loosened strap and pulled until the healing cut in his left forearm felt as though it were opening. He changed his grip, braced one foot against the wood and tore the band apart.

The knot inside expanded through the gap. It had no face. Kael would remember that afterward, because in the moment he had been certain it was looking at him.

He drove his sword into the split.

The creature bent over him. Nera’s rope went slack. Kael let the sword go and fell sideways as timber closed around the place where he had stood.

He landed on the shelf, or what remained of it. The lead-faced box struck his thigh. He caught its handle and slid with it down the broken wall while the colossus settled into the ditch. Roots lashed twice across the water. A thin gray cloud moved through the trees at knee height, carrying the same brief smell of a room full of people.

Then Tor was gripping the back of his coat and swearing at him to get up.

They carried the box to the beech. Kael sat down with his back against it. His sword lay caught in a root on the opposite bank. For several breaths he could do nothing except watch it and breathe.

“You can fetch that later,” Nera said.

“It was expensive.”

“So are funerals.”

Rook looked at the collapsed house. “Not if you’ve already arranged the stones.”

Nobody laughed. After a moment, Rook sat beside Kael and offered him water.

***

The inner packet was dry. Tarred cloth had protected it beneath the lead, and the swollen box had held the folds shut. Anwen opened it with the careful impatience of someone unwrapping an injury.

Kael had retrieved his sword. He cleaned it while Rook arranged the papers on a flat stone. Tor stood over them, flexing his sound hands, his sleeves muddy to the elbow.

“Here,” Rook said. “Read the heading.”

Anwen read it quietly. “Received under sanctuary.”

Below were wagon numbers, household names and columns for the count of people. The return pages were thinner. Against several entries, a later hand had written *departed under escort*. Others bore only *returned*. No destination followed. No recipient had signed.

Rook opened his grandmother’s book. “She kept waiting for her aunt’s family. This is the money she sent to inquire. Three payments.” His finger paused. “Four. That one looked like turnips.”

Tor laid an iron tag beside a wagon number. He had brought it from the forge, where it had been wired to a bundle of old stock. The number matched. So did the household mark beside it.

“They paid for passage,” Nera said.

“They were promised shelter,” Anwen replied.

Kael looked between the records. None contained the order that had closed the road. None named the person who had commanded the soldiers. But the households had arrived under sanctuary, and the separate books showed people waiting for their return long after the official column said it had happened.

“We keep these apart when we copy them,” he said. “Which hand wrote what. Where each came from.”

Rook nodded. “I know how to copy a book.”

“I know.”

“You say it as if that surprises you.”

Anwen reached for the shrine pages. Kael put his hand on them first.

“Now the token.”

Her fingers stopped.

He took the brass crow from his oilskin pouch. The black paint had worn from one wing. Under it, AVEL followed the old curve of the metal. The red cord lay in a tangled loop.

“Tell us from when Sella came to you.”

Anwen glanced at Nera, then Rook. Tor stayed where he was.

“The road men found the cache when they opened the drain. They paid Bram to bring the sack. Oren showed me one of the tokens. He'd painted it.” She touched her sleeve instead of the brass. “He'd scratched through the paint to make the old letters clear. He wanted me to say the name.”

“And you did.”

“Yes.”

“What happened?”

“The lamp beside us went out. The rear door struck its frame. I relit the lamp. Sella asked if she should leave the sack with me.”

Kael waited.

“I sent her away with it.”

Rook sat back on his heels.

“After Evin,” Anwen said, “I couldn’t have it in the chapel. I thought if they kept moving while I found the burial form—” She stopped. “I put gray thread over the red cord she'd already tied. I told her not to untie it. I sent them to the stones as if the thread would hold until I arrived.”

“Did you believe it would?” Nera asked.

“I wanted it to be enough.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Anwen’s mouth tightened. She looked older in the cold light, and younger than Kael had thought her beside the shrine. “I didn’t know. I let her think I did.”

“And the coat?” Kael asked. “You kept it drying.”

“I said I'd bring it when I followed them.”

“When did you mean to follow?”

“Before dusk.” She pressed her hands together. “I went back to the burial book. One entry referred me to another. I kept thinking the next page would tell me how to carry the sack without opening it again. Then somebody came for a dressing. I changed it. I washed the cloths. I looked up and the window was dark.”

“You could have asked someone to go with you.”

“Yes.”

“You didn't.”

“No.”

Tor bent and picked up a fallen page before the damp could reach it. He smoothed it against his thigh. He did not look at Anwen.

“The wire?” Kael asked.

“I never put wire on a burial token.”

“That only tells us you didn’t.”

She nodded. “There’s a patrol receipt in the shrine’s intake book. Dated the morning after they should have reached the stones. Two men came back asking for the objects. One had blood on his leggings. They said the cart was already down when they arrived.”

“You kept that from me too.”

“Yes.”

Kael set the brass on his palm. He remembered the thread beneath the flattened turns of wire, the pack’s tracks crossed by boots, the older damage under the later binding. The receipt and patrol account would join those observations. They would have to be checked. He could not make a clock out of a knot.

Rook asked, “Did you ask them about Oren?”

Anwen’s face changed. “They wouldn’t answer.”

“And you sent us to ask you.”

She lowered her head.

The wood was full of small sounds again: water beneath the broken bridge, a branch rubbing another branch. Bracken scratched at the log where Nera had left him. Tor went to lift him down.

Kael wrapped the token and returned it to his pouch.

“We’re taking the intake book,” he said. “And you’ll say this to Elna and Mira. Sella bought medicine there. They waited for her.”

“I will.”

“Today.”

Anwen gathered her pages. The first attempt failed because her hands were shaking. Rook watched, then held the flour sack open without speaking. She put the register inside.

On the way back, Anwen waited her turn at the broken bridge. She crossed on the chimney stones, using the iron bar while Nera held the safety line. On the other bank she thanked them both. Neither answered, but Nera waited until Anwen stood clear before she loosened the rope.


# Chapter 11


## The Widow's Version


Elna had forgotten that she had kept the shoe.

It lay at the bottom of the chest beneath winter cloth, wrapped in one of Harl’s shirts. The leather toe had curled upward where he had left it too close to the fire. He had hidden it for six days. On the seventh he had asked, with extravagant innocence, whether she had considered wearing her good boots indoors.

She sat on the floor with the shoe in her lap and laughed once.

At the table, Anwen raised her head.

“Don’t,” Elna said.

“I haven’t said anything.”

“You were arranging your face.”

Anwen looked down at the papers again. There was a trace of mud on her cheek. Elna had noticed it when she came in and had decided not to tell her.

The kitchen was warmer than it ought to have been. Elna had put too much wood on because she needed something to do while Anwen explained. Now the back of her neck prickled. The shoe smelled faintly of old soot. She could remember exactly the pressure of Harl’s fingers when he had taken her cold feet into his hands. She could also remember him turning toward the window whenever a cart stopped outside.

“I wrote it down for the children,” she said.

“What did you write?”

“That he kept the gate during the war. That there wasn’t enough food. That men would have come through and taken what we had.”

Anwen did not reach for the page. That small restraint almost made Elna angrier.

“He said the same things. I made them easier to read.”

Outside, somebody was splitting kindling. The blows came in pairs, followed by a pause to set another piece on the block.

“Did you know about the sanctuary?” Anwen asked.

“I knew people had been told to come. He wouldn’t say who told them.” Elna rubbed the curled leather with her thumb. “Once, after we’d both had too much cider, he said there was a woman with a copper pan. She kept holding it up. He couldn’t tell whether she was offering it or trying to show she had something to cook in.”

“What did you say?”

“I told him to sleep.”

She set the shoe beside the chest. She had thought for years that confession would arrive like sickness: unmistakable, requiring the house to stop around it. Instead she wanted a cup of tea. Her feet hurt from sitting on the boards. The back door had swollen again and would need planing before the rain returned.

“You can write that he closed it,” she said. “Put his name beside the act. But you aren’t taking the shoe.”

“No.”

“Or the letters. Or every foolish thing I remember.”

Anwen’s eyes moved to the shirt around the shoe, then away.

Elna got up slowly. “You’ll need more light.”

She trimmed the lamp and brought it to the table. Anwen made room for her, pushing the official pages aside. On the sheet Elna had prepared for the children, *held the gate* stood in her neatest hand.

She crossed out *held*.

The next word would not come. She tried *guarded*, saw what she was doing, and pressed the pen too hard. Ink pooled against the downstroke.

“Closed,” she said aloud.

Anwen waited.

Elna wrote it. Then she added that people had been promised sanctuary, that they had reached Greyfen and been kept outside. She could not write how many. She could not say what happened at the other end of the road. Underneath she wrote, *He loved us. I used that to make this smaller.*

The pen caught in the damp place and tore the paper.

“Damn him,” she said.

Anwen moved as if to take her hand. Elna pulled it back.

“I’m angry with you as well.”

“I know.”

“You don’t. I haven’t finished deciding.”

They sat until the kindling blows stopped outside. Then Elna read the account through. It was short, and it left out most of the marriage. She had expected that to feel dishonest. It felt instead like choosing what belonged to the question she was answering.

“Will you want to read it?” Anwen asked.

“To the children. After supper. You may keep a copy of the first part.”

“And the last sentence?”

Elna considered it. “That stays here.”

Anwen marked a clean sheet and began to copy. Elna watched her working fingers. They had washed Harl’s body. They had brought broth when Elna could not bear the smell of cooking. They had tied thread around the thing Sella carried away.

When Anwen finished, Elna gave her a cloth for the mud on her face.

***

By morning, Rook had taken over the unused end of Nessa’s bakehouse. He said the warmth was good for drying ink. Nessa said the rent was carrying flour. Kael found him with white dust in his hair and two fingers held above the table so they would not mark the pages.

“You look honest,” Kael said.

“That’s the flour. It wears off.”

The originals lay nearest the wall: the tally-house packet, Anwen’s shrine leaves, Rook’s family book and the fresh copy Elna had permitted. A fifth volume, narrow and black-covered, was the shrine’s intake book.

Kael bent over its last pages. The patrol receipt named three recovered objects and acknowledged an inquiry for the remainder. The date was later than the travelers’ expected arrival at the stones. Anwen’s note beneath it said that the men reported the cart already overturned. Different ink, different hand. Kael had asked her to make that distinction before anyone copied it.

It did not tell him exactly when every turn of wire had been added. It did make the claim of a later recovery attempt harder to dismiss.

Rook drew a ruled line across his clean sheet. “There.”

“What’s missing?”

“A house beyond the lime pit. A woman’s married name. The lane my grandmother used after the war.”

“Will the copy still match?”

“The wagon number and the people on it will. Anybody who needs to compare the rest can ask me.” He leaned close to inspect an ink blot. “I’m preserving a road account, Kael. I don’t owe a stranger directions to my cousin’s bedroom.”

Nessa set a tray of flat loaves on the table. “You owe me that sack.”

“I carried two.”

“You carried one twice because you put it in the wrong place.”

Rook made a wounded noise and stood.

The men came while he was outside.

There were three of them. Kael recognized the flattened wire at the senior man’s belt before he saw the seal on his coat. They had come through the village openly, which mattered. Their boots were clean enough to show they had slept under a roof. One was barely old enough to grow the beard he kept rubbing.

“Shrine records,” the senior man said. “Where are they?”

Nessa continued laying loaves on the tray. “You want bread, you join the queue.”

“By the steward’s direction, documents relating to recovered road property are to be delivered for examination.”

Kael put down the intake book. “Which documents?”

The man looked at the table, then at Kael’s sword. “The ones you’ve spread through a bakehouse.”

“These aren’t all shrine property.”

“That will be determined.”

“By whom?”

The young soldier gave a small sigh. He had heard this question before. Kael looked at him and saw that he was watching the oven rather than the door.

The senior man took out a folded order. It bore Merrow’s name and a narrow impression of Vargan wax. Kael read it without taking it. The wording allowed collection of road objects and related records to prevent unauthorized handling. It said nothing about where they would be kept or when they would be returned.

“You’ll give a receipt for each,” Kael said.

“We’ll give a receipt for the bundle.”

“Each document. Including its owner and the number of leaves. You’ll sign that you received them in legible condition.”

“I don’t have time to inventory a kitchen.”

Nessa slid the tray past his elbow. “Then you don’t have time to take it.”

The third man moved toward the papers. Kael stepped into the narrow space between bench and oven. It put his sword within reach and his back dangerously close to hot brick. He kept his hands empty.

“Don’t,” he said.

The man stopped. The senior soldier’s face lost its patient expression.

Rook appeared in the doorway with a sack on his shoulder. He took in the room, carried the sack to the correct corner and dusted his hands.

“I wondered when this would happen,” he said. “You’re late.”

The soldier turned. “You were told?”

“If there’s paper anywhere in Vargan’s country, Merrow eventually wants it.” Rook pulled out a stool. “We’ve prepared a copy. You can take that today. The originals need witnesses for the transfer. One of the owners is upstairs nursing a sick child. Another’s gone to the cemetery. Unless you’d prefer to search both places with that young man carrying his sword through the beds.”

The young man stopped rubbing his beard.

“A copy is insufficient,” the senior soldier said.

“For comparing the numbers?” Rook asked. “They’re the same numbers.”

He offered the redacted sheets. The soldier read the first page. His eyes moved slowly. Kael could hear bread cracking as it cooled on the rack.

“Where are the addresses?”

“The dead don’t receive post.”

“I mean the people keeping these.”

“We’re here.” Rook spread his arms. “Warmest room in Greyfen.”

The soldier folded the sheets. He could insist. Kael saw him measuring how many people stood beyond the open door, and how readily an argument in a bakehouse might become a fight in which everybody remembered the loaves.

“The steward expects cooperation,” he said.

“Then let the grain carts through,” Nessa replied.

“Routes have to be inspected.”

Rook’s expression changed by a fraction. “Whose routes?”

“Any route carrying relief under Vargan protection.”

“And once you’ve inspected them?”

“The steward can authorize regular passage.”

Kael watched Rook look at the order again.

The men left with a copy and a receipt stating exactly what it was. The senior soldier refused a loaf. The youngest bought one with his own coin, breaking it before he reached the square.

Nessa shut the door against the cold.

“You enjoyed that,” she told Rook.

“Immensely. I’ll shake for an hour.”

He sat down. His knee was moving beneath the table, fast enough to tremble the inkwell. Kael steadied it.

“What did you mean, you wondered when?”

“They’ve been asking about deliveries. At the south road. The mill road. Anywhere a wheel turns.”

“You didn’t say.”

“You were under a tree trying to stab it.”

Kael let that pass. Rook had handled the soldiers better than he could have. He disliked needing that to be true.

They divided the copies before noon. Anwen took the shrine set back beneath her coat. Rook kept the household sources and tally-house leaves in his buckled document bag. Kael carried the patrol receipt’s copy alongside Bram’s battered ledger, separated from Oren’s token by a fold of oilskin.

Elna came in with her own page. She placed it before Rook and waited while he checked the words against the copy he had already made.

“That much,” she said, touching the first paragraph. “The rest belongs to us.”

Rook trimmed the sheet below the agreed lines. The scissors were Nessa’s, meant for cutting the edges of sacks. They left a slightly ragged margin. Elna ran a finger along it, then took the lower portion home.


# Chapter 12


## What We Carried


Nera could tell from the sound that the load was wrong.

The hauling chain struck the upright with a slow metallic knock. It should have hung clear. Instead it had twisted around its own lower length, pulling the cradle sideways every time the wind moved the suspended crates.

“Stop hauling,” she said.

The mill hand at the capstan gave another half turn.

“Stop.”

He let go. The handle kicked backward. Kael caught it before it hit the man in the chest, and the teeth of the locking bar dropped into place.

Below them, the drainage cut ran brown between stone walls. The old loading platform stood on the upper bank, where carts could come within reach of the mill’s lifting frame. A wagon had brought the relief freight as far as the broken approach. From there it had to cross the gap on the cradle.

Someone had tied two crates together to save a journey. One held medicine, dressings and four jars of strong spirit; the other contained augers, clean iron straps and replacement pulley blocks. The second crate was now hanging lower than the first.

“Whose knot?” Nera asked.

The mill hand looked at the chain.

“I asked because I need to know how it was tied.”

“Mine. It held on the cart.”

“A cart has a floor.”

She leaned over the platform rail. The lower rope had slipped beneath a projecting nail. Each sway of the cradle drew it across the point. Fibers hung loose, pale against the wet wood.

Tor climbed up behind her and put a hand on the upright.

“The foot’s moving,” he said.

She looked down. The frame had been repaired with a new block on an old sill. The new wood was sound. Water had eaten the sill beneath it.

“Can we lower?”

“If we take the twist out first.”

“Can we take the twist out with both crates?”

Tor did not answer immediately. She liked him for that. Men who answered quickly around lifting gear generally wanted somebody else to stand beneath it.

“I wouldn’t,” he said.

Nera unwound the spare line from her shoulder. It was good hemp, bought with money she had intended to spend on a new coat. She had stopped thinking of purchases in those terms after the first wet week, but whenever she passed a needle through the old coat she remembered the rope.

“Rook. Get the wagon back to level ground. No, leave the horse where it is. Move the empty wagon by hand. I don’t want it running down onto us.”

“It has been a pleasure owning a spine,” Rook said, and went.

“Kael, keep the capstan. Don’t let him take the handle again.”

The mill hand flushed.

“You,” she told him, “bring two planks and a crowbar. Quick enough to be useful. Slow enough to arrive with them.”

Mira stood at the lower edge of the platform, watching the medicine crate. She had come to meet the freight, sleeves rolled up beneath her coat. There was a patch of green on the side of one hand where a remedy had stained her skin.

“How much of that can get wet?” Nera asked.

“The cloth can be washed. The sealed jars might survive. The powdered root won’t.”

“And how much do you need?”

Mira looked at her. “Don’t ask me that way.”

“I need an answer.”

“Then ask me what it does. That jar is for pain. Those packets bring fever down. The dressings save us tearing up everyone’s remaining shirts.” She swallowed. “There are clean roots in the smaller box. For families who won’t take the ash-grown ones.”

Tor rubbed a thumb over the sill. A piece of it came away beneath his nail.

“The tools get us a proper repair here,” he said. “And at the water gate. Without them we’re borrowing from the forge and making the missing pieces.”

Kael held the capstan with both hands. “Which takes longer to replace?”

“The medicine,” Mira said.

Tor nodded reluctantly. “The tools take work. Some of the roots take a season.”

“Then we keep the medicine,” Kael said.

Nera looked at him until he added, “If you can separate them.”

“I can. You’ll lower when I tell you.”

She went down the ladder to the narrow stone lip above the water. The lip was slick with green growth. Above her, the cradle turned and struck the frame. She felt the knock through the wall against her shoulder.

For a moment she was back beside a different cart, Jem insisting that he could hold a wheel higher than she could. He had been all elbows then, offended by any task that allowed her an advantage. Her mother had settled it by making them both hold the wheel while she changed the pin. By the time she finished, their arms were shaking so badly neither wanted to claim victory.

Nera breathed out. She put the memory aside with care. It was useful to remember the sound of them laughing; she would not let it take her foot off the stone.

“A little toward me,” she called.

Tor levered the frame while Kael eased the capstan a fraction. The medicine crate swung within reach. Nera passed her good rope beneath it, keeping her head outside the path of the lower crate. The rope snagged on the corner.

“Hold.”

She could have climbed onto the cradle. It would have made the knot easy and given Tor another weight to carry. She took the long way, flattening herself against the wall and reaching beneath the crate with the hook on her belt.

The hook caught. She drew the rope through.

Above, Rook said something she could not hear. Then Kael shouted, “Nera.”

The sill cracked.

She finished the knot. A piece of rotten timber struck the water beside her. She ducked as the frame tilted, bringing the lower crate down against the wall.

“Take my line!”

The rope tightened across her palm. She released it and climbed two rungs while Tor and the mill hand hauled from above. The medicine crate rose clear of the lower one. The bad lashing stretched around the nail.

“Cut it,” she told Kael.

He had left the capstan only after wedging its handle against the rail. He leaned over, knife in his right hand. For an instant the crates hung together beneath him.

Then the tool crate fell.

It struck a projecting stone, burst open and hit the drainage cut in separate pieces. A pulley block bounced once in the current before disappearing. Iron straps slid beneath the brown water without a splash.

Mira flinched at the sound, although her own crate was safe on its separate line.

“Now lower,” Nera called.

They lowered it slowly. Kael returned to the capstan; Tor kept the frame from shifting farther with his bar. Nera guided the crate onto the two planks at the landing. It touched down crookedly. She made them lift it again, an inch, and turn it until both corners were supported.

Only then did she untie the rope.

***

Kael sat on the broken platform while Mira opened the crate below. He could still feel the capstan turning in his hands. The left sleeve of his shirt had pulled tight against the healing cut, but the dressing was dry. He decided not to mention how close he had come to splitting it.

“Let me see your arm,” Mira said without looking up.

He sighed.

“That was very moving,” Rook told him. “A brave man brought low by a woman who has eyes.”

Mira set the jars in a row, checked the wax seals and passed Nera the smallest box. The clean roots inside were dry. Nera closed it and sat beside the crate for a moment with her palms on her knees.

Tor had gone down to the water with a pole. He recovered one auger and two iron straps, both bent. The rest had lodged beneath the platform or gone farther downstream. He laid the salvaged pieces on the grass.

“We can straighten those,” Kael said.

“Yes.”

“The pulley?”

“We can’t straighten a missing pulley.”

Kael accepted the rebuke. Tor wiped the auger with a scrap of cloth, turning it to see whether the cutting edge had chipped. He had wanted those tools with an intensity Kael usually associated with thirst.

Nera climbed back to the platform and began coiling the rope. She cut away the section scraped by the wall. It was nearly an arm’s length.

“I’ll pay for that,” Kael said.

“With what?”

He considered the question. “Eventually.”

“I’ll put it beside the others.”

Rook crouched to help her coil. “Your mother used to keep a list on the back of the flour board, didn’t she?”

Nera’s hands stopped. “You knew her?”

“No. You told me. The first time you refused credit.”

“I didn’t refuse credit. I refused your arithmetic.”

“The result was similar.”

She smiled reluctantly. “Leth wrote the sums on the flour board because nobody could pretend they hadn’t seen them. Jem once wiped off three days’ bread to make room for a drawing.”

“An expensive artist.”

“A fish with legs. She made him eat the burnt crusts until he’d copied the numbers back from the kitchen book.”

Rook laughed. Kael had not known the names. He stood still while Nera tied the shortened coil. He wanted to ask how old the boy had been. He could feel the question arranging itself to sound harmless.

Nera looked up. Whatever she saw in his face made hers close.

“Carry the small box,” she said. “Mira needs it kept level.”

He carried it.

They moved the freight into a dry storeroom on the mill’s eastern side. The room had a proper door, a roof free of holes and no open flame. Mira counted everything twice. Rook entered the losses on the relief sheet while Nera watched him do it.

The entry for tools took more space than the entry for medicine saved. Kael understood why Nera had insisted on both.

When the others went to look at the damaged frame, Mira caught his sleeve.

“Now.”

“It’s closed.”

“I’d prefer to learn that with my own eyes.”

He sat on a packing case and unfastened the cuff. She removed the outer wrapping. The cut was red along the edges, the skin beneath the stitches shiny and new. She pressed beside it. He kept his hand still.

“You pulled it.”

“A little.”

“That’s a quantity people invent when they want me to stop asking.”

“Would a large quantity help?”

She glanced up, and despite herself the corner of her mouth moved.

He liked the way she worked when she was annoyed: precise, sparing, unwilling to let the annoyance enter her hands. He had seen other healers make pain into correction. Mira simply loosened the fresh bandage where it crossed his wrist.

“You were right about the roots,” he said.

“Tor was right about the tools.”

“We couldn’t keep both.”

“I know. Let him be unhappy about it.”

Her fingers rested for a moment against the inside of his arm. He looked at them, then at her. The storeroom door stood open. Outside, Rook was explaining that he had never once claimed to understand machinery. Nera answered that this had become apparent.

Mira tied the bandage and did not immediately move away.

“You can ask,” she said.

Kael’s mouth had gone unexpectedly dry. “Would you have supper with me?”

“I eat supper most nights.”

“Somewhere Rook isn’t.”

“That will narrow the choice.”

He laughed. She touched the seam of his coat at the shoulder, a quick smoothing gesture that had no medical purpose.

“After we’ve finished here,” she said. “And if you aren’t bleeding somewhere else by then.”

A heavy sound crossed the roof above them. Both looked up. Dust sifted from a beam. Something scraped slowly over the slates, paused, and moved toward the main mill.

Mira took her hand away.

Outside, the wheelkeeper was staring at the roofline. Tor had stopped in the middle of picking up his bag.

A gust carried warm, bitter air through the yard. Kael smelled burned grain, and beneath it something closer to hair.


# Chapter 13


## Ash at the Mill


The wheel was still turning.

That was the first thing Kael understood when he reached the main yard. Something large had settled above the mill, the men at the upper windows were shouting, and a woman had fallen on the loading steps, but the wheel continued its heavy, comfortable rotation. Water filled the buckets and fell from them. The building shook at the rhythm of ordinary work.

Tor seized the wheelkeeper by the coat. “Stop the feed.”

“The gate won’t drop.”

“Then take the belt.”

The man stared at him.

Tor pushed him toward the side door. “The belt. Off the drum. Go.”

Kael looked up. The creature lay along the roof ridge with its wings folded over it like torn sailcloth. Its head hung below the eastern eave, watching the loading steps. The throat swelled. Smoke escaped between the plates around its jaw.

“Away from the steps,” Kael called.

The woman had twisted her ankle. Another worker tried to haul her upright by both wrists. It only made her cry out.

Nera went to them while Kael drew his bow.

He aimed for the space below the creature’s eye. The arrow struck a cheek plate, skipped and lodged in the roof. The head moved toward him with surprising care. He saw that the nostrils were crusted in pale ash.

“Kael,” Mira said.

He caught her warning and ran sideways.

The breath crossed the place where he had stood. It was less a flame than a blast of glowing dust. Grass went black. A length of spilled flour flashed orange along the ground. Heat struck the side of his face, and he threw himself behind the stone trough beside the wall.

The creature lifted its head. For three breaths it remained still, throat working as it drew air back into itself.

Nera had the injured worker on her shoulder. The other man carried her legs. They reached the cover of the freight shed before the head turned again.

“How many inside?” Kael shouted.

“Five above,” Tor answered. “Two in the lower room.”

“Can they use the back?”

“There isn’t a back. There’s the wheel.”

Kael looked along the wall. The mill stood between the yard and its watercourse, with a stone channel dividing the lower building from the steep bank. A narrow maintenance passage ran beside the channel. Its far end was hidden beneath a wooden gallery. Steam or smoke drifted from it.

Tor followed his gaze. “The sluice. That crosspiece lifts the inspection shutter with it. We get them open, the passage can draw air.”

“And the people upstairs?”

“They can come down the inspection ladder. If they can see it.”

The wheel stopped with a groan. Inside the mill something continued to rotate, slowing, its bearings giving a high complaint. The wheelkeeper emerged coughing and bent over the trough.

Rook had gone through the office door.

Kael swore. He made it halfway there before Rook reappeared, carrying a ledger so large he had to brace it against his ribs. A second man followed with a wooden cash drawer.

“Wrong book,” Rook told him. “Put that down and take the wage pages.”

The man disappeared again.

“Get behind the wall,” Kael said.

“With pleasure.”

Rook set the ledger on the ground beyond the shed. Its binding had split, showing older gatherings sewn into the current book. Kael saw an entry marked with the same wagon sign they had brought from the tally house.

“Later,” he said.

“It may not be here later.”

“Neither will the men in the mill.”

Rook shut the book. “What do you need?”

“A clear path from that gallery to this shed. Wet cloths. Tell anyone who comes out to keep moving.”

“I can do that.”

Kael went for the sluice.

He had to cross twelve paces of open yard. The creature watched him from above, wings lifting slightly to balance its weight. He waited until the throat began to swell, stepped toward the trough and then doubled back across the gravel. The breath hit the water with a noise like a great sheet being torn.

He reached the channel wall through a cloud of steam.

Tor was already there, kneeling at the gate’s winding gear. Rust had seized the lower guide. Without the new pulley they had lost in the freight cut, there was no easy way to lift the counterweight out of line.

“Give me your bar,” Kael said.

“You can have the end of it.”

They put it beneath the crosspiece together. The gate lifted a finger’s width and stuck. Water pushed through the gap, carrying black sediment against their boots.

“Again,” Tor said.

The roof creaked. Kael looked up and saw a wing unfold against the pale sky. The membrane was pierced in several places, its thick leading edge scarred white. The drake shifted toward the gallery, following the sound of people inside.

“Now,” Tor said.

Kael heaved. His left arm burned. Tor drove a wedge into the opening, changed the angle of the bar and struck the guide with a hammer. Rust broke away in flakes. The gate shuddered upward.

The water took it.

The crosspiece jumped, almost wrenching the bar from their hands. The counterweight dropped into its pit. Water flooded the lower channel. Above it, the inspection shutter rose with a scrape of warped wood, admitting a current of cooler air through the gallery. Smoke began to move toward them, then over their heads.

Kael wrapped a wet cloth over his mouth and went inside.

The lower room was close and red with reflected light. Flour dust coated the beams. A boy old enough for a man’s wages was crouched beside a stopped shaft, one hand clamped around a torn sleeve. Beside him, an older worker had wedged his boot between two rungs of the fallen inspection ladder to keep it from sliding.

Above, feet searched for the rungs.

“Leave the ladder,” Kael told the older man. “I’ll hold it.”

“It’ll go.”

“Then help him first.”

The boy stood when the man pulled him. Kael took the ladder’s weight. It was heavier than it looked, its upper end still hooked around a beam. He pushed its foot into a corner of the stone floor.

One worker came down, then another. The third froze halfway, coughing into her shoulder.

“Look at my hand,” Kael said.

She looked down at him.

“This rung. Your foot there.”

She moved. He counted the remaining workers aloud, less to reassure them than because he needed the number. The last carried a folded bundle against his chest. Kael nearly told him to drop it before recognizing the blood spreading through the cloth wrapped around his forearm.

The building shook.

A claw came through the ceiling beside the ladder. The beam above Kael turned, shedding dust. He let the ladder fall and pushed the last worker through the passage ahead of him.

They came out into daylight with the drake’s head reaching down toward the gallery.

Kael called Oathfire before he had quite cleared the doorway. The flame struck inside the open throat. It broke the creature’s gathering breath into a cough of sparks.

He staggered against the wall. For a few moments his fingers would not close.

Nera caught the last worker and dragged him toward the shed. Kael followed until he could see all seven sitting or lying beneath its roof. Mira was already cutting a sleeve open. Rook passed cups down the line, spilling water over his boots.

“Seven,” Kael said.

“I counted,” Nera answered.

The creature dropped from the roof into the yard.

Its weight drove the foreclaws deep into the wet ground. It folded one wing before the other, favoring a joint near the shoulder. The gallery roof had broken beneath it. Behind the beast, smoke rose from the exposed upper room.

Kael had no strength for another full cast. He picked up his bow from beside the trough.

“Get them farther back,” he told Nera.

“Where are you going?”

“The channel.”

The beast came after him. On the ground it moved in quick, ugly rushes, pausing with its head low between them. Kael put an arrow into the soft fold behind its jaw. It snapped at the shaft, distracted just long enough for him to cross the maintenance plank over the sluice.

On the opposite bank he turned.

The drake stepped onto the gallery’s fallen framework. Old timber cracked beneath its foreclaws. It tried to open its wings, but one membrane caught on a projecting iron bolt. Kael could see the throat moving again.

He sent his second arrow into the injured wing joint.

The beast jerked sideways. Its weight went through the broken framework, and the open sluice dragged water beneath its chest. One foreleg slipped into the channel. The throat opened too soon; hot dust struck the water and burst into steam around its head.

Kael went down the bank while it was blinded.

He struck behind the jaw, where the arrow had opened the hide. The sword sank and stopped against bone. The drake twisted. He pulled free, ducked beneath the wing and nearly fell on the wet stones. Its tail struck the bank above him, showering him in soil.

He waited for the head to turn back.

When it did, he thrust upward through the throat’s stretched underside and held until the blade began to bend.

The body struck the channel wall. Kael let go and climbed, digging his fingers into mud. Behind him, claws scraped stone. The sound became slower. Then the water grew loud again.

He stayed on his hands and knees until Nera reached him.

“Leave the sword,” she said.

He looked at her.

“For five minutes, Kael.”

***

They moved the ledger into the freight shed when the smoke thinned. The drake’s body remained in the channel, one wing laid across the stones. It would take ropes, a clear head and more people to shift it safely. Tor refused to let anyone try before morning.

Rook worked with the wheelkeeper beside him. They copied the oldest freight entries onto dry paper while Kael held the curling corners flat. Some pages were stained with ash so deeply that rubbing them only spread it. The wheelkeeper supplied the meanings of the column marks. He knew the measures; he had been taught them by the man before him.

“This is ash brought in,” he said, touching one heading. “This is ash taken to the beds. These numbers tell you which load.”

The loads matched the wagons in the sanctuary register. Not every one. Enough that Rook stopped moving his pen and sat for a long time looking at a line of figures.

“And the return column?” Kael asked.

The wheelkeeper shook his head. “I only know the milling marks.”

Kael let him keep that answer. They had a chain of accounts now, and each link was ugly enough without making any of them bear more than it could.

Mira came to the doorway. She had blood at the cuff of her sleeve, and the wet cloth she had used against the smoke hung around her neck.

“How many sacks can you produce from clean grain?” she asked the wheelkeeper.

He looked toward the damaged building. “With that wheel?”

“Once it’s safe.”

“I’d have to see the lower bearings.”

“And until then?”

“There’s reserve flour in the west store. Some of it belongs to the Keep.”

Nera made a mark on her relief sheet. The matter would become an argument when they had the strength for one.

Rook finished the copy. He wrote where the pages had come from and what condition they were in. The wheelkeeper put his mark beneath the description. Tor witnessed it, then made Rook read the numbers back.

They laid the damaged leaves flat to dry rather than feed them to the fire. One loose working sheet had scorched almost through. Rook copied the legible figures before setting it apart in a shallow pan where it could no longer shed glowing fragments into the other pages.

Kael looked out at the workers beneath the shed. A woman was trying to laugh at something the injured boy had said. The laugh kept turning into a cough. Mira went back to them, carrying one of the clean-root packets Nera had saved.

Tor held the lamp closer to the ledger. The light showed the number of a wagon whose household Rook’s grandmother had waited for, and the weight of ash delivered from it.

Outside, the stopped wheel dripped into the dark.


# Chapter 14


## Iron Remembers


By daylight, the dead drake looked smaller and more difficult to move.

Its wing had settled across the drainage cut. The membrane held water, each sagging hollow adding weight to the body wedged beneath the gallery. Every few minutes a trapped pocket emptied with a splash. The smell reached the yard whenever the wind turned.

Tor walked the bank with the wheelkeeper. He had slept sitting against the freight store, and one side of his face bore the woven pattern of a sack. He carried no hammer. He kept stopping to put his hands on things: the gate housing, a post, the loosened edge of a stone.

“We take the water around it first,” he said. “Then we cut the wing free. Nobody goes under the gallery until I say.”

Nera repeated the order to the workers. Rook wrote down the rope lengths they were using, then crossed out the total and began again after Nessa borrowed one line to hang blankets.

She had arrived with Elna before dawn, bringing broth, bread and a handcart that had lost a wheel pin twice on the journey. Kael had seen Tor repair it while eating. The smith held a piece of bread between his teeth, worked the peg into place, and then looked offended when Elna laughed at him.

Now the water was rising in the cut.

Kael and two workers cleared brush from the relief channel. It had been little more than a ditch, neglected while the main sluice carried the flow. They dragged a mat of roots onto the bank. The first rush of water revealed a shallow bed of pebbles and old nails.

One nail still had a loop of wire fastened to it. Kael left it where it lay. There were too many bits of metal here for every one to become a clue.

Tor came to inspect the current. “Another foot on the left.”

Kael dug.

“Your other left.”

“You could say east.”

“I could say a good many things.”

The new channel began to draw. Across the cut, the wheelkeeper descended the short service stair to release a retaining chain that had tangled with the fallen gallery. Tor watched from above. The chain was under little strain now; that was why he had allowed it.

Kael was lifting the last root mat when the bank made a sound like somebody tearing thick cloth.

A patch of stonework dropped below the wheelkeeper. He fell against the stair. The chain caught his coat, and the gallery shifted, dragging him toward the opening beneath it.

Tor went down the remaining steps.

“Stay there!” Kael shouted, already running.

He did not know which man he meant. The wheelkeeper had one hand around the rail. Tor caught the back of his belt and hauled. For a moment both men were above the water. Then a broken plank turned beneath Tor’s boot.

He fell forward, put out his left hand and struck the edge of the lowest stone.

Kael heard the break.

Tor made a small, surprised noise. His right hand kept its grip on the wheelkeeper’s belt. Water pushed against their legs. Kael reached them from the upper step, caught Tor beneath the arm and pulled until his own boots slid. Nera took the back of Kael’s coat. Together they dragged both men clear as the remaining stair folded into the cut.

The wheelkeeper lay on his stomach coughing. Tor sat with his left forearm across his knees.

His wrist had changed shape.

He looked at it for several seconds before saying, very quietly, “Oh.”

Mira was beside him at once. She took his elbow, then looked toward Kael.

“Clear the way to the store.”

Tor tried to stand unaided. His face went gray. Kael supported him on the right, which the smith accepted with a fury so controlled it showed only in the angle of his jaw.

Inside, Mira made him sit on the low bench. He watched her hands rather than his own. When she touched the wrist he jerked backward, striking the wall with his shoulder.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

“Don’t apologize. Do it.”

“I need to know what I’m doing first.”

Kael went out to fetch clean water. He took longer than necessary because he could hear Tor through the open door and knew the smith would hate being heard.

***

The brace was made from two smooth pieces of crate wood. Mira wrapped them with linen and supported Tor’s forearm in a sling tied over his right shoulder. The left fingers remained visible, swollen and scraped but warm.

“You don’t lift with it,” she told him.

“I hammer with the other hand.”

“You don’t hammer either.”

“You think I can’t hold—”

“I think you hold the work with your left hand. I have seen you do it.”

Tor looked away.

“We’ll watch the swelling. You’ll tell me if the fingers change or the pain changes. And you’ll let someone else carry your bag.”

“How long?”

She did not soften the answer into a few convenient days. It would be weeks before she could judge the healing properly, longer before he knew what strength had returned. Kael watched Tor absorb each part separately. The injury was one thing. The work already promised was another.

When Mira left to check the wheelkeeper, Tor asked Kael to bring his hammer.

“No.”

“I want to put it away.”

Kael fetched it. Tor could not fasten the loop on his tool bag with one hand. He tried twice, then pushed the bag toward Kael without looking at him.

Outside, the workers waited beside the channel. The relief cut was open, but the retaining wall had failed. Nobody wanted to be the next person told it was safe.

Tor came to the doorway.

“We clear it from the top,” he said. “With a line from the beech. No one stands on that bank.”

The men looked at his sling.

“I can still see,” he snapped.

Nera uncoiled the rope. “Then show me where you want it.”

She listened while he explained, asked him to repeat the part about the anchor, and tied it herself. Kael cut the dead wing away from the broken frame. It was unpleasant work, slow and close. By the time they hauled the body clear of the channel, his clothes were streaked with black fluid and his hands shook from the effort.

Tor watched from the yard. Once he stepped forward to help. Elna put a bowl into his good hand before he reached the rope.

“Hold that.”

“I’m busy.”

“So am I.”

He stood there with the broth while the others hauled.

***
