They found the marks while inspecting the surviving wheel housing.

The outer strap had rusted through near its lower rivet. Beneath it, protected from weather, a row of small cuts remained in the iron. Kael had seen the same mark on Tor’s tag and beside a household in the tally book: three strokes joined by a shallow curve.

Tor saw him looking.

“I know,” he said.

The smith sat on an upturned bucket because Mira had ordered him to keep his arm still. Nessa stood beside the open flour store, counting sacks with the wheelkeeper. Beyond them, the injured workers had spread blankets in the sun.

Kael crouched by the strap. “You made this repair?”

“The outer piece. Six winters ago. Maybe seven.”

“You saw the marks then.”

“I’ve seen them on half the old stock that comes into Greyfen.”

Kael waited.

Tor’s right hand closed around his knee. “I ground them off when they were in the way. Sometimes when they weren’t.”

“Why?”

“Because a buyer asks questions when there’s somebody else’s mark under the new one. Because the door needs hanging before the rain. Because iron costs money.” He looked at the wheel. “Choose whichever makes me sound best. I’ve used all of them.”

Kael rubbed a thumb over the shallow cuts. The metal was cold.

“Whose mark?” Tor asked.

Rook brought the copied sheet. He read the household name and the wagon number, then showed Tor where the entries matched. Tor studied them for a long time.

“Leave it visible,” he said.

“The strap needs replacing.”

“Then keep the piece. Make a tag for where it was used. And give me a clean strap.”

Rook glanced toward the empty tool crate on the bank.

Tor shut his eyes briefly. “When we have one.”

They gathered around the mill’s working plan in the yard. The upper room was unsafe. One bearing had shifted. The lower stones could run at reduced speed once the housing was braced, provided they kept the damaged feed separate and stopped to inspect it. The old ash channels would have to be closed and rebuilt. Tor could explain every repair. He could perform none of them himself.

Nessa had counted enough reserve flour for a short interval, less if everyone who had heard the mill was closed came to claim a share.

“They will,” Elna said.

“I know they will. I’m not blaming them.”

“You sound as though you are.”

“That’s my counting voice.”

The wheelkeeper wanted to begin that afternoon. Mira wanted the injured workers sent home. Nera wanted a separate dry store and a route that would not take sacks under the damaged gallery. Rook wanted to know who was paying for clean iron.

Kael looked at the accounts spread beneath the stones holding their corners down. He had fought the thing on the roof because it was killing people. The next decision offered no comparably clear target.

“If the mill stays shut,” he asked, “where does the next flour come from?”

“Vargan,” Nessa said. “When they let the carts through.”

“And if it runs?”

Tor pointed with his good hand. “That channel remains closed. These two joints get braced first. Someone checks the bearing every hour. A person who knows what they’re looking at, not whoever loses an argument.”

“Limited milling,” Kael said.

“Limited,” Tor repeated. “I won’t have you remember only the first word.”

They agreed to use clean grain from the separated store, keep the damaged stock apart and record what went through the stones. The wheelkeeper would supervise the run. Tor would direct the repairs from the yard. The workers who could safely return would be paid from the first sales, and a share of those sales would buy the replacement channels and iron.

The man with the torn forearm asked what happened if he could not work.

Nessa looked up from her figures. “You still eat.”

“For how long?”

She took a breath. “Long enough for us to have another argument about it. Sit down before Mira sees you standing.”

It was a thin arrangement. It needed supplies they did not yet possess, wages they had not earned, and cooperation from people who had every reason to be tired. They wrote the terms where the wheelkeeper could point to them when someone urged him to run faster.

Kael helped carry the first clean sack into the lower room. Before the stones turned, Tor made them stop and adjust the brace. Then he made them stop again.

When the small quantity of flour finally began to fall, nobody cheered. Nessa put her hand beneath the chute, rubbed the meal between her fingers and said it would do for coarse bread.

Tor sat outside with his left wrist cradled against his chest. His hammer lay in Nera’s hand. He watched her set the next wedge, corrected the angle, and waited while she tried again.


# Chapter 15


## Bread and Ash


Nessa burned the first batch because everyone kept asking when it would be ready.

“There,” she said, holding up a loaf with a blackened bottom. “Now you know.”

The workers in the freight shed laughed, cautiously at first. She scraped the worst of the crust into the hearth and cut the rest into pieces small enough to hide the uneven rise. The flour from the limited run was coarse, and the borrowed oven drew harder on one side than the other. She said both faults belonged to men who would not be eating until they fixed them.

Tor looked down at his sling.

“Not you,” she said. “You can criticize from the bench. You were always good at that.”

Kael had washed in cold water behind the store. The smell of the drake remained in his coat despite everything he had tried. He hung it away from the table and sat beside Rook, who moved his bowl farther from the hanging cloth.

“An insult to the stew,” Rook explained.

“What’s in it?”

“Nessa won’t tell me. Apparently I ask with the wrong expression.”

“Beans,” Nessa said. “Onions. One rabbit. The rabbit volunteered less enthusiastically than the beans.”

“A proper feast.”

“You can have the head if you’re going to review it.”

The bowl passed down the table. Kael watched people make room without being asked. A worker slid farther along the bench for the woman whose ankle had swollen. Someone put a rolled sack beneath her foot. The boy with the torn arm pretended not to need help breaking his bread until Mira broke it while speaking to somebody else and left the pieces within his reach.

Anwen had brought two more blankets from Greyfen. She laid them beside the injured workers and asked whose dressings needed changing. Several people accepted the blankets without looking at her. She began folding the empty sacks, then unfolded them when Nessa said she needed them whole for the next load.

Kael felt the silence around her and left it alone.

Before sitting down again, he took the small wrapped bell from his pack and set it beside Elna's place. She opened the cloth with one hand, keeping the other beneath Tor's tilted bowl.

“You remembered,” she said.

“I made a rubbing of the marks. They aren't clear enough to give us a household yet.”

She turned the bell and found the place with her thumb. The rigging they had used at the cemetery was gone. A little wax remained near the handle.

“Keep looking,” she said. “But I'll have this back.”

“It's yours.”

She tucked it into the bag beside her feet, then rescued Tor's bowl before he could protest that he had been managing it perfectly well.

She went on holding the bowl while he ate, talking about the bakehouse roof as if that were why she had come over.

“The rain comes through the little join above the oven,” she said.

“It needs flashing.”

“Yes. You told Nessa that last year.”

“She told me she’d pay last year.”

“I heard that,” Nessa called.

“Then you owe me for two conversations.”

Elna smiled. After a while she said something quieter. Kael caught only Harl’s name and the word *bell*. Tor put the spoon down. He listened without preparing an answer. When he spoke, Elna shook her head and pointed toward the injured wrist. He looked annoyed, then almost amused.

Kael turned away before he could hear more.

Mira arrived at the table after everyone else had begun. She had washed her hands, but her sleeves were still rolled to the elbow. Nessa had kept a bowl covered for her.

“Sit,” she said.

“I’m going to.”

“You say that and then find another thing.”

Mira sat beside Kael. Her knee touched his beneath the table and stayed there.

For a few minutes they ate. He had forgotten how hungry he was. There was a depth to the stew that the ingredients did not explain, a sweetness from onions cooked long enough to lose their sharpness. The bread rasped against the roof of his mouth. He scraped the bowl with the last piece, regretting the mouthfuls he had swallowed too quickly to taste.

Then the runner came with the new grain order.

He was wet to the waist, having forded the shallow water below the broken approach. Rook took him to the hearth and Nessa gave him a bowl before anybody asked what he carried. He produced the folded paper from inside his cap.

The seal was Edric’s. The hand beneath it was a clerk’s, clean and generous with the margins.

Kael read it once. Then he read the line about allotments again.

“What?” Mira asked.

He passed it to her.

The order called supported households to witness a renewed road covenant. Names would be entered against the seal. Grain protection and allotment were to be recorded beside household assent.

Rook leaned over Mira’s shoulder. “Beside,” he said. “A nice word. Doesn’t take up much room.”

Nessa wiped her hands on her apron and took the sheet. She read slowly, moving her lips at one phrase.

“Where does it say what happens if they don’t sign?”

The runner looked at his bowl. “The receiving officer said the columns had to agree.”

“Which officer?” Kael asked.

“At the south granary. I don’t know his name.”

“What did he mean, agree?”

The man swallowed. “A household can’t be entered for the flour without being entered for the renewal.”

The shed grew quiet enough for Kael to hear the runner’s spoon touch the bowl.

Anwen stood beside the hearth with an empty sack in her hands.

“How much flour?” Nessa asked.

“There’s grain waiting. More than I’ve seen there in months. They were unloading when I left.”

That made it worse. Kael could have hated an empty promise cleanly. Somewhere beyond the mill, men were stacking sacks that would keep Pell and the boy at the table and half of Greyfen alive through the cold. The grain existed. So did the condition attached to it.

“I’ll go to the Keep,” he said.

Nera looked up from the relief sheet. “How?”

“By the road.”

“The crossing is damaged. Senn’s people are taking supplies farther east. We have medicine here, hungry households beyond the landing, and a wheel that can barely turn. Which of those follows you to the Keep?”

“The order came from Edric.”

“Yes. He’ll still be there after we get a cart across.”

Kael pushed his bowl away. He knew she was right and disliked the shape of his own impatience. He wanted a door he could enter, a man who could be made to answer. Nera wanted the next load to reach the people who had already been promised it.

Nessa laid the grain order flat. “The old landing has a roof. There’s a cooking hearth under the stone side. If we put the households there, we can feed more of them on less fuel.”

“Lantern Reach,” Rook said.

“You know another old landing with a roof?”

“Several. I could charge you to discover them.”

Nessa ignored him. “We start there. Common pot. Dry wood. Places to sleep for the ones who can’t get home. The mill sends what it can.”

“And when that runs out?” the injured worker asked.

Nessa looked at the grain order. “Then we’ll have Edric’s answer.”

She did not make it sound certain. Kael trusted her more for that.

***

Mira found him outside, sitting on the low wall above the quiet wheel.

“Your supper,” she said.

She carried the piece of bread he had left behind. He took it and moved to make space. She sat close enough that their shoulders touched.

The moon had not risen. Lamps in the freight shed made small rectangles on the yard. Someone inside began a song and forgot the words after the second line. Rook supplied a verse that clearly belonged to a different song. The resulting dispute sounded happy.

“You were going to leave without telling anyone,” Mira said.

“I said I’d go.”

“You said it after you’d decided.”

He broke the bread. “I haven’t gone.”

“Because Nera stopped you.”

He considered several answers and disliked all of them. “Yes.”

Mira took the smaller half from his hand. She chewed while watching the wheel.

“I don’t want to spend my evenings wondering which useful thing you’ve found to die beside.”

“I was hoping for a more flattering complaint.”

“You can earn one later.”

He looked at her profile. There was a faint indentation across the bridge of her nose from where she had tied the wet cloth during the fire. Her hair had escaped its fastening in several places. He wanted to touch it and was suddenly uncertain what to do with his hands.

She noticed. “That’s a different expression.”

“I’m trying not to improve the evening by saying something stupid.”

“You’ll tire yourself out.”

He laughed, and she turned toward him.

The kiss was brief at first. He had imagined it often enough that its actual awkwardness surprised him: her cold nose, the edge of the wall beneath his palm, a strand of hair caught between them. She pulled back to free it and swore softly. Then she kissed him again.

He put his right hand at her waist. She leaned into him, and the wanting in him became less abstract, less safely confined to the moment before something might happen.

When they separated, she rested her forehead against his.

“There,” she said.

“A flattering complaint.”

“Don’t spoil it.”

They stayed on the wall until the cold reached through their clothes. Mira’s hand lay over his right hand. He could feel how tired she was in the weight of it.

“There’s a room above the west store,” she said. “Elna’s sleeping in the shed with Nessa. She says my feet are cold.”

“Are they?”

“Dreadful.”

He looked toward the stairs. “Do you want company?”

“Yes.” She squeezed his hand. “I do.”

The room contained a narrow bed, a stool and a cracked pitcher. Kael set the pitcher on the floor before either of them knocked it over. Mira shut the door, leaned against it and laughed at something he had missed.

“What?”

“You looked at the window latch before you looked at the bed.”

“Habit.”

“I know.”

She crossed the room and put her hands against his chest. He unfastened her coat slowly. Beneath it she wore a plain shirt with a patch at the shoulder, the stitching small and uneven. He kissed the place where the collar had rubbed her skin.

For once she was neither examining an injury nor carrying something to somebody who needed it. When he reached for the lamp, she stopped his hand long enough to kiss his fingers.

Then she let the room go dark.

***

Kael woke because Mira had taken most of the blanket.

He considered waking her to dispute it, then saw the gray light at the window and the lines of exhaustion that sleep had only partly eased. He moved closer instead. Her feet were as cold as promised.

For a while he lay still. From the shed below came Tor's voice, calling for somebody to help him with a fastening. Kael would have to go down soon. Beside him, Mira had turned her face into the pillow and was breathing through her open mouth. A strand of hair tickled his chin. He moved it carefully, trying to gain another minute without waking her.

She stirred when he tried to free his arm.

“Where?”

“Water.”

“Bring some back.”

He found the pitcher, discovered it was empty and went downstairs in his unlaced boots. Nessa was already feeding the oven. She looked at him, at the pitcher, and then at his boots.

“If you fall down those steps,” she said, “I’m leaving you there until after breakfast.”

He filled the pitcher without answering.

When he returned, Mira had dressed and was combing her hair with her fingers.

“I’m going to Greyfen before the landing,” she said. “Pell needs another look. So do the people who didn’t come here.”

Kael put the water on the stool. He had been about to ask whether she would travel with him. The unspoken question embarrassed him.

“How long?”

“As long as it takes.”

He nodded. She watched him in the small mirror’s imperfect reflection.

“This,” she said, turning to touch his wrist, “I want this. I also have work.”

“So do I.”

“Then we’ll have something to complain about when we meet.”

He kissed her before she could reach the door. She stayed for it, smiling against his mouth, and then took her medicine bag downstairs.

By the time Kael brought the pitcher back to the kitchen, Nessa had set him to slicing onions for Lantern Reach.


# Chapter 16


## The Empty Tables


Lantern Reach possessed three tables, none of which could be trusted with a bowl.

The first had lost a leg. The second stood firmly until weight touched one corner, when the whole frame folded with an air of wounded surprise. The third was a door laid across two barrels. Nessa examined them in that order and said the door had the most promising future.

Kael put down the sack he was carrying. The landing roof stood against a low stone wall above the river. Its open side faced the prevailing wind. Beyond it, the old crossing had lost two spans, leaving a pair of piers with water sliding around their shoulders. On the far bank, a narrow service path climbed between reeds and bare willow.

“There’s a hearth,” Rook said.

Nessa looked at the blackened hollow in the wall. “There was a hearth. Now there’s a place where people have put things they didn’t want to carry home.”

Kael began taking out broken crockery.

The first household arrived before they had cleared the ashes. Siv came with water jars hung from a shoulder yoke and stopped at the edge of the roof as if uncertain whether work entitled anyone to shelter. Kael took one jar before it could strike the table’s remaining leg.

“There’s room against the wall,” he said.

“For how long?” Siv asked.

“Today. We’re working on the rest.”

Siv nodded and went to bring the other belongings under cover.

Bera arrived next, carrying a broom and a bundle that seemed to contain half a house by the variety of sounds it made. A spoon fell out onto the stones. Bera retrieved it, inspected the mud and put it in a pocket.

“You have a roof,” Bera said.

“Some of one,” Rook replied.

“I’ve lived beneath worse.”

The broom began moving before Kael could offer a task. Bera swept the sheltered side of the landing in long strokes, driving grit through the gaps between the stones. By the time the next cart arrived, there was a place to put its bundles without losing them in the dirt.

Tor came on that cart, furious with the ride and unwilling to admit he had needed it. Elna had put a folded coat beneath his left elbow. He tried to give it back when she helped him down.

“Keep it,” she said.

“You’ll be cold.”

“Then you can complain for both of us.”

He sat on a stone near the broken tables and began directing the work. Kael braced the first table while Rook fitted a borrowed leg. The replacement was too short. Tor noticed before they stood it upright.

“I thought the ground could make up the difference,” Rook said.

“The ground’s been here longer than you. It isn’t obliged.”

They cut another piece.

Nera worked on the windscreen. She chose canvas with more patches than original cloth, folded its worn edge around a pole and asked Kael to hold it while she passed a line through the loops. The wind filled it at once, nearly lifting him off his feet.

“Lower,” she said.

He lowered his end. The canvas snapped against the roof post.

“Less ambition, more lower.”

He laughed despite himself. She gave him a brief look, surprised by the sound, and then returned to the knot.

They fastened the screen clear of the hearth’s heat. Nessa lit a small fire and watched the smoke climb the wall. It gathered beneath the roof. She made them loosen the upper corner of the canvas. Cold air entered, but the smoke began to move outward.

“People can be cold at one end,” she said. “They can’t breathe at neither.”

The tables stood. The hearth drew. Water warmed in a black pot while Elna laid bowls along the door-table and sorted the chipped ones from those that might cut someone’s mouth.

Then they began arguing about the food.

The woman from the mill with the swollen ankle wanted a parcel sent to her own house. A man carrying two empty sacks said a common pot would be mostly water by the time everyone had dipped into it. Siv asked whether the landing would stay open after dark. Bera wanted somewhere to set a bowl down without having to defend it from the next person’s elbow.

Nessa stood with her hands on the edge of the soundest table. “We have one good hearth and enough dry wood to make it useful. We can send shares home, but then each house finds its own fuel.”

“We’ve found fuel before,” the man said.

“Yes. You have. She can’t walk to the trees.” Nessa pointed at the injured woman, who immediately looked offended at being used in the argument.

“I have neighbors,” the woman said.

“Then your neighbors can carry a cooked share from here.”

The man shook his empty sacks. “And who decides the size?”

Rook put a measuring bowl on the table. “This. It has the advantage of being unable to dislike you.”

“You can fill it differently.”

“You can watch.”

Kael thought that would settle it. It did not. Someone wanted the order of service written down. Someone else said writing only helped people who could read. A child began crying near the wall, and the sound made every adult speak more loudly.

Nera waited until there was a gap. “I can carry shares to the households beyond the reed path. Once a day. That’s what I can manage while we work on the crossing.”

Siv offered water for the pot. Bera offered to keep the place beneath the roof clear, provided people stopped putting their boots where the bowls belonged.

The injured woman said she could cut vegetables sitting down.

One by one, the common kitchen became possible. It did not become universally liked. The man with the sacks said he preferred his own fire, then accepted a portion for the people waiting at his house. Nessa gave it to him in a covered pot and told him she wanted the pot back more urgently than his approval.

Kael sat at the table with Rook’s ink and wrote what they had agreed. The daily stores would be counted in view of whoever wished to stand there. The kitchen would cook a common meal; people unable or unwilling to eat at the landing could take their share away. The work list had gaps. He left them.

“You’ll need a cook after tomorrow,” Nessa said.

He looked up. “You’re leaving?”

“I have an oven in Greyfen. I have people expecting bread from it. I can start this. I can teach someone the pot. I cannot become the mother of every hungry person who finds the river.”

Kael put down the pen. “Who can take a turn?”

Nobody answered immediately. Then the injured mill worker raised a hand.

“If someone carries the water.”

Siv tapped the yoke.

Nessa nodded. “Then sit by me. I’ll show you what happens when beans pretend to be ready.”

***

Near evening, Nera took Kael down to look at the lower landing.

It was a short descent on uneven steps. The river seemed wider from there, its opposite bank hidden in the dark movement beneath the reeds. A painted depth mark ran up one pier. Half the paint was gone.

“We’ll have to read both sides,” she said. “A current like this can give you a pleasant departure and a very bad arrival.”

Kael crouched to touch the wet mark on the stone. “Was it higher?”

“This morning. When the mill let the water through.”

They stood listening. Up at the kitchen, somebody dropped a bowl. Nessa’s voice carried down clearly, asking whether it had been dropped on purpose because accidental breakage would disappoint her more.

Nera smiled.

“You could keep a place here,” Kael said.

“A kitchen?”

“A freight store. A roof.”

“I’ve thought about it.”

He waited, careful not to turn her answer into a promise.

“A wide door,” she said. “So you don’t have to tip every barrel sideways. Shelves you can reach without standing on the load you’re supposed to be protecting. A room upstairs with a chair that belongs to nobody else.”

“An ambitious chair.”

“It would have a back. I’m tired of sitting on things designed to carry turnips.”

Kael looked back at the roof above them, trying to fit another storey over it. The chimney would have to be raised. Nera had probably measured that already.

“Would you stop traveling?”

“For a week, perhaps. I might hate it.” She looked across the river. “I’d like to be able to choose.”

Kael nodded. A fish broke the surface near the pier, briefly silver in the falling light. Nera leaned forward to see where it had gone.

When they returned, the first bowls were going out. Bera had found a cloth to put over the door-table’s old lock hole. Siv set the filled water jar beside the hearth. The injured woman tasted the beans and made a face; Nessa laughed and added something from a small packet she had concealed in her apron.

Kael carried two bowls to the household nearest the wall. He had to go back for spoons.

***

Rook had counted the remaining flour three times before he decided to answer Merrow.

He did the last count after supper, when the kitchen was occupied with washing and nobody would mistake him for a man offering to lift more sacks. The total did not improve. The new mill could feed the landing briefly. It could not supply Greyfen, the injured workers, the farther farms and the households gathering under this roof without someone going short.

Merrow’s reply to his inquiry lay beneath the lid of the document box. Rook had sent the question after the patrol came to the bakehouse. Could regular relief traffic be cleared through the current inspections? What would the steward require?

The answer was short. A route and a schedule. Loads declared in advance. Inspection at designated points. Subject to compliance, passage could be regularized.

Rook had made a living from words such as *subject*. He knew the room they left for someone else’s convenience. He also knew the traders at the south granary had grain that could be bought without entering a household on the renewal list, if somebody could get permission to move it.

He had enough coin for a first purchase. After that, the mill’s sales might cover another. The arithmetic was narrow, but it existed.

He took out the route sheet.

Nera’s schedule was practical: where loads waited, where the wheels had to be checked, how long a crew needed between the mill and the old road. Rook drew a clean version with no household names. He left out the path to his cousin’s place and the marks identifying private stores. He replaced the crew names with a number.

What remained still showed where a cart would be.

He put the pen down.

The young soldier at the bakehouse had bought his own bread. Rook remembered that vividly and distrusted himself for remembering it. A hungry man with a little coin could still carry out an order that ruined someone else’s supper.

He could ask Kael. Kael would want a guarantee Merrow would never give. He could ask Nera. She would ask whether he had seen the inspection terms, and he had only this letter.

Behind him, a child asked for more bread. Nessa explained that there would be another meal tomorrow. The child asked whether tomorrow had already begun.

Rook took up the pen again.

He added a line requiring written notice of changes to the inspection arrangements. It gave him something to point at later, which was useful. It did not give him control.

He signed his own name.

The granary carrier was waiting by the empty cart, stamping warmth into his feet. Rook handed over the copy in a folded cover addressed to Merrow. Inside he placed the order for grain and the portion of his money needed to begin it.

“No household lists,” he told the man.

“I only carry what you give me.”

“And a receipt when it gets there.”

“If the steward’s clerk gives one.”

Rook almost took the packet back. Then he thought of the sacks he had counted and let the man put it inside his coat.

He watched the cart leave until its wheels stopped sounding on the stones. The choice had not brought the flour any closer yet. It had merely sent the paper farther away.

He returned to the kitchen and took a turn at the washing. His hands were better suited to ink than cold water, but there was no reason to tell anyone that.


# Chapter 17


## A Place at the Table


The last portions of Lantern Reach’s first meal were being served when someone accused Nessa of favoring the people nearest the pot.

She looked into the measuring bowl, then at the man who had spoken. “Come and hold it.”

He hesitated.

“You’ve a better view of favoritism from there. Show us.”

He came forward, embarrassed now that people were watching. Nessa put the bowl in his hands and ladled the stew into it. A little ran over his thumb. He swore.

“Hot,” she said.

The laughter from the tables was brief and unkind. Kael saw the man’s ears redden and moved to take the filled bowl before the moment could grow teeth.

“Where does this go?” he asked.

The man pointed toward the wall. His household was sitting beneath a patched blanket. One of the smaller figures had fallen asleep with an empty cup held against the chest.

Kael carried the bowl over. When he returned, the man was helping Nessa divide the next portions.

That was how most of the evening went: an argument, a small accommodation, another argument about the accommodation. Someone objected to the draft from the open top of the windscreen. They lowered it, the smoke gathered, and the same person asked for it to be raised. Elna moved the people who could not stop coughing to the end nearest the river. Bera found a stone for the rocking bench and made three people stand while she put it under the leg.

Siv carried water until the skin beneath the yoke’s strap had rubbed raw. Anwen noticed and offered a folded cloth. Siv accepted it, shifted the jars, and kept going.

Kael tried to remember the last time he had spent an evening at a table without deciding who would watch the road. Before he found an answer, someone asked for another spoon.

Near the hearth, Tor had fallen asleep sitting upright. Bracken lay against the toe of his boot, drawing warmth through the stone. The smith’s left hand remained propped on Elna’s coat. Occasionally he woke, checked that his fingers still moved, and looked angry at having been caught doing it.

Rook ate standing near the sacks. Kael noticed him fold and unfold a piece of paper, then put it away when Nera came past.

He waited until the last serving had gone out.

“What is it?”

Rook glanced toward the table. “Can we speak somewhere that doesn’t require Nessa to stop washing?”

Nessa kept washing. “I can hear two things at once. It’s one of my misfortunes.”

Rook took a lamp and led Kael to the empty side of the landing. Nera saw them go and followed without being invited. Her glance moved from Rook's folded paper to Kael's face. She set down the bowl she had been carrying.

“I sent Merrow a copy of the relief route,” Rook said.

Kael looked at him.

“And the traveling schedule,” Rook added.

The lamp flame bent in the wind. Nera put a hand around its glass chimney.

“Which schedule?” she asked.

“The one we’ve been using for the loads. The eastern route, once the crossing is ready.”

“You sent him my crew’s movements.”

“The names aren’t on it.”

“Are their bodies on the road?”

Rook’s mouth tightened. “Yes.”

Kael had expected a longer explanation, perhaps an excuse prepared in advance. The direct answer left him angrier than evasion would have. It made the act solid.

“Why?”

Rook took out Merrow’s letter. “Because he offered regular passage after inspection. Because there’s grain for purchase at the south granary that doesn’t have a household oath tied to it, and we can’t eat any of these books. I paid for a first load. My money.”

Nera read the letter, holding it close to the lamp.

“Subject to compliance,” she said.

“I can read.”

“You can also hear yourself pretending that tells us what they’ll do.”

“They already know where the roads are.”

“They didn’t know where I meant to put people on them.”

He looked away.

Kael took the letter from Nera. He recognized the same care with which the collection order had avoided specifying what happened after the records were taken.

“When?”

“An hour ago. The carrier went north.”

“Can you call it back?”

“Not by shouting.” Rook stopped himself. “No. Not tonight.”

Nera stood with both hands on the edge of the lamp shelf. Kael had seen her face danger with less stillness.

“You should have asked,” she said.

“I thought you would say no.”

“Then you did think about asking.”

Rook nodded once.

The river moved beneath them. Kael wanted to say something that would make the page return, the route unknown, the grain arrive anyway. He had no such sentence.

“Show us exactly what you sent.”

Rook produced his retained copy. He had removed addresses and the private store marks. The route remained clear: the landing, the mill approach, the stretch of road beyond the marsh where a loaded cart had few choices. The normal traveling times were written beside each stage.

Nera traced the narrow section with a finger.

“Here,” she said. “They could stop a load without giving it room to turn.”

“It says inspection.”

“I have seen inspections.”

Rook folded the original letter along its existing crease. “So have I.”

He looked tired suddenly, stripped of the nervous brightness that usually made him seem younger than he was.

“I wanted the flour to come,” he said.

Kael could understand the wanting. He did not know what to do with it yet.

Nera took the route copy. “No crew goes blind. We tell them what you gave away. We get an answer about the road before the next convoy enters that stretch.”

“I can write again.”

“You will. You’ll ask who conducts the inspection and what they may take. You’ll ask in words a person can answer.”

Rook nodded.

“And you stop bargaining with my work without me.”

“Yes.”

She left with the copy. Kael stayed a moment longer.

“You trusted him?”

Rook gave a short, humorless laugh. “I trusted a transaction. It’s a familiar mistake.”

“That’s almost a joke.”

“I know. I’m trying to stop.”

***

There were household forms on the table by morning.

Siv had brought one from the allotment officer’s notice stand. Bera had brought another, creased into a narrow strip and tucked behind the handle of her broom. Neither had completed the line beneath the declaration.

Kael read them while Nessa stirred the morning pot.

The wording was plainer than the proclamation. A receiving household would witness and sustain the renewed covenant of the road. Grain due was entered in the next column. Below, a space marked *confirmation* preceded the place for the receiver’s sign.

“They said I could make a cross,” Bera said. “Very generous. I’ve been making my own marks longer than that boy’s been lifting a spoon.”

Siv tapped the grain column. “They filled that part before I answered. Like it was already settled.”

“What did you tell them?” Kael asked.

“That I’d bring it back.”

“Will you?”

Siv looked toward the jars beside the hearth. “I’d like to know what they’re asking me to carry first.”

Nessa put a bowl in front of her. “Carry that.”

Bera sat down with a grunt and took the place beside Siv. The unsigned forms lay between them until Kael moved them clear of the spilled broth.

More people arrived as the day warmed. Some brought completed forms and wanted to know whether the kitchen would look after their allotments. Others had refused and feared that taking food here would put them on a different list. Nessa kept serving. She grew less pleasant with each repeated question, but the size of the portions remained the same.

Kael helped Rook make a separate count of what the kitchen used. It contained sacks, measures and hours of work. The renewal forms stayed in another packet, along with notes of where they had been offered. Rook did not suggest sending anything to Merrow.

Nera spoke to the relief crew in the open yard. Rook stood beside her and explained what he had done. One worker walked away before he finished. Another asked whether the grain was at least certain to come.

“No,” Rook said.

Kael heard how much that answer cost him. It cost the others more.

***

Toma arrived toward noon with a sack of onions and news that Pell had eaten a whole egg.

He delivered both as though they might restore order to the landing.

“She complained about it afterward,” he told Mira, who had come with him. “Said the yolk was hard.”

“Then her opinions are recovering.”

“She asked if you could make the medicine taste different.”

“I can make it taste worse. That usually satisfies a patient’s curiosity.”

Toma laughed, too loudly from relief. Kael liked him for it. The farmer looked leaner than he had in Greyfen, but his eyes were clearer. Pell was back at the fold, watched by people who knew her, with Mira returning as the treatment required.

Toma had brought marks from the old supply road as well: where the ground was firm, which cart track had flooded, where Senn’s men had recently stopped a load.

“You saw them?” Kael asked.

“I saw what they left. Cut rope. Empty sacks. The driver came back on foot.”

“Did the driver say who?”

“Senn’s road. Senn’s demand. He said the guards didn’t speak.”

Nera looked at Kael. This was the next answer they needed, after a working crossing.

Toma offered to guide them through the firm ground. Mira began to object, then stopped and asked who would be with Pell. He told her. She considered it, gave him two instructions that sounded ordinary until Kael heard the fear underneath them, and agreed to look in again.

Later, after the washing, Rook scratched a board for Three Marks on a scrap of plank. He set out pale pebbles and dark beans. Bera immediately accused him of making the spaces too small for honest fingers.

“There are no dishonest fingers,” Rook said. “Only advantageous ones.”

“Then mine have had a long career.”

They played while Nessa counted the evening fuel. Tor watched with his braced wrist supported on a folded blanket. He corrected Bera’s first move, was told to mind his own broken hand, and spent the rest of the game predicting disasters under his breath.

Bera won.

Rook insisted it had been an instructional game. She swept the beans into her palm and said she had learned quite a lot about his excuses.

Kael watched Nera at the other end of the shelter. She was mending a strap. When Rook laughed, she looked up, then returned to the stitching. He had not been put outside the work. That did not mean the work between them was repaired.

Mira sat beside Kael on the low wall. She smelled of cold air and the soap Elna used.

“How’s the arm?” she asked.

He showed her without being told twice.

“An improvement,” she said.

“The arm or me?”

“I’ll need longer to judge the second.”

She checked the dressing and let his sleeve fall. Their hands stayed together for a moment, hidden between them. Across the shelter, Nessa caught the movement and looked pointedly at the stack of unwashed bowls.

Mira laughed and stood.

Kael followed her to the water barrel. They washed in companionable irritation while she told him how Pell had tried to feed Bracken a spoonful of medicine on the previous visit. Bracken, asleep against Tor’s boot, gave no sign of the narrow escape.

By night, the first complete day’s food had been cooked, served, carried to the outlying households and accounted for. There were fewer sacks and more dirty cloths than in the morning. The common kitchen existed now in the stubborn form of something people expected to find again.

Nera spread the crossing sketches on the cleared table. Tor moved his chair closer. On one side lay the broken bridge; on the other, a drawing of the old grain lighter pulled up above the reeds.

“Tomorrow,” she said, “we look at the far bank.”


# Chapter 18


## A Road Kept Open


The far bank took most of the morning to reach.

From the landing it looked close enough to throw a stone across. On foot, Kael had to follow Nera upstream until they found the old maintenance footway, cross one at a time above the narrow run, and come down through a tangle of willow and reeds. Toma went ahead with a billhook. The footway admitted a person carrying a small pack. A cart would never have reached its first turn.

At the far end, Nera stopped beside a stone almost hidden by brown weed. A line of flood debris lay above it.

“This goes under,” she said.

Kael looked back at the narrow boards. “In winter?”

“After enough rain. Sometimes before winter.”

They continued downstream. Lantern Reach’s roof appeared between the trees, across the water. Nessa was already cooking. Smoke rose in a thin column before the wind flattened it.

The river ran from north to south, bending away below the broken bridge. The far landing lay on the east bank, a little downstream of the near one. Its old stone edge was sound, though the earth behind it had washed into a hollow.

Toma struck the ground with the back of the billhook. “We’ll fill that. A loaded wheel drops there, it’ll take the axle.”

Nera put her pack down and walked the bank. Kael followed while she looked at the current, the roots, the old iron fittings driven into stone. She tested a ring near the waterline and rejected it without discussion. The rust had eaten through the underside.

Farther upstream stood a living willow with a trunk too broad for Kael’s arms. Its roots held the bank in a dense, raised web. Nera pressed her heel into the soil around it and dug at the edge with her knife.

“Here,” she said. “With a wide strop. Nothing narrow cutting into the bark.”

Kael put his hand on the trunk. It felt solid enough to hold a house.

“After rain, we check the bank,” she added. “A sound tree can leave with the earth around it.”

They marked a place for the guide rope and a separate position for the hauling block. Toma cleared the approach. Kael worked on the hollow until his back ached, carrying stones rather than trusting loose earth to support a wheel.

When they returned by the footway, Tor wanted every measurement repeated.

He sat beneath the kitchen roof with a plank across his knees, drawing in charcoal with his right hand. His left wrist was braced and swollen. He had slept badly. It made his questions shorter and his standards no easier.

“The bridge would need two spans,” he said. “Sound timber. New seats on the middle pier. More iron than we have.”

Rook stood over the sketch. “It would stay open.”

“Until something needed repairing,” Tor replied. “As bridges tend to do.”

“The ferry needs a crew every time.”

“Yes,” Nera said. “And we have people who can learn it. We don’t have two bridge spans hiding behind the flour.”

Kael looked toward the grain lighter pulled above the reeds. It was broad and flat-bottomed, eight paces long, with a low rail around the working deck. Mud had dried along one side. Young shoots grew beneath it where the hull kept the frost away.

“Can it float?”

“Most things can,” Tor said. “Briefly.”

They inspected it together.

***

The lighter had belonged to the landing before the road took most of its trade. Its hull was better than its appearance. The boards beneath the tarred cover were sound. Two seams had opened, and one section of the rail would have to be replaced. A mill hand who had worked the summer grain boats crawled beneath it and came out with black mud in his beard.

“I’d trust the bottom,” he said. “After the seams.”

Nera asked him whether he would operate the ferry if they made it safe.

“I’ll take a turn,” he said. “I’m not sleeping on it.”

“Neither am I.”

Tor had them lift the bow onto blocks. He directed the work from a stool, growing steadily more irritated at the distance between knowing what a hand should do and being able to move it there himself.

“Less,” he told Kael.

Kael eased the packing into the seam.

“Less force. More patience.”

“I’ve heard they’re interchangeable.”

“From who? Someone whose boat sank?”

Nessa came down to see how long it would take. She left with the answer that the ferry would be ready when it stopped admitting the river. Rook asked if Tor had considered a career selling reassurance. Tor pointed at a board and told him to fetch it.

By afternoon, the new near-bank post was sunk and braced. They had chosen clean timber from the mill’s remaining stock, leaving an open space in the repair pile that somebody would eventually have to fill. The guide rope was thick enough to make Kael’s hands ache merely coiling it. Nera inspected its entire length, rejecting a damaged section and knotting the shortened ends only where they would remain on shore.

The separate hauling line went through blocks at the landings. The lighter’s upstream side carried two running eyes wrapped to prevent the rope rubbing against bare iron. The guide would keep the hull from being swept downriver while the hauling line moved it across.

The first launch carried no freight.

Kael watched from the far bank, having taken the footway again with Toma and the gear for the willow. Nera and the ferry hand pushed off from the west side with a light messenger line aboard. They rowed upstream of the landing before turning across. The current caught the broad hull and took it farther south than Kael had expected.

Nera corrected early. The ferry hand pulled on the other oar. They reached the east bank below the intended berth and let the boat rest against the mud while Toma caught the bow line.

“That’s why we try it empty,” Nera said.

Kael helped pull the heavy guide across on the messenger. They laid the broad strop around the willow, protecting the bark, and tensioned the line until it rose clear of the working deck. Nera checked the running eyes herself before allowing the hull to move again.

Across the river, Tor watched with an expression that was visible even at that distance.

The empty return was easier. It also revealed water seeping through a seam beneath the forward bench.

They took the lighter out again.

Rook stood on the bank with two sacks ready for the first load and watched them unload the repair tools instead.

“Tomorrow?” he asked.

“If it’s dry where it ought to be,” Nera said.

He set the sacks down.

***

Mira changed Tor’s dressing that evening. Kael held the lamp while she checked the swollen fingers. Tor asked whether the hand looked better. She said it looked like a hand that had been broken recently.

“You’re all in a fine profession for withholding hope,” he said.

“I can give you a foolish answer if you prefer.”

He grunted. After a moment he asked for the honest one, and she gave it as gently as she could without altering it.

Elna brought him food after Mira finished. She had cut the bread into pieces he could manage. This time he thanked her before she found something else to talk about.

Kael helped Mira wash the cloths. She had come from the fold and would go back after the crossing trial. Pell was improving. Several other people were not, and Mira spoke of them by their needs rather than as a list of successes and failures.

“You’re going east after this,” she said.

“With Nera. Rook and Toma.”

“To Senn.”

“First. Then the Keep.”

She wrung out a cloth, watching the water fall into the basin. “Tell me before the plan changes.”

“I will.”

“Even if you think I’ll object.”

He nodded. She looked at him then, her expression softened by fatigue but otherwise quite serious.

“You can object too,” she said. “I don’t promise to agree.”

“That seems familiar.”

She smiled. They finished the washing before she kissed him. He liked that as much as the kiss, the ordinary task allowed to reach its end.

***

The first load crossed under a low sky the following morning.

They had tested the repaired seam at dawn. It remained dry. Nera marked the safe loading places on the deck and made Kael shift the grain twice until it lay low and balanced. The medicine box went under a tarred cloth near the middle. A small stack of clean bedding filled the remaining space.

“Four with freight,” she told the waiting workers. “Six adults if we’re carrying people light. That doesn’t mean six and whatever you can hide beneath your coat.”

The ferry hand repeated the limit. Rook made a board for it, adding simple figures beneath the words.

Kael boarded with Nera, the ferry hand and Toma. Across the river, two workers waited to help unload. Rook remained at the near-bank line, under Tor’s direction. Bracken tried to follow Kael onto the ramp and was diverted by Nessa, who had discovered that a warm brick wrapped in cloth was more persuasive than an argument.

Nera checked the guide, then the separate hauling line. She looked at the far-bank willow, its new strop pale against the bark.

“Ready,” she called.

The lighter moved out.

For the first few paces the bank seemed to slide away more quickly than the boat advanced. Kael kept his feet wide and his hands off the ropes until he was told what to hold. Toma stood beside the bedding, one palm resting on the load to feel it shift.

The current struck the bow and turned it slightly downstream. The guide tightened. Through the soles of his boots, Kael felt the whole hull take the force.

Nera adjusted their angle. The ferry hand hauled steadily, taking in line without rushing it. On the bank, Rook let out the return length through his hands. Tor shouted something about keeping the line clear of the post, and Rook moved at once.

Halfway across, a pulse of faster water came down from the north.

The lighter lifted, then settled harder against the guide. One of the grain sacks rolled a hand’s breadth. Toma stopped it with his knee.

“Leave it,” Nera said. “Stay low.”

Kael had begun to rise. He sat back before his movement could make the shift worse. Water slapped the lower rail. For a few seconds he could hear nothing except the current against the hull and the creak of the running eyes.

The far-bank strop held.

Nera waited for the pulse to pass before calling for the next pull. The ferry moved forward again, slower now. Kael watched the landing grow distinct: a new plank, a dark wet patch on the old stone, the white knuckles of a worker waiting with the receiving line.

The bow touched the berth.

“Hold,” Nera called.

They held while the line was secured. Only then did Toma step onto the landing and take the first sack. Kael followed with the medicine box. His legs felt foolishly uncertain on ground that no longer moved.

They carried the freight beyond the washed-out hollow, onto the place they had filled with stone. The workers checked the sacks and bedding against Nera’s list. The medicine box reached a dry shelf in the waiting cart. Nothing remained aboard except the crew’s gear.

Nera made Kael look back before they left the landing.

“There,” she said. “A crossing isn’t finished when you reach the bank. It’s finished when the load can go on.”

He might once have heard only a lesson aimed at him. This time he saw that she was speaking to the ferry hand as well, and to Toma, and to herself while she checked the next job.

They crossed back empty. Tor demanded an account of the faster current. Nera gave him the timing, the pull on the guide and the small movement of the sack. He added another mark to the inspection board beside the warning about rain.

Nobody described the ferry as finished. It was working. That was enough to begin with.

***

Rook handed his locked document bag to Elna before they left.

“The household book and the old tally leaves,” he said. “The copies we’re taking have the addresses removed.”

“I know what a copy is.”

“I know you do.”

She took the bag and put it with her own dry things. Anwen kept the shrine originals separately. The mill’s signed account had been copied again, one set staying with the wheelkeeper and one going east in Kael’s oilskin.

Kael checked his own packet: Bram’s battered ledger, the copied patrol receipt, the sanctuary and milling extracts, the current grain forms. Oren’s brass crow remained wrapped apart, its red cord tucked beneath the cloth so it could not catch. He closed the pouch and tightened the fastening.

Bracken had settled beside Tor’s boot. His strained hind foot was no longer troubling him on level ground, but the warm brick had settled the question of the morning’s travel. Kael crouched and rubbed the unplated hollow beneath his jaw.

“Look after him,” he told Tor.

“Which of us?” Nessa asked.

Tor looked down at the small creature and, with his good hand, shifted the brick closer.

Mira met Kael at the ramp. She gave him a clean roll of cloth and told him not to confuse carrying it with permission to need it. He kissed her without trying to make the moment private from everyone on the landing. Rook found something urgent to inspect on the rope.

Nera checked the straps on Toma’s pack. The farmer carried little enough to move quickly, along with the supply-road notes he had brought from the fold. Rook wore his smaller satchel. He had shown them the second letter to Merrow before sending it; no answer had yet come.

The ferry took the four of them east with the ferry hand. They traveled light. At the far bank, Kael helped secure the line before stepping ashore.

Behind them, the common kitchen’s smoke rose above the roof. Tor’s voice carried across the water, telling somebody to move a hand before the rope took the skin off it.

Toma pointed out the firmer path toward Senn’s road. Nera took the lead. Kael followed, with the records high against his chest and the sound of the working ferry still audible behind him.


# Part Three The Price of Bread



# Chapter 19


## A Soldier Without a Banner


The first sentry had been dead long enough for rain to find its way through the soles of its boots.

It stood where the road narrowed between two heaps of quarried stone, holding a spear across its body. A coat hung from the spearhead. Someone had put it there to dry. Beyond it, the camp smelled of boiled leather and old cabbage, with the sweet, shut-in smell Kael had learned to notice before he saw a body.

Nera stopped at the first heap of stone.

“Is that supposed to frighten us?”

“Yes,” Rook said. “I object to how little effort it's taking.”

There was no wind. A strip of faded cloth drooped from the watchtower, its device cut away. Kael counted tents, then cooking fires, then the gaps between the tents where other fires ought to have been. Six shelters. One fire. No horse lines in use. The place had been built for a company and was being kept by a man who had stopped admitting that the company was gone.

Toma moved to the verge and studied the boot marks around the stone heap. Kael gave him the pack containing their records.

“Stay back until we know what holds them.”

Toma set the pack behind the stones and loosened the water skin at his belt. Nera checked the path behind them. Rook watched her do it.

“I took the houses off the copy,” he said.

“You said.”

“And the names.”

“You said that too.”

The schedule he had given Merrow was folded into the silence between them. It had been there all morning, through a breakfast of cold bread and the ferry crossing. Nera had not raised her voice about it. Kael would have preferred her shouting. Rook might have found a place to put an answer.

The sentry turned its head.

A fly crawled across one pale eye and disappeared behind the lid. Its spear lowered until the point rested over the road.

“Captain Senn,” Kael called.

From the tent nearest the tower a man emerged carrying a chipped washing bowl. He wore a plain coat buckled over mail and had shaved around a nick on his chin. His hair was close-cropped and almost entirely grey. There was nothing theatrical about him. Even his annoyance appeared measured.

“You're early.”

“We didn't arrange a time.”

“The deliveries generally come after noon.” Senn emptied the bowl beside his tent. “State your business.”

“The men taking grain from the lower road. The people who don't come back from your toll.”

“My men have not been down there in eleven days.”

“Whose men are these?”

Kael nodded towards the sentry.

Senn set the bowl upside down on a stool. “They hold the approach.”

Behind him another figure moved between the tents, dragging a foot. A third stood beneath the tower with a shield resting on its raised knee. Their equipment matched nothing: a mill hook, a buckled sword, a woman's leather belt threaded through an army scabbard. Kael could see why a traveler might call them soldiers from a distance. Nearer, distance became something to wish for.

Nera came forward to stand at Kael's shoulder.

“We have people coming through tomorrow,” she said. “They won't pay you.”

“Which people?”

“Hungry ones. You can inspect the empty cooking pots if you'd like.”

Senn regarded her, then Toma. His gaze stopped on the pack behind the stones.

“The road is under my protection.”

“Then it's a road,” she said. “Open it.”

He drew a short rod from his belt. Three narrow rings had been wired to one end. When he struck it against the stool, the sentry's spear jerked aside.

Kael started forward.

Senn struck again.

The shield-bearer came out from under the tower and the man with the hook crossed behind it. The sentry thrust.

Kael caught the shaft against his scabbard and felt the wood skid across the healing cut on his left arm. He turned with it, shoulder to the sentry's chest. The body was heavier than its wasted appearance suggested. Something packed beneath the coat shifted and grated. He pushed it into the stone heap and stepped clear as the hook struck where his ankle had been.

“Back to Toma,” he told Nera.

She was already moving sideways, waving the farmer behind the stone heap.

Senn had not drawn his sword.

“Set down your weapons,” he said. “We can discuss passage.”

The sentry recovered its spear. Its mouth opened. Kael heard a wet click, followed by two syllables that belonged to no instruction Senn had given.

The shield-bearer advanced.

Kael retreated past a fallen tent peg. The creature followed in a slow, economical step, keeping its shield high. It had once learned that movement somewhere. Or some fragment had found a body capable of repeating it. Kael could not tell, and the distinction was useless while a sword was coming at his face.

He parried late. Iron rang. Pain ran up his forearm and stopped at the elbow.

The hook caught the tent rope behind him.

Kael ducked under the shield edge, drove his shoulder into the bearer and felt it yield a fraction. Enough to get past. He reached the dead firepit before both creatures turned.

A road captain watching a fight ought to have watched the armed man. Senn was watching the sentry at the stones.

Kael looked too.

The third ring on the command rod had come loose. It lay in the dust beside the stool. Senn's eyes moved from the ring to the sentry and back.

Kael understood just enough to be frightened.

“Can you stop them?”

“Put your sword down.”

“Can you?”

The hook came down. Kael let it bite into the firepit's charred beam, pinned the shaft with his boot and cut through the creature's wrist. The hand remained around the wood. The body continued towards him.

Nera threw a stool.

It struck the shield-bearer at knee height. The creature staggered against a tent and brought half the canvas down. Kael heard Rook swearing as he hauled the pack farther from the road.

Senn hit the rod against the watchtower.

The two intact rings sounded clear and thin. The sentry faltered. The shield-bearer turned halfway towards its captain, sword still raised.

Then, from inside the collapsed tent, came a child's small, bewildered voice.

“There's room.”

Kael almost looked for the child.

The hook-bearer struck him with its severed arm.

He went down on one knee. Something hard inside the coat knocked against his temple. He rolled beneath the next blow and saw the coat open: scraps of cloth, a breastbone too small for the body, rusted buckles tied together. A vessel assembled from what the road had left.

Senn looked sick.

“There's room,” the voice said again.

“You hear them every night?” Kael asked.

The captain reached for his sword.

“They quiet when the camp quiets.”

Nera had found an iron crowbar among the quarried stones. She stood with it across her body, feet braced in the ruts. Kael could see her calculating the distance to Senn. He shook his head once. Behind the captain lay the tower stairs, the clear space, the commands the dead kept attempting to obey.

“Tell me what you make them do,” Kael said.

“Hold. Challenge. Close.”

“What else?”

“Advance when I give the second—”

“What makes them stop?”

Senn's jaw worked.

Kael gave ground. The shield-bearer followed. The torn tent dragged around its legs.

“They stand down at relief,” the captain said.

“Who relieves them?”

No answer.

A laugh escaped Rook, sharp and frightened. “That's a vacancy you should have advertised.”

Kael circled towards the tower. The creatures turned with him. The sentry had abandoned the stones and was coming along the road, spear point dipping and lifting in time with its damaged gait.

“You've been keeping watch with something you can't dismiss.”

“I could dismiss them.”

“Then do it.”

“From the post.”

The shield came at Kael's chest. He caught its rim and pulled sideways, giving the sword an opening he had to trust the bearer would take. It did. He stepped outside the thrust and shoved the creature down into the firepit. The old beam broke under its weight.

“Nera. The stairs.”

She moved before he finished. The lever struck across the hook-bearer's shins. It fell; she kicked the weapon beyond its reach and jumped clear of the reaching hand.

Kael seized Senn by the coat and drove him towards the tower.

The captain hit him in the mouth.

It was a competent punch, short and ugly. Kael tasted blood. He pushed harder.

Senn's boot found the first stair. The sentry thrust between them. Kael trapped the spear against the railing with his sword, but the pressure forced the blade towards his own throat. Below him, Nera brought the lever down on the shaft. Once. Twice. Wood split.

Senn caught the upper rail and climbed.

Kael followed. The hook-bearer scrambled beneath the stairs, reaching through them. A blackened finger caught his trouser cuff. He tore free, leaving cloth behind.

At the platform, the wind found them. Kael could see the empty horse lines now, and a row of boots outside a tent no one slept in. Senn had lined them neatly, toes towards the door.

The captain lifted his rod.

“Relief of the west approach,” he said.

His voice broke on the last word.

Below, the shield-bearer dragged itself upright.

Senn tried again, louder. The sentry turned its head.

“Lay down. At relief, lay down.”

The rod rattled without its third ring.

“They can't hear it properly,” Senn said.

Kael took the rod from him. The captain reached, an angry reflex, and stopped when Kael raised his sword.

For a moment they stood close enough to smell each other's breath. Senn's eyes were grey, bloodshot from poor sleep. Kael could have cut him. A small part of him wanted the simplicity of it.

Instead he struck the flat of his blade against the remaining rings.

The note was harsher. Longer.

Below, the sentry grounded its broken spear.

“Again,” Senn said.

Kael struck.

Senn spoke the relief order carefully, in the voice of a man teaching a recruit to fold a blanket. The shield-bearer lowered its sword. The hook-bearer crouched beneath the stairs.

The small voice inside it said, “All of us?”

No one answered.

They waited until the sentry's hand opened.

***

The dead did not become harmless because they were quiet.

Nera made Senn repeat the order while Kael gathered their weapons. They locked the hook and swords in the empty grain shed. Rook fetched the fallen ring and put it on the stool as though it were a dangerous insect. The sentry remained beside the stone heap, head bowed over the broken spear.

There were only three dead guards. Senn had allowed travelers to think there were more.

“Your living men?” Kael asked.

“Two took work at the Keep. One went home. The rest drifted.”

“After the raids.”

“After the wages stopped.”

Nera opened the grain shed. Four sacks stood against its back wall. She drove her knife into one seam and let a handful fall into her palm.

“Seed barley.”

“It was a levy.”

“It was somebody's spring.”

Senn looked past her towards the tower.

Kael wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. The cut on his arm had begun to bleed again, slowly, beneath the sleeve. He was thirsty. He wanted to sit down and be cruel to someone who would not surprise him.

Senn unbuckled his sword belt.

The act was awkward with Kael's blade held near his throat. Nera took the belt before it fell, then the little knife from the captain's boot. She found a second one inside his coat and gave him a look.

“For eating,” he said.

“You'll use a spoon.”

Rook bent to pick up the command rod. Kael stopped him.

“Leave it.”

“Someone ought to mend the ring.”

“Tor can look at it. Later.”

Rook straightened slowly. He looked older when no one laughed with him.

Toma brought the water skin. He kept his eyes on the sentry while Kael drank until his teeth hurt.

Before the light failed, Nera sent the farmer back along the path they had come. They needed food, carts for the seized goods and someone willing to help guard a living man. Toma knew the sound ground now. He took her note and promised to return with the first kitchen load.

Senn sat on the stool beside the abandoned washing bowl.

“There's an account,” he said.

Kael lowered the cup.

“In the tent. I began it when the men left.”

“Of what?”

The captain rubbed the clean patch on his chin. The shaving nick had opened.

“The gate,” he said. “And what I did after.”

Nera tied his hands in front of him, leaving enough room for the circulation. Senn watched her knot.

“Will you hear it?”

Kael looked towards the grain shed, where the four sacks stood in the cold. Then at the spear in the road, and the coat still hung on it.

“In the morning,” he said.

He had no wish to hear a frightened man explain himself before he had seen what that man had kept.


# Chapter 20


## The Officer's Hand


Senn's account began with the weather.

Kael read the first line twice, then set the paper on the table.

“It was raining.”

“It was.”

“You've given it a whole paragraph.”

The captain sat opposite him with his hands unbound. A length of rope passed around his waist and the chair, loose enough to let him write. Nera had taken away the lamp, the stool with the sharp leg and a tin of blacking she said could be thrown in someone's eyes. Senn had objected more strongly to losing the blacking than to the rope.

“I was establishing the conditions.”

“You were establishing somewhere to hide.”

Outside the tent, a cart wheel knocked over stone. Toma had returned from Lantern Reach with Mira and Elna, three of Nera's workers and enough bread to make Rook's welcome sound less strained. They had brought two small handcarts: one for the kitchen's pots and one with bedding and meal for the journey to Vargan. Nera's tar-lined tool chest rode on the second. She had cleared it for the papers, which were beginning to suffer in their packs.

Toma was helping unload. Bracken watched from a box beneath Mira's seat. She had brought him at Tor's request after he spent half the night scratching at the ramp, unwilling to accept that the ferry had carried Kael beyond his reach. The dead guards had been wrapped in spare canvas and carried into the ruined lime store after Senn repeated the relief command. Kael had watched their hands until the door was barred.

Rain began to tap the canvas.

Senn glanced up.

“No,” Kael said.

The captain pushed the account across the table.

There were seven pages. In the middle, the writing became smaller and more regular, as though accuracy could be reached by squeezing the letters closer together. Kael found dates, quantities, a description of the old sanctuary gate. Beneath the heading Disposition of Persons, Senn had written: *Delivered to the civil enclosure.*

“Delivered.”

“That was the term.”

“And after they went through the gate?”

“We held the northern track.”

“Against whom?”

“People trying to leave.”

Senn's right hand lay flat on the table. The little finger had healed crooked from an old break. As he spoke, it lifted slightly, then pressed down.

“How old were you?”

“Twenty-two.”

Kael tried to picture the man without the lines at the corners of his mouth, without the careful grey crop, standing on a muddy track with a spear he believed someone else had the right to tell him to use. He could picture it too easily.

“Who gave the order?”

“Captain's office. Vargan seal.”

“Edric?”

Senn looked at him.

“He was a child.”

“I know. I want it written.”

The captain took up the pen.

Kael waited while he added the sentence. They had gone over the present patrols before dawn. Senn's raids were his own. The Keep's recovery men wore different equipment, carried written warrants and sometimes bought grain from the same frightened households they later searched. Those distinctions would be tiresome to an angry crowd. They remained distinctions.

“Halvern,” Kael said.

The pen stopped.

“He was with the escort.”

“Did he know the road behind was closed?”

“He found out.”

“And then?”

Senn looked towards the tent flap. Elna's voice carried through it, sharp with an argument over whether a basket of onions counted as breakfast.

“He came to the track. Asked where we expected them to go. He had a woman with him who kept holding out a pass. Her thumb had rubbed the ink away.”

“What did you do?”

“Sent her back.”

“And Halvern?”

“He wouldn't go.”

The captain's hand curled around the pen.

Kael had asked men about fights before. Usually they offered a distance, a weapon, who moved first. Senn gave him the woman's spoiled pass. He had been carrying it in his head for thirty-two years, and had still found room there to take seed barley from a farmer last week.

“Did you see him die?”

“Yes.”

“Write what you saw. Leave out what you were told afterwards.”

Senn bent over the page.

***

Elna ate standing up.

The tent smelled of wet wool and the bread Mira had warmed over the camp's single fire. A little fat glistened on the crust. Senn watched Elna break it and did not touch his own portion.

“So,” she said. “You're him.”

“There were several officers.”

“I'm sure that made everything easier.”

Kael shifted, but Elna turned her eyes on him and he stayed where he was. She had asked to come. He had told her what Senn had admitted. The rest belonged to her.

“Harl stood at the village gate,” she said.

The captain's face changed at the name, but Kael could not tell whether it was recognition or an effort to provide one.

“I don't remember every—”

“He was broad. Brown beard then. He'd put flour on his boots to stop the leather squeaking. Fool thought anyone cared if he squeaked.”

Senn looked at the table.

“I remember a man taking the crossbar.”

“That could have been him.”

“Yes.”

“Could have.”

The captain nodded.

Elna put the remaining bread in her pocket. She took a folded scrap from the other pocket and laid it before him. Kael had seen her carry it before, though never spread it open. Three lines in a younger hand. A little stain shaped like a thumb.

“He wrote that the strangers went on under escort. I used to read that when people talked.”

Senn said nothing.

“Did anyone go on?”

“Some escaped the first closure.”

“That isn't what I asked.”

“No.”

The tent flap moved in the wind. Elna caught it with her heel.

“No,” Senn said again. “The people recorded as returned did not leave by the northern track.”

She folded the scrap along its old crease.

“Did he know?”

“I can't tell you.”

“You can tell me whether a man at the gate would have heard.”

Senn's eyes met hers.

“Yes.”

Elna sat down so abruptly that the bench scraped the earth.

Mira put a cup near her hand. Elna did not take it. For a while there was only rain and Toma outside trying to quiet Bracken, who had decided the onion basket contained something intended for him.

“I was going to slap you,” Elna said.

Senn's mouth tightened.

“I spent the journey thinking about it. Chose which hand. Took my ring off.”

She held up her bare finger, irritated by the admission.

“Do you want me to stand?”

“Oh, sit still.”

Mira turned away before the smile reached her mouth. It was a small, unwilling thing, and Elna saw it.

“Yes, very funny. I've come all this way and he offers to help.”

The sound that left Elna then could have become laughter. It broke before it chose. Mira sat beside her. Kael watched Elna press the folded scrap against her knee until the paper bent.

Senn reached for his untouched bread.

Nera stopped his hand.

“Wait.”

He looked at her.

“The workers you've been taking from. They need the barley back.”

“I have an inventory.”

“Good. We'll use it.”

***

They argued about custody while rain ran down the back of Kael's collar.

Rook wanted Senn secured at the nearest place with a lock and other people to watch it. Toma said the nearest such place was the Keep, whose stamp appeared on too many of the papers. Nera pointed out that keeping a prisoner consumed food, hands and hours, and she would not have the work quietly added to her crew.

“I can walk,” Senn said.

“You can also stop talking.”

Nera had spread a piece of canvas over the cart. The four sacks of barley stood on it beside two confiscated axes, a bag of coins, three bracelets and a child's shoe. They had found the shoe in the levy chest. Senn said it had been used to hide money. Toma put it apart from the rest, sole down, as if its owner might still need the shape.

“Who counts all this?” Rook asked.

“You,” Nera said.

He started to answer, then changed his mind.

Mira took Kael by the sleeve and led him behind the tent. He went because the set of her jaw promised a worse examination if he resisted.

“Arm.”

He pulled the sleeve up.

“You've torn the edge.”

“The dead man was impolite.”

“You could have explained that you'd already been treated.”

“He wouldn't listen.”

Her fingers were warm despite the rain. She cleaned the cut, checked the skin around it, then wrapped a narrow fresh strip over the old dressing. He watched the damp curls at her temple. She had been awake late at the kitchen; there was flour in the fold of her cuff.

“What?” she asked.

“Nothing.”

“Then stop looking as if you're about to apologize for my face.”

He leaned down and kissed her, lightly, because he had split his lip.

She touched the corner of his mouth.

“That one too?”

“It was the captain.”

“Of course it was.”

She kissed the uninjured corner, then pushed him back towards the others.

“Eat something before you start deciding where to put a prisoner.”

By midday they had an arrangement nobody liked enough to mistake for a victory. Senn would travel under guard. The two workers who had come from Lantern Reach agreed to take the first watches for wages from the recovered levy, subject to a public account of the money. One was an older ferryman with a stiff knee; the other a broad-shouldered woman who had driven supply carts before Nera hired her. Both inspected the rope and the captain's boots themselves.

Kael wrote the seizure list. Rook counted. Toma checked the grain against marks he recognized. Nera signed for custody until they could bring the matter before people with claims and a place to hear them.

Senn watched each signature.

When Kael slid the confession pages towards him, he moved his hand away.

“Is that the price?”

“What?”

“Of staying alive.”

Kael felt everyone listening.

“If I don't sign,” Senn said, “do you take me into the trees?”

“No.”

“Do you leave me here with them?”

“No.”

The captain looked towards the lime store.

“You'll face what you've done,” Kael said. “I won't promise what people will decide. This account is another matter.”

Elna stood beneath the cart's lifted canvas. Water dripped past her face.

“He could lie at the hearing,” she said.

“He could.”

“He could tell only half.”

Kael looked down at the report. *Delivered to the civil enclosure.* A tidy phrase. He could hear his own old clerk asking whether *position untenable* would do.

“Yes,” he said.

Senn picked up the pen.

He made three corrections before he signed. In one place he changed *fired upon* to *I ordered the archers*. In another he crossed out *attempted departure* and wrote *people running back towards us*. He left the weather paragraph intact.

“I remember the rain,” he said.

Kael let it stand.

They made two copies. Toma read aloud while Kael compared the second, stumbling over one word and asking rather than pretending. The original went into Nera's document chest. Kael took a copy in his oilskin. Rook carried the other in a false bottom beneath the bread.

When they finished, Senn asked for the little eating knife.

The cart driver handed him a spoon.

***

They left the camp before dusk.

The dead remained inside the lime store with the command rod locked in an outer box. Kael marked the door with charcoal and sent a message back with the kitchen's cart: Anwen would need to come with someone who understood the oldest burials. Until then, no traveler was to open it. The worker returning with the pots also took the four sacks of barley and Nera's list of the farmers owed them. The rest of the recovered property stayed under seal for claims at the Keep. Kael disliked leaving the work incomplete. He liked less the idea of pretending a wrapped body had become only a body because the road needed clearing.

Senn walked between his guards, hands tied in front. He had refused a cloak until Nera pointed out that treating his fever would be more work for everyone.

Rook stayed near the cart, checking the bread compartment each time a rut tilted it.

Elna rode beside Toma. She had put her ring back on.

At the bend, Kael looked back. The tower's cut banner had vanished into the rain. He could still see the little row of boots beside the empty tent.

Senn was looking too.

“Were they friends?” Kael asked.

“The living ones?”

“Yes.”

“For a time.”

Kael waited.

“I thought they'd come back when I made the road pay.”

Ahead, Nera called for the cart to stop while she checked a washed-out section. Rook went to help, but she sent him to the far wheel without looking at him.

The captain lowered his head and kept walking until the guard's hand closed on his shoulder.

They waited there together for the road to be made passable.


# Chapter 21


## Hold the Rear


The answer to Rook's second letter reached them at the last farm before the causeway.

A granary carrier brought it with the purchase receipt. Merrow had named the road patrol as the inspecting authority. Loads would be examined under the household provision; disputed goods would be held against a written receipt. The passage of persons would not be hindered.

Nera read that sentence to everyone, then showed them the narrow road on Rook's copy.

“He hasn't said what makes a sack disputed,” she said.

“No,” Rook answered. “He hasn't.”

They could send the cart back. Senn and his guards could use the ridge track, and the papers could go with them. The meal and the bedding would take another day to reach Vargan by the lower road; the older guard had already limped through the last mile, and the sky promised rain.
