Mira wanted shelter for the night. Toma would go on foot if that was the choice. Elna asked Nera to inspect the crossing herself before deciding. Kael looked at the promise about persons. He had seen worse promises honored, and better ones broken.

“If they dispute the meal, we ask for the receipt,” he said. “We take Senn and the papers on.”

Nera folded the letter. “We try it once. If the bank isn't sound, the cart goes back.”

She walked the causeway before she allowed a wheel onto it.

The old road crossed a flooded meadow on a ridge of packed stone. Willow roots held the western edge; the eastern edge had been cut away by the last rain. Halfway across, a culvert fed brown water beneath the road through an opening too narrow for the branches gathered against it.

Kael stood with her at the first marker.

“Your cart first?” he asked.

“People first. Then the cart. You at the rear.”

He looked towards the willows. The road beyond rose towards Vargan, out of sight behind a ruined toll shelter. He disliked how neatly the shelter covered the approach.

“I could go ahead.”

“You could. Toma's already there.”

She pointed. The farmer was testing the next stretch with a pole. His daughter was safe at the fold with people she knew, and he had spent the morning discussing the merits of two different varieties of winter bean with Elna. Kael had mistaken the conversation for an absence of worry until he saw how often Toma touched the little knitted cap tucked through his belt.

Nera tightened a fastening on the chest.

“I lead. You stay behind the last person. If we stop, you stop. If the cart goes over, let the cart go.”

“And if somebody's under it?”

“Then we'll need you there.”

She left him with that.

The captain's two guards crossed with Senn between them. His hands were tied, but the rope to the older guard's belt had been removed for the water. Elna and Mira followed. Toma led Bracken by a loose cord, keeping the animal from investigating every floating branch.

Rook stayed by the cart.

Nera came back.

“I told you to walk ahead.”

“Wheel's dragging.”

“I can see it.”

“The left brake wants another finger's room.”

She knelt and adjusted it herself. Rook stood helplessly over her. When she rose, he gave her his place beside the wheel without another word.

They had traveled perhaps fifty paces when the men came out of the toll shelter.

There were seven. Kael counted the crossbows first: two, both already spanned. The rest carried short spears or clubs. Yellow cloth showed at their shoulders beneath dark leather capes. Vargan work, made in batches. The same flattened wire held the warrant case on the leader's belt.

“Inspection,” the leader called.

His voice was bored. Kael had learned to be wary of men who sounded bored with what they were about to do.

Nera stopped the cart on the firmest stretch.

“Road's narrow. Let the people through.”

“Everyone stays.”

One crossbowman moved to the high bank. The other covered the road behind Kael. They had measured the place before choosing it.

Rook went pale.

Kael saw him looking at the warrant before the leader had unfolded it.

“That's my mark,” Rook said.

Nera turned.

“The fork. I marked it on the copy.”

“You told us.”

“I know.”

The leader held the paper against his chest to keep it from the rain.

“Relief transport under review. Unlicensed grain. Materials removed from estate works. Statements and copies pertaining to Vargan administration.”

“Read the signature,” Nera said.

“Steward Merrow, acting under the household provision.”

“Show it.”

The man lifted the page.

Rook shut his eyes.

Kael could see Merrow's name from where he stood. Under it, in another hand, somebody had added a direction: *Hold at the culvert before the upper fork. Persons may proceed after examination of loads.*

Persons might proceed. Nobody had thought to include that part in the spoken order.

Nera read it twice.

“You can examine on the high road. Let us off this bank.”

The leader glanced towards the crossbowman above her. He was young, with a patchy beard and wet sleeves too long for his arms. He appeared less certain than the man reading the warrant.

“The load stays.”

“It weighs more than the edge will take.”

“Then unload it.”

“Here?”

The leader folded the paper. “You heard.”

Senn stepped forward.

The older guard caught his elbow.

“I know the provision,” Senn said. “It doesn't authorize armed seizure on a public crossing.”

The patrol leader looked at him for the first time.

“Well. Captain.”

Something unpleasant passed between them, too quick for Kael to name.

“You can discuss it at the gate.”

“I intend to.”

The man's attention moved to the rope around Senn's wrists.

His smile had no humor in it.

Nera put her hand on the cart brake.

“People go first.”

The leader caught her wrist.

Kael moved.

He got three steps before the crossbow on the bank snapped.

The bolt struck the cart's side and went through a sack of meal. White dust burst into the damp air. Bracken flattened himself beneath the wheel. Toma pulled the cord and shouted at the animal as though this were a domestic disagreement that could still be solved with volume.

Nera drove her elbow into the leader's face.

The younger crossbowman stared at the empty bow in his hands.

“Hold!” the leader shouted, and nobody did.

The cart lurched.

Rook grabbed the shaft. The wheel nearest the water struck a buried stone and slid outwards. Nera hauled on the brake. Kael reached the rear corner as the bank beneath it gave a soft, horrible sigh.

“Leave it!” she shouted.

Elna scrambled back towards the firm road. Mira seized the back of her coat. The cart swung sideways, its load shifting towards the broken edge.

Rook held on.

Kael saw the wheel rise. He shouted Rook's name, but the shaft caught the trader under the ribs before he could let go. Rook hit the willow trunk with a sound Kael felt in his own chest.

The cart dropped.

Its rear axle wedged across the culvert. One wheel hung over the water, spinning. Grain poured from the torn sack and vanished into the flooded meadow.

The patrol leader came at Nera with his club.

Kael met him between the cart and the bank. There was room for one sword stroke and no room to miss. He struck the club hand with the flat, caught the man's collar and drove him into the cart rail. The man went down, gasping through a bloodied nose.

“Back,” Kael said to the nearest spearman.

The spear point shook.

Behind him, Nera was giving orders.

“Mira, Rook. Toma, the cord. Elna, go up. Take Bracken. You two, keep Senn moving.”

The older guard hesitated between his prisoner and the injured man.

“Your job,” Nera snapped. “Do your job.”

He took Senn uphill.

Kael faced the second crossbow. The man holding it had not fired. He was watching his leader in the mud and trying to judge whether the warrant would protect him from what happened next.

“Put it down,” Kael said.

“Give us the chest.”

“No.”

“It's in the order.”

“Then come and read it to me.”

The man looked at the blade.

A cry came from beneath the cart.

For one startled moment Kael thought it was another fragment. Then the woman guarding Senn called back from the slope.

“He's under there.”

One of the patrolmen had fallen with the axle. His sleeve was caught between the wheel and the stone lip of the culvert. Water slapped his mouth each time he tried to pull free.

Nera saw him.

“Kael.”

The crossbow stayed pointed at his chest.

Kael sheathed his sword.

The man with the bow flinched at the movement.

“He's one of yours,” Kael said. “Help.”

There was a moment when nothing seemed to happen. The water moved. The torn sack emptied. Rook made a sound he was trying to keep inside himself.

Then the crossbowman lowered his weapon.

Kael climbed down beside the cart. Mud filled his boot. The trapped man seized his arm with both hands.

“Let go,” Kael said.

The man held tighter.

“Let go or I can't lift it.”

Nera had the cart lever. She pushed it through the spokes from above and braced against the road. Toma laid the rope around the trapped man's chest, keeping clear of his throat. The crossbowman took the other end without looking at any of them.

“Now,” Nera said.

The wheel rose half an inch.

Kael tore the sleeve away from the axle. Skin came with it. The man screamed and swallowed water.

“Pull.”

They dragged him out onto the stone. He vomited against Kael's boot and began to cry.

Nera did not wait for thanks.

“The chest.”

It was trapped under two flour sacks on the tilted bed. Kael could reach it if he stepped onto the axle. He could also feel the axle moving.

“Which?” she asked.

“The statements.”

She understood. The chest held originals and copied registers, but the essential accounts traveled in more than one place. He had Senn's copy and the older extracts against his body. Rook had a second packet under the bread. Some of the rest could be lost.

Nera cut the ropes around the load.

The chest slid towards Kael. He caught its handle, felt his left forearm threaten to give, then got his right hand beneath it. Toma hauled the other end. They pushed it uphill into Mira's waiting hands.

The axle broke.

Kael jumped as the cart rolled into the water.

A spear struck the road where his foot had been. The patrol leader had got up. His broken nose spread blood across his mouth.

“Those papers stay.”

Nera threw the empty brake block at him.

It hit his shoulder and sent him back against the shelter wall. She took the chest from Mira, passed it to Toma and turned to the remaining patrolmen.

“You have the grain,” she said. “You have your inspection. If you want another man in that ditch, put him there yourselves.”

No one moved.

On the high road, Elna had picked up a fist-sized stone. She held it at her side, ready.

The leader looked from her to Kael, then down at the ruined cart.

“Record the obstruction,” he told the crossbowman.

Kael almost laughed.

Nera touched his elbow.

“Rear,” she said.

He stayed until the last of their people was above the fork. Then he backed away, watching the men below reclaim what floated within reach.

***

Rook could not get a full breath.

Mira had him propped against a milestone with his knees drawn up. He tried to tell her something and stopped halfway through the first word.

“Small breaths,” she said. “Don't prove anything.”

His eyes found Kael.

“The copy,” he managed.

“We have the chest.”

“Bread.”

Kael looked downhill. One of the patrolmen was hauling the wicker hamper onto the bank. The false bottom would not survive a determined search.

“I have another copy,” he said.

Rook closed his eyes.

“He said people could pass,” Rook whispered.

Nera stood over him with mud to her knees. She took Merrow's reply from her coat. The rain had softened one corner.

“I read it too.”

“We should have taken the ridge.”

“Perhaps.”

She crouched so he would not have to look up at her.

“I chose to bring the cart. That's mine. But they chose this place from the route you sent before any of us could answer you.”

Rook nodded, then winced.

“You told us afterward. You helped us get this.” She folded the letter again. “It didn't give us the choice we had before you sent it.”

Mira put a hand between them, checking the movement of his ribs. Nera waited until she had finished.

“I'm not asking you why you wanted bread,” Nera said. “I'm asking whether you'll do this again when someone offers you a better bargain.”

“No.”

“You said that quickly.”

“I haven't much breath.”

It was almost a joke. No one helped him with it.

Rook looked towards the flooded meadow. The broken cart lay on its side. A bundle of blankets drifted away beneath the willows.

“I thought I could make one part of it work,” he said. “Deliveries. Times. I know those things.”

Nera stood.

“You're off the route book.”

He nodded.

“When we need a copy, somebody else reads it before it leaves us.”

“Yes.”

“We'll carry you to the Keep. Mira will look after you. Don't turn either of those into an answer to what you've done.”

He pressed his lips together and looked down at his hands.

Kael found two straight branches in the hedge. Elna took off her outer coat to help make the litter, complaining that it was her good one and that Rook would owe her a lining. He tried to answer.

“Save it,” she said. “I charge by the word.”

When they lifted him, he made no sound. That frightened Kael more than the cries at the culvert.

They went slowly after that. Senn walked under guard behind the litter, no longer asking to explain the warrant. Toma carried the chest. Nera carried the route book herself.

At the next rise Kael stopped to count them.

Nera saw him doing it and waited.

“All here,” he said.

“The blankets aren't.”

“No.”

“And most of the meal.”

“No.”

She looked down the road, towards the first high wall of Vargan.

“We'll need somewhere dry before we argue again.”

Kael took his end of the litter. Rook's fingers tightened around the branch as they lifted, and the little procession moved on.


# Chapter 22


## The Man Who Keeps Keys


There were three queues outside Vargan's lower gate.

The first was for grain. The second was for the papers required to join the first. The third consisted of people who had been sent away from one of the other two and refused to go home.

Merrow walked among them carrying a ring of keys and a bowl of hot chestnuts.

He gave a chestnut to a child, told a woman her household number belonged on the left of the form, then saw Rook on the litter.

His hand stopped above the bowl.

Kael waited until he came close.

“The culvert.”

Merrow looked at the mud on their clothes, then at the chest in Toma's arms.

“I authorized an inspection.”

“We noticed.”

“Was there resistance?”

Nera laughed once.

The steward's eyes moved to her.

“Your man fired a crossbow on a narrow bank,” she said. “We lost a cart, meal, blankets. One of his men nearly drowned.”

“Nearly?”

Kael felt something in him become very still.

“We pulled him out.”

Merrow looked down at the chestnuts.

A child behind him held out an expectant hand. He gave her the bowl, forgetting to count them.

“Bring him through,” he said.

The gatekeeper protested about the queue. Merrow turned with a sharpness Kael had not yet heard from him.

“This one can't stand in it.”

A woman waiting for grain said, “Neither can my mother.”

Merrow stopped.

For a moment Kael thought he would pretend not to have heard. Then the steward pointed to a bench inside the gate.

“Bring her there.”

He led the litter through without looking back.

***

The old washroom had a stove, two narrow beds and a smell of soap that had got into the stone. Mira made the steward find dry linen and sent Kael for water. By the time he returned, Rook's shirt had been cut open.

The bruising had darkened along his side.

Rook saw Kael looking and tried a smile.

“You should see the cart.”

Mira put two fingers lightly against his ribs. The smile vanished.

“Still.”

“I am still. I've reached a professional standard.”

“You can talk when breathing stops hurting.”

“That could damage my trade.”

Merrow stood in the doorway with the clean sheets folded too precisely over his arm. Nera took them from him.

“Out.”

“I need the particulars.”

“You'll get them. Out.”

In the corridor, Kael shut the door.

Merrow drew a paper from his coat. It was the counterpart of the warrant at the culvert, folded into a thin packet. Kael recognized Rook's neat marks beneath the added instruction.

“No homes,” Merrow said.

“He told us.”

“The intention was to regularize the deliveries. Establish what was being carried.”

“You listed statements and copies.”

“Materials removed from estate property are subject to review.”

“Dead people's names aren't a sack of your lord's flour.”

Merrow looked towards the washroom door.

“I know what the order says.”

“Then stop reading it to me.”

The steward flinched.

Kael took the paper before he could put it away. The signature matched. Above it was an authorization to hold unapproved grain and records. The space for expected persons was empty.

“Did you expect seizures?”

Merrow rubbed his thumb over the edge of a key.

“Yes.”

“Did you tell Rook?”

“I said there might be a delay.”

“Did you tell your men to wait until we'd crossed?”

“No.”

Kael folded the warrant.

“I'm keeping this.”

“It's my office copy.”

“Make another.”

Merrow did not reach for it.

***

Vale arrived carrying a stool upside down over her head.

“Move,” she said.

Kael moved. She brought it through the doorway, turned it upright and put it in the only dry patch beneath the window. There was blue ink on her cheek and a torn seam at one cuff.

“Merrow says you have something wet.”

“Several things.”

“I was hoping for a wall.”

She knelt beside the chest. Toma set it down carefully. The wood had swollen around the lid.

Vale looked up at him.

“Did anybody stand on it?”

“No.”

“Good. That makes you more useful than the last man who brought me a document.”

Nera opened it with a knife and patience. Inside, damp had reached the outer wrapping, but the inner packets remained dry. Vale did not touch them until Nera named what each contained: extracts from the Deep Wood register, mill totals and the protected household material, Senn's signed statement.

At the captain's name, Vale's hand paused above the cloth.

“Where is he?”

“Under guard,” Kael said. “Alive.”

“Did he sign before or after you tied him?”

“After. He was told refusal wouldn't change his custody.”

She looked at him, assessing whether he understood what she had asked.

“He made corrections,” Kael added. “They're on the original.”

“I'll need to see those.”

“You can.”

“Not every private household sheet.”

Nera said, “No.”

Vale nodded, without appearing disappointed.

“You'll want Lord Edric.”

“We'll want the command ledger.”

That changed the room.

Outside, somebody was arguing about a number on a grain ticket. Vale closed the chest lid halfway, leaving her hand on it.

“Who told you there was one?”

“Enough people have found the spaces it ought to fill.”

“That's a clever answer.”

“It's the one I have.”

She looked at Merrow.

He had positioned himself beside the door, where retreat might be mistaken for an errand.

“The old military accounts are restricted,” he said.

“They weren't born that way,” Vale said.

“They're restricted now.”

“And some of them are taking water.”

Merrow's expression tightened.

Vale rose and showed Kael her torn cuff.

“The north drain is blocked. We moved half the shelves yesterday. I have two porters, one of whom thinks leather can be dried in a flame. If your people help, I'll have somewhere to lay out what you've brought.”

“And the ledger?”

“Help me keep the ceiling off the records first.”

She did not offer him a bargain. She had already picked up her stool.

Kael followed.

The store court stank of wet grain. Under the arcade, dark patches spread along the wall. Vale showed him where water came through a joint in the stone. A narrow drain ran behind a cupboard heavy enough to require six hands and an argument.

Toma set down his bundle.

“Is that ash wood?”

“Is that important?”

“If we split the back, it won't carry its shelves again.”

Vale looked at the cupboard with sudden concern.

“All right. Don't split it.”

Kael and Toma emptied the shelves. Nera found a wedge. Merrow held the lamp badly until Vale took it from him and gave him the broom.

Behind the cupboard, the drain mouth was packed with plaster and a rag somebody had lost and then chosen not to recover. Kael lay flat in the dirty water and pulled it out. Something pale scuttled away from his fingers.

“Inkblind,” Vale said. “Leave it. They like the paste.”

“Does it bite?”

“Only if you taste of binding glue.”

“I haven't checked.”

A short laugh escaped her.

They cleared the channel. Water began to move, carrying plaster dust into the yard. By the time they set the cupboard back, Kael's boots squelched and Toma had a splinter deep in his palm.

The farmer waited while Vale found tweezers.

“I've got a petition,” he said.

“So has half the queue.”

“Mine's about people.”

“That hasn't made it unusual.”

Toma took the reply without offense.

“Seasonal workers. They hired on for the gardens. When the stores shut, they couldn't pay back the advances. Their tools are held too. I've worked those beds. I know what they're owed.”

Vale stopped searching.

“You were detained?”

“No. I went home when my work ended. They didn't.”

He gave her the folded petition. His handwriting was slow and painstaking. There were three marks below it from people who could not write their names.

“Pell helped with the margins,” he said, embarrassed. “She said I kept wandering.”

Vale examined the straight ruled lines.

“She was right.”

Merrow took a breath.

“I can have the advances reviewed.”

“Do it,” Vale said.

He looked at her.

“Now,” she added, and put the tweezers into Kael's hand.

***

Merrow found him later beside the kitchen steps.

By then the light had gone flat and grey. Mira had settled Rook. Elna was arguing with a cook over whether onions used to flavor stock counted as having been fed to the people who needed them. Nera was buying back two blankets from a servant who had bought them from a patrolman that afternoon.

Kael was trying to clean drain water out of his boot.

“There's something you should see,” Merrow said.

“Another form?”

“A book.”

They went through a service passage to the disused north kitchen. Dust lay on a great cold hearth. A chopping bench stood against the far wall, its surface hollowed by generations of knives. Beneath it was an iron strongbox bolted to the floor.

Merrow locked the door behind them.

He had two keys for the box. One was his own, small and flat. The other bore a white wax thread through its bow.

“Household seal key,” he said. “For the weekly count. It goes back tonight.”

“Why two?”

“So one clerk can't empty the cash alone.”

“Is there cash?”

“Some.”

He lifted the lid.

A narrow ledger lay beneath a tray of worn coins. The cover had no title. Inside, the pages were divided by household, date, requested amount and decision.

The last column was headed Refusal.

Kael leaned nearer.

“No undertaking,” he read.

“Declined renewal.”

“Would not sign on behalf of absent spouse.”

Below each were quantities of grain.

Zero. Zero. Half measure withheld.

“Who keeps this?”

“I do.”

“The public book doesn't have the reasons.”

“No.”

“Why?”

Merrow looked offended by the difficulty of answering.

“Because somebody would ask me one day.”

Kael turned a page. He saw the same household twice, then a third time. A child had become another mouth in the interval between entries. The allotment had not increased.

“Or because you couldn't bear to think you hadn't written it down.”

The steward closed his eyes briefly.

“That too.”

“Give it to me.”

“No.”

Kael looked up.

Merrow had both hands on the lid.

“The kitchen still has to open tomorrow. The stores. The passes. If he finds this gone, he'll put someone else at the desk.”

“Someone worse?”

“Possibly. Or someone who doesn't keep a second book.”

It was a poor answer. It was also the answer the man had spent years arranging his life around.

Kael took his hands off the ledger.

“You'll have to choose more than a column.”

“I know.”

“You keep saying that.”

Merrow closed the box and turned both locks. The click was small in the empty kitchen.

At the door he stopped.

“Vale will be in the lower archive before dawn. The drain work needs signing off.”

“Does it?”

“It does now.”

Kael watched him tuck the household seal key inside his coat.

“What happens if we're found?”

Merrow opened the door.

“Don't bring the chest,” he said. “Leave the people who own those papers out of this part.”

When Kael returned to the washroom, Rook was awake. He lay on his uninjured side, watching the door as though it might have better news if he kept enough attention on it.

“Did he mean it?” he asked.

“Which part?”

Rook shut his eyes.

Kael put a cup within reach of his hand and sat down on the floor to take off his other boot.


# Chapter 23


## Blood Under Stone


Vale had brought three pens.

“One will split,” she said. “One will disappear. The third is for the work.”

Kael held the lamp while she fitted a key into the archive door. He had been awake most of the night listening to Rook fail to find a comfortable position. The castle before dawn was full of useful noises: buckets, a broom, someone coughing in a stairwell. Behind each noise a person had got out of bed to keep Edric's household moving.

Vale listened too.

“The baker's boy,” she said as footsteps crossed above them. “He's late.”

“You know them all?”

“I know when they walk over my ceiling.”

The key turned.

Inside, the lower archive smelled of wet plaster and leather. Shelves stood on stone feet. The oldest books occupied the far wall, behind a grille whose lock was newer than the iron around it. Water ticked into a bucket near the drain they had cleared.

Kael put down the lamp.

Vale took a folded strip from her sleeve. Merrow had written four words on it: *Temporary removal for drying.*

“No number,” she said.

“Does that matter?”

“It will when somebody counts.”

She unlocked the grille with a second key, one she had not shown him the previous afternoon.

“Also Merrow's?”

“Mine. He knows I have it. He generally prefers not to remember.”

The military books were arranged by year. Most had been rebound. The volume they wanted had a brown back and a strip of pale leather where a title had been scraped off.

Vale touched the strip.

“That happened before I kept this room.”

“How long before?”

“I can't tell from leather.”

Kael accepted the correction.

They carried the book to the table. It was heavier than it looked. Dust lay thick at the edge of the pages, except near the middle, where somebody had opened it often enough to darken the paper with handling.

Vale found the index.

There were headings for fodder, transport, billeting, wounded. A later hand had added *Sanctuary movements* in the margin. The page number had been crossed out so hard the pen had torn the sheet.

She turned to it anyway.

The promise was there.

A formal guarantee of shelter under the Vargan seal, addressed to the officials at Greyfen and the shrine. Families and persons displaced by the fighting were to be admitted, counted and protected until safe onward passage could be arranged.

Kael had heard the promise from people who distrusted their own memories of it. Here it lay in a clerk's steady hand, with a date, a tally of blankets and an instruction about the distribution of salt.

Two pages later, the road closed.

*The northern approach is to be held against return. No persons admitted to the civil enclosure are to regain the outer road before certification of clearance.*

Beneath it, squeezed between lines: *Archers authorized against attempts to break the closure.*

Senn's signature appeared among the acknowledgements.

Kael rested his hand beside it.

Vale read over his shoulder. Her lips moved on the word *persons*.

The return register followed. Column after column of households. Some names had been abbreviated; others reduced to numbers. In the final column, a repeating phrase.

*Returned under escort.*

Kael turned back to the order preventing their return.

Then forward.

Vale pulled the lamp closer.

“They don't give a destination,” she said.

“No.”

“Or an escort strength.”

“No.”

She traced the edge of a line without touching the ink.

“There should be another movement book.”

“Was there?”

“We'll find out if it survives.”

Kael thought of the Deep Wood fragments, the mill's ash totals, Elna holding Harl's letter. All those little pieces had made him imagine a missing page that would put the matter in order. The page existed. It made the disorder deliberate.

Vale laid clean paper on the table.

“Read me the headings.”

He did.

She copied without speaking. When a pen split, she put it aside, gave him a look and took up the second.

The sound of the nib became the room's measure of time.

Kael paced while the ink dried. In a side cupboard he found coils of binding wire, sealed bundles of current warrants and a tray of broken tags. He lifted a coil. The flattened sections matched the wire they had taken from Wychwood.

“Here,” he said.

Vale did not stop writing.

“Current stores?”

“Yes.”

“Then it tells us where the wire comes from.”

“It doesn't tell us when it reached the cart.”

“No.”

He set it down. Anger wanted to make every object agree with him. He had begun to recognize the sensation and disliked how familiar it was.

Behind the warrant tray lay a narrow packet, tied in red office thread. The date on the cover was six years earlier. Edric's name appeared below it in a decisive hand.

Kael broke no seal; the packet had already been opened. Inside was an instruction to withdraw the sanctuary movement ledger from ordinary consultation. Copies were to be retained only by the household office. Public inquiries were to be answered from the certified summary.

The summary said the displaced families had gone on under escort.

He brought it to Vale.

She read it once, then again more slowly.

“This is his.”

“The old order?”

“No. This hand. This direction to withhold it.”

Six years. Edric had been a grown man with stores, clerks and choices.

Vale set aside the finished copy of the old command and began another sheet.

Above them, a door slammed.

Both of them stopped.

The footsteps that followed were heavier than the baker's boy's.

***

Merrow's voice came through the ceiling.

“The room is being dried.”

A second voice answered. Kael could not make out the words, only the refusal in their cadence.

Vale blotted the second sheet.

“Go to the back stair.”

“What about the book?”

“It comes.”

She wrapped it in the cloth that had held their paper. Kael took it beneath one arm while she gathered the copies. One pen rolled off the table and disappeared between the stone feet of a shelf.

Vale looked at the gap.

“Called it.”

The grille door stood open. She left it that way. They slipped behind the last shelves as a key scraped at the outer door.

The back stair descended.

Kael had expected an exit into the yard. Instead he found a low passage, damp enough to shine. Vale followed him down, carrying the lamp close to her knees. A watch lantern hung beside the stair. Kael lit its wick from her lamp and took it down.

“Where?”

“Old sanctuary stores. Then the undercroft steps.”

“You've used it?”

“For empty shelving.”

“Recently?”

She did not answer.

Behind them the archive door opened. Someone shouted Merrow's name.

Kael quickened his pace.

The passage turned sharply around a buttress. At the turn stood an armored figure with its sword point resting on the floor.

He stopped so fast Vale struck his back.

The knight raised its head.

A strip of rotted cloth hung beneath its helm. One hand had no flesh on the knuckles; the other wore a gauntlet polished by movement. It stepped forward, and the sword point cut a line through the wet dust.

Kael set the watch lantern beside the buttress and passed the wrapped ledger to Vale.

“Back.”

She retreated to the buttress.

The knight came only as far as the line its own blade had drawn. It held there, shield angled across the narrow passage.

Kael could not get around it. Behind them, human voices entered the stair.

He drew steel.

The knight struck from stillness.

Kael caught the blow high, too high, and the impact drove him against the wall. Stone tore his coat at the shoulder. He recovered before the second stroke and saw the shape of the fight: a committed advance, the sword returning slowly, the shield always following a fraction late.

“Stay where you are,” he told Vale.

“I had no other plan.”

He gave the knight room.

It stepped over its line.

Kael retreated once more, drawing it into the bend where its shield caught the buttress. The creature pulled against the stone, exposing the rusted joint beneath its sword arm.

He struck there.

Metal split. The arm sagged but the blade remained upright, held by something that did not need sound flesh.

The knight pushed into him.

His heel slid in the water. The shield crushed him against the wall. For a moment he had no breath and no reach for a useful cut. Through the helm opening he saw a jaw moving, grinding on a word that had lost its voice.

Vale struck the lamp against the wall.

Flame ran down the spilled oil between the knight's feet.

It turned towards the sudden light, shield shifting just enough.

Kael got his blade under the broken arm and drove it through the gap beneath the breastplate. He felt resistance, then the loose collapse of things packed inside. The sword arm dropped. The shield scraped his face as the body folded.

The fire climbed the rotted cloth.

Vale threw her coat over it and stamped until the flames died.

Kael stood bent over his knees, trying to breathe.

“Was that—”

“No,” she said quickly, then stopped. “I don't know who that was.”

He looked at her.

Her face had gone pale in the remaining lamplight.

“We don't put a name on it because we need one,” she said.

A guard called from above.

Vale took up the ledger. Her burned coat was wrapped around the copies. Kael retrieved the watch lantern.

“This way.”

They stepped over the body.

Beyond the turn, the passage widened into a chamber with old niches along the walls. In one stood a broken lantern. A fragment of red paint marked a doorway at the far end. Kael felt a pressure there, like the moment before a remembered blow.

A man stood beyond the doorway.

Only for a breath. Bareheaded, head bowed, one hand against the stone. Then the lamplight moved and the space was empty.

Vale caught Kael's sleeve.

“Later.”

He wanted to argue. The voices on the stair decided it for him.

They climbed a narrow service stair and emerged behind the laundry. Steam filled the air. A woman lifting sheets from a tub looked at their wet boots, the burned coat and the wrapped book.

“Absolutely not,” she said.

Vale pushed past her.

“Charge the archive.”

“Again?”

***

They found Nera in the washroom, cutting bread into pieces small enough for Rook to eat without sitting up.

Kael put the ledger on the table.

Mira saw the scrape on his cheek.

“I sent you for records.”

“They had a guard.”

“Of course they did.”

Vale spread the copies.

Her hands were shaking now that the work had stopped. She pressed them flat against the table until the tremor eased.

Nera read the first page without sitting down.

Senn's statement lay beside it. The acknowledged order and the account of the man who had obeyed it matched in the places that mattered. The false returns matched the gaps they had followed from the woods.

Rook asked for the suppression instruction.

Nera hesitated before handing it to him.

He read, breathing shallowly.

“Six years,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He had six years to find another arrangement.”

Vale looked at the paper.

“He chose one.”

They made a second copy before breakfast.

Toma carried one set out beneath his petition, accompanied by Senn's female guard, to a dry store room Nera had hired beyond the outer gate. Kael kept another set. Vale marked the originals they had consulted, page by page, so a later reader could see exactly where the copies came from.

The ledger itself remained on the table.

When Merrow came, his collar was crooked and one cheek was red.

“Lord Edric will see you.”

Vale folded her burned coat over her arm.

“He can see me too.”

Merrow looked at the ledger.

“I think,” he said, “he was expecting that.”


# Chapter 24


## The Price of Bread


Edric was on his knees beside an old woman when they entered.

A basin stood beneath her chair. She had been sick on the polished floor and was apologizing with the furious persistence of someone whose body had betrayed her in public. Edric held her shawl away from the mess while a servant brought water.

“You haven't damaged anything,” he told her.

“The rug—”

“Was ugly before you arrived.”

The woman laughed, then coughed hard enough to frighten herself. Edric waited until she could breathe. He asked the servant whether she had eaten, listened to the answer and gave instructions for thin broth, a room near the kitchen and somebody to fetch her daughter.

He rose with difficulty. His knees cracked.

Kael had come prepared to dislike the first thing he saw.

Edric washed his hands in a second basin.

“Her allotment was delayed,” he said. “A number copied incorrectly. Merrow, have it corrected before she goes.”

“Yes, my lord.”

“Sit.”

Breakfast had been left on the side table. Bread, eggs, a dish of sliced pear. Kael's stomach tightened at the smell.

Vale remained standing until Edric gestured to the chair nearest the light.

“You'll want to see what you're reading.”

“I already have.”

“I gathered.”

Kael set the command ledger between them.

Edric looked at the brown cover. His face did not change much. The fingers resting on his cup tightened, then loosened.

“Where are the copies?”

“Outside your archive.”

“How many?”

“Enough to make that an unhelpful question.”

Edric regarded him.

“Eat something. You'll make better threats.”

Kael took bread because refusing it would feed nobody else. Vale accepted a pear and cut it into precise pieces without tasting them.

Edric opened the ledger at the old order. He knew the place.

That knowledge did more to Kael than a confession would have.

“You found it six years ago,” Kael said.

“Yes.”

“And withdrew it.”

“Yes.”

Vale placed the separate suppression direction beside it.

Edric read his own signature.

“I remember signing.”

“Why?”

“Because the harvest had failed north of the ridge. Two bridges were down. The stores were short, the mill was producing half its normal measure, and every household wanted to know whether its neighbor had been given more. Then this came out of a cupboard.”

“A cupboard,” Vale said.

“A room beneath the south stair. The roof had leaked.”

“People had been looking for those records.”

“Yes.”

“And you decided the rain was the problem.”

Edric looked at her.

“No. I decided that opening a thirty-year quarrel while people were stealing seed from each other's fields would make the next winter worse.”

“It was twenty-six years then,” Kael said.

The lord glanced down at the page.

“Twenty-six.”

Kael had corrected a number because he wanted to strike him.

Edric closed the book.

“I didn't write the command.”

“No.”

“I was eleven when that road closed.”

“No one's asking you to have been older.”

“What are you asking?”

“To stop using their silence.”

For a moment Edric seemed tired rather than angry. He moved the bread dish between them.

“When I was eleven,” he said, “a woman in the lower town traded a silver comb for a sack of bran. I remember because my mother had one like it. I thought the merchant was a fool. Silver for something you fed to pigs.”

Kael said nothing.

“By spring she had sold the other comb too.”

“Did she get enough to eat?”

“I don't know.”

“Then it isn't a useful ending to the story.”

Edric almost smiled.

“No,” he said. “It isn't.”

He rang a small bell. A clerk came to the door with a stack of household forms.

“Bring the current return,” Edric said.

The clerk put the stack down and left.

Edric spread three sheets across the table. Grain received, grain held, grain allocated. His finger moved down the columns.

“This is what's here. This is what we expect. This is what will be spoiled if the west wall stays wet. You cleared the drain yesterday. Thank you.”

Kael looked at the figures. He knew enough about carts and sacks to see that the shortage was real.

“There are people taking from us,” Edric continued. “People concealing stores, inventing dependents, selling portions twice. I need a reliable household count.”

Vale took one of the forms.

“This asks whether they'll uphold the renewal.”

“It establishes participation.”

“In receiving flour?”

“In keeping the settlement that protects the roads.”

She turned the sheet so the line lay before Kael.

*The undersigned household assents on behalf of its present and absent members.*

“Absent,” Kael read.

“Most households have someone away.”

“Then ask the person who's away when they come back.”

“And issue grain in the meantime to an unknown number?”

“You just said you have a count.”

Edric's finger stopped moving.

Outside the room, the old woman had begun apologizing to a second servant. A door closed gently, cutting off her voice.

Kael laid the form beside the suppression direction.

“These are different things,” he said. “You keep putting them on the same table.”

Edric leaned back.

“The grain doesn't appear because I separate the papers.”

“No. But you're choosing who can have it.”

“I am responsible for it.”

“That wasn't the question.”

***

They went to the stores because Edric wanted Kael to see the work.

Perhaps he also wanted the servants to see him showing it. Kael could not tell. The lord walked through the counting room without slowing, recognized a porter by his limp and asked whether the new hoist had eased his back. He ordered a rotten sack opened and the sound grain separated rather than the whole lot discarded. He noticed a measure had been shaved short at the rim.

The stores worked because people knew he might notice.

That made what he chose not to notice harder to bear.

At a receiving hatch, a woman held a folded ticket. The clerk shook his head. She unfolded it, pointed, folded it again.

Edric stopped.

“What's wrong?”

“No renewal mark,” the clerk said.

“My husband is at the quarry,” the woman said. “They told me to put his assent.”

“You may sign for the household,” Edric told her.

“He said he wasn't signing anything from here.”

The clerk looked down.

The woman saw Kael watching and mistook him for someone who might help because he carried a sword.

“I have three children.”

Edric took the ticket.

For one moment Kael thought he would waive it. The old woman upstairs would have broth, a room and her daughter. This woman stood close enough for him to see the raw place on her thumb.

“Take the provisional half measure,” Edric said. “Bring him when he returns.”

“Half won't—”

“It's what I can issue without a settled household return.”

The clerk reached for the smaller measure.

Kael took coins from his belt.

“I'll pay the difference.”

Edric turned to him.

“And the next household?”

“This one first.”

The woman looked from one man to the other, hating them both a little. Kael recognized the look and handed the coins to the clerk rather than making her ask.

Edric allowed the purchase.

They walked on.

“You cannot buy all of it,” he said.

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Kael stopped beside a stack of empty sacks.

“Yes. I have spent most of what I came here to earn. Rook can't carry a cup without help. Tor's wrist is broken. Nera lost a cart because your steward sent men to search a crossing. I know exactly how little I can buy.”

Edric's gaze moved to Merrow.

The steward said, “The instruction was mine.”

“Were you asked to stop a loaded cart there?”

“No.”

“Then why there?”

Merrow's eyes lowered.

“The route copy gave the time and place.”

Edric's anger was immediate, and Kael could see why a clerk might find obedience easier than explaining himself.

“A route is not an instruction to choose the weakest bank.”

“No, my lord.”

“And did the patrol recover the grain?”

“Some.”

“The records?”

Kael answered.

“Some copies. Not the originals they wanted.”

Edric turned back to him. The anger did not disappear. It changed its object.

“You see the problem.”

“I see several.”

***

The settlement Edric offered would have fed Lantern Reach for a month.

He made the offer in a small room overlooking the store court, away from the breakfast dishes and the sick woman. There was no ceremony to it. Grain, blankets, timber rights for the ferry, safe conduct for relief carts. Senn would be held for inquiry. The old command would be acknowledged as an abuse under dead officers. Anwen would make a statement about the failed containment. The mill would continue under inspection.

In return, the household renewal would remain in force.

“Your people would be exempt from the first collection,” Edric said.

“My people.”

“The people at your kitchen.”

“Nessa's kitchen.”

“Very well.”

Kael looked through the window. Toma stood below with his petition in both hands, waiting for someone to tell him which door to use. He had pulled a splinter from his own palm badly, and kept rubbing the sore place against his coat.

“And the workers he came for?”

“Seasonal advances. I looked at it this morning. Two charges were duplicated. One tool bond should have expired.”

“Will you release them?”

“Yes.”

“Whether I agree to this?”

Edric's expression hardened.

“Yes.”

Kael nodded.

“Good.”

The word irritated the lord more than an insult would have.

Vale had been silent through the offer. Now she set her untouched pear on the windowsill.

“You'd publish the command,” she said, “but keep the instruction suppressing it out of the account.”

“The present administration must remain capable of administering.”

“So that part stays missing.”

“Would you prefer nobody issuing grain?”

“I'd prefer an accurate account of the person issuing it.”

Edric looked at Kael.

“You've found an excellent clerk.”

Vale's chair scraped the floor.

Kael held her gaze for a moment, then turned back.

“She came herself.”

The lord exhaled through his nose.

“You have a month before the kitchen needs another answer. Winter doesn't wait for your hearing.”

“Then let the stores feed people while we hear it.”

“Without a renewal?”

“Without that condition.”

“No.”

The plainness of it settled the matter.

Edric had seen the old woman, the short measure, the real shortage and the woman at the hatch. He had all the information necessary to understand what he was choosing.

Kael gathered the papers.

“We'll put the orders before the village. The old command and your direction to hide it. Private households stay out.”

“You lack the whole history.”

“Yes.”

“You could break something you can't rebuild.”

“Yes.”

The lord looked towards the court.

For a while neither spoke.

Then Edric said, “I won't have that book carried into a tavern and waved over people's heads.”

“Neither will I.”

“Leave the original under seal.”

Vale answered before Kael could.

“Two witnesses to the seal, a written receipt and a list of the copied pages. No alteration before a public comparison.”

Edric looked at her.

“You make it sound like I cannot be trusted.”

She picked up the pear.

“I can write that less awkwardly if you like.”

***

They sealed the ledger in the presence of two clerks.

Vale made each clerk read the page list aloud. Edric signed the receipt. Kael kept one copy, Vale another. The separate six-year suppression instruction remained with Vale, with a witnessed note recording where it had been found. Edric could dispute what his decisions meant. He could no longer quietly turn them into a missing cupboard.

Merrow was told to surrender the office ring.

He put it on the table one key at a time. The sound was tedious. Edric watched without impatience.

“You'll accompany them as far as the empty house at Hearthward,” he said. “See whether it can be made habitable. You've spent enough of my stores on promises. Find out what one costs.”

Merrow's face went grey.

“Am I relieved of office?”

“You are away from your desk.”

The steward bowed, though there was nobody behind him to see the habit.

Outside, Toma met them with his petition folded beneath a release.

“They're letting them go,” he said, surprised enough to be almost cheerful. “Tools too. One man wants to come with us. The other two want home.”

Kael could not make himself resent the good news.

“Let them choose.”

Toma looked at him as if that were an unnecessarily elaborate response to something obvious, then went to find Nera.

At the washroom, Mira was feeding Rook broth one irritating spoonful at a time.

“He can hold it,” Kael said.

“He pours it on himself.”

“Only twice,” Rook said.

Kael sat beside the stove. The bread in his stomach felt heavy. He had refused a month of food, and the refusal had not filled a single sack.

Nera came in carrying the blankets she'd recovered.

“How bad?”

He told her.

She spread one blanket over Rook and kept the other for the road.

“Then we'll need to count what we have.”

No one offered to call the refusal brave.

Kael got up and helped her count.


# Chapter 25


## The Last Witness


Senn asked whether the man beneath the archive still wore his own face.

Kael had come to ask about the old sanctuary passage. He had not expected that question.

“I saw him for a moment.”

“Bareheaded?”

“Yes.”

The captain sat on the edge of a narrow bed. His guards had divided the room with a rope, more as a reminder than a useful barrier. The cart driver was mending a glove beside the door. The older ferryman slept with his stiff leg stretched towards the stove.

“Halvern took his helmet off,” Senn said. “When he came to us. He wanted us to see him.”

Vale stood beside Kael with a floor plan tucked under her arm.

“Could there have been another officer there?”

“Yes.”

“Could you identify him from what Kael saw?”

“No.”

She nodded. Senn watched her make the distinction in her face.

“There's a recess off the sanctuary stores,” he said. “The escorts waited there for the gate clerks. A lantern bracket beside the door. He put his helmet beneath it.”

“Why?”

“To carry a child through. She kept hitting the metal when he lifted her.”

Kael could picture the sound.

Senn rubbed his crooked little finger.

“He asked us where the people were meant to go.”

“What did you say?”

“That the order was to hold.”

“Did he ask again?”

“Yes.”

The cart driver drew thread through leather. The little scrape went on after Senn stopped speaking.

Vale unfolded the plan.

“Show me the place.”

***

They went below after breakfast, when the household was busy enough to ignore another work party.

Merrow had arranged the inspection as part of their departure. He did not accompany them. Kael suspected he could not bear another locked room.

Vale carried the copied sanctuary promise, the copied closure order and Senn's signed account. Each had its own wrapper. Kael had cleaned his sword but left it sheathed beneath his coat. He brought a strip of felt, a small hammer, two wedges and an extra lamp. He had learned from the previous visit.

The burned knight remained in the passage.

Someone had pushed the body against the wall and spread a rough cloth over it. Kael checked the hands before stepping past. Nothing moved.

Vale paused.

“We should have found who it was.”

“We still might.”

“Perhaps.”

They continued.

Below the archive the air was warmer, with the damp mineral smell of water that had traveled a long way without seeing daylight. In a channel beside the stair, something pale contracted when Kael's boot struck the stone. A Knell Leech, thick as his wrist, lay against the wall. It moved at the next vibration, lifting its ridged mouth from the wet surface.

Vale recoiled.

“I thought those were smaller.”

“They are when they're young.”

“That's an appalling defense of anything.”

He put the felt beneath the lamp so its base would not ring on the floor. They went around the channel slowly. The leech settled as their steps softened.

At the sanctuary chamber, Kael wedged the outer door open.

He wanted a way back that did not depend on a swollen latch.

The lantern bracket Senn had described was there. Beneath it, half hidden by dust, lay a dented helm. A child had scratched a wavering star into its rim.

Vale crouched.

“Don't move it yet.”

She set down the papers and sketched where it lay.

Kael waited, listening to water drip.

On the wall above the recess somebody had cut words into the stone. Most were too worn to read. One remained: *admitted*.

The air changed.

Kael looked up.

The man stood in the doorway.

He was perhaps forty, perhaps older; the broken light made age difficult. His coat hung crookedly across one shoulder. A sword rested in his hand, its point lowered. There was a dark mark at the side of his neck.

Vale rose without a sound.

The man's eyes passed over her and stopped on Kael.

“Is the north road open?”

Kael's mouth had gone dry.

“Not then.”

The sword lifted.

He had given the wrong answer, or an answer to the wrong moment.

The figure stepped forward.

Kael drew in time to catch the first blow. It felt real enough. The impact stung the healing cut on his forearm. He turned the blade aside and gave ground, careful not to step into the channel.

The figure came after him with a soldier's blunt economy.

“Where do you expect them to go?”

Kael blocked again.

The question filled the little chamber.

“Wait,” Vale said.

The figure did not turn.

She unfolded the sanctuary promise.

Kael caught the next stroke near the hilt. His shoulder struck the lantern bracket. The old iron bent under his weight.

Vale began to read.

The formal language sounded absurd in the chamber: households, provisions, safe conduct. The sword halted with its edge close enough to Kael's throat for him to see rust along it.

“Read the end,” the figure said.

Vale did.

*Until safe onward passage can be arranged.*

The man lowered the sword.

Kael stayed very still. Then he lowered his own.

“I brought them here,” the man said.

“What should we call you?” Vale asked.

He looked at the papers.

“Halvern. The escort came through at dusk.”

***

The chamber remained around Kael. Water still dripped into the channel. Vale stood beside the lamp with paper in her hands.

Yet he could smell smoke.

It came with wet wool, crowded breath and something cooking too long in a pot. Voices pressed against the walls. A little girl was furious about a lost shoe. A woman insisted she had been given a pass. Men argued near a gate Kael could not see.

Halvern stood among those sounds as if he had never left them.

“The road was watched,” he said. “We brought the wagons by the lower turn. One wheel came off. We carried what they could lift.”

Kael saw only what seemed caught around the man: a hand on a cart rail, the helmet set beneath the lantern, a child's foot swinging above mud. The fragments came without a view of the whole place. There was no height from which all the blame could be arranged.

“They said the stores couldn't bear another night.”

“Who?” Vale asked.

Halvern turned towards a voice neither of them could hear.

“Open the northern way.”

The answer was lost in the room's low murmur.

“I have their passes.”

A pause.

“Then send someone who can change it.”

His sword hand tightened.

Kael felt the old impulse to step back, to let the scene run beyond him because it had happened already and could not be helped. He made himself stay.

Halvern looked at him.

“Would you have sent them back?”

Kael almost answered the question he wanted to be asked.

Instead he said, “I don't know what I would have done here.”

The figure studied him.

Vale laid the closure order on the stone beside the promise.

“This is the command they acknowledged.”

Halvern looked down.

The dark mark at his neck spread. Kael heard a scrape of boots, a man's sharp instruction, then a silence so sudden that the dripping water sounded loud.

Halvern had fallen to one knee.

“I would not give the track.”

He said it without triumph. His face held an anger that had run out of places to go.

“They took my sword.”

“What happened after?” Vale asked softly.

The figure touched his neck.

“I don't have after.”

Kael looked away. On the floor beside the helm was a broken buckle. A small object for all that surrounded it.

When he looked back, Halvern was standing.

“Did they get through?”

Vale's eyes closed for a moment.

Kael answered.

“No.”

The chamber seemed to contract.

“Some may have escaped other ways,” he added, because the qualification mattered and because he was ashamed of how much he wanted it to help. “We don't know all of them.”

Halvern looked towards the doorway.

“I told her she could put the shoe on outside.”

The voice was quiet enough that Kael nearly missed it.

There was no answer worth giving.

***

They remained until the pressure in the room began to ease.

Vale asked no questions about Edric. The man before them had died twenty-six years before the lord signed his suppression order. Kael watched her fold that newer paper away untouched. She would not ask a dead soldier to know a living ruler's choices.

He envied the restraint.

“I left people,” he said.

Vale looked at him.

Halvern's attention returned slowly, as if from another room.

“A different road,” Kael said. “Eight years ago. I had an order to withdraw.”

The figure waited.

“I keep thinking about where the order ended.”

“Where were they?”

“Behind me.”

Halvern's eyes moved to the open doorway.

“I don't know your road.”

Kael swallowed.

“No.”

He had imagined that refusal might be contagious: that hearing a man who had refused could put something right in the man who had obeyed. Standing in the cold, he felt the childishness of it.

He knelt beside the helm.

“May I move this?”

Halvern looked at the object.

“Under the light,” he said.

Kael lifted it carefully. The metal was cold and unexpectedly light. A scrap of cloth clung inside. He placed it on the stone shelf beneath the bracket. Vale set the lamp beside it, on the felt.

The wavering scratched star caught the light.

Halvern leaned close.

For a moment he looked only tired. The sword in his hand had become a dark line, then an absence. He sat on the edge of the recess where escorts had waited for clerks.

“Leave the door,” he said.

“We will.”

The sounds of the crowded gate thinned. Kael could hear his own breathing again. A drop struck the channel. The Knell Leech moved somewhere beyond the stair.

When the lamp guttered, the recess was empty.

Kael did not call him back.

Vale sat down on the floor and put her papers on her knees. She wrote while the memory remained clear enough to distinguish what had been said from what she wanted it to mean.

Kael stayed beside the doorway.

After a while she asked, “Did you hear the shoe?”

“Yes.”

She wrote that too, then stopped.

“It won't be the same on a page.”

“No.”

She blew gently across the ink.

***

They left Vargan the following morning.

A carrier from Lantern Reach had brought Anwen's answer about the camp. She and two helpers had separated the borrowed iron from the wrapped bodies, kept the identifiable objects and buried what remained. The fragments had faded during the work. She had recovered no whole person from them. The road was clear. Kael folded the brief note and put it with the camp inventory; he would not let the cleared road become the only fact anyone kept.

Rook rode on a pallet in a borrowed cart, furious about every stone in the road and ashamed of being furious when other people were pushing. Nera had written the loan down, including the price of a broken wheel, and refused to let him negotiate it.

“I'm still capable of arithmetic.”

“Good. You can check our flour when we stop.”

He accepted the smaller task with a look Kael pretended not to see.

Senn walked between his guards. Toma returned from the hired room beyond the outer gate with the second packet of copies still sealed beneath his petition. He had found the released seasonal workers and seen two of them away towards their homes. The third took paid work helping at the cart. He was a quiet man with a damaged thumb who preferred to be addressed by his job, and Nera obliged him.

Merrow came last with a travel bag and no office ring at his belt.

Vale carried the protected packets herself. A duplicate set traveled separately with Kael. The old original ledger remained under seal behind them; Edric's instruction to hide it did not.

From the upper road they could see smoke over the Keep kitchens. The grain queues had formed again.

Kael looked until the turn took them out of sight.

That evening, in a hollow sheltered by alder, Nera checked the cart fastenings while the others ate. Kael went to help. She handed him the spare rope without a word.

They worked for several minutes.

“I want to correct the account,” he said.

She kept her eyes on the knot.

“Which account?”

“Mine.”

“You've said the road couldn't be held.”

“Yes.”

“That part was true.”

He pulled the rope through the loop. His hands knew what to do when the rest of him did not.

“I never came back.”

Nera stopped.

Across the hollow, Rook laughed at something Elna had said, then gasped at the pain in his side. Mira told him it served him right.

Nera put the fastening down.

“Tomorrow,” she said.

Kael nodded.

“After breakfast. You can help unload first.”

“All right.”

She picked up the rope again and made him undo the knot he had tied too tightly.


# Chapter 26


## The Word Retreat


Nera chose a place where they could see the road.

She brought two stools from the cart and put them on opposite sides of a flat stone. Vale came with paper, ink and a board to write on. Kael had expected the formality to make speaking easier. It gave him more things to look at instead of Nera.

The morning's unloading was done. Rook lay beneath a blanket, inspecting their flour counts with an intensity that suggested arithmetic had personally wronged him. Toma and the hired worker were repairing a loose cart rail. Mira had gone to find dry alder branches for the fire.

Vale put the ink on the stone.

“I haven't seen the original report.”

“I know.”

“You remember where you filed it?”

“The western field office. End of the wet season. Eight years ago.”

“And the wording?”

“Some of it.”

“Then I'll mark that.”

Nera sat with her hands between her knees.

Kael looked at the road.

“We were taking twenty-seven people through.”

“Twenty-nine,” she said.

He turned.

“One woman had two children under the flour sheet. They weren't on your list.”

“Twenty-nine.”

Vale waited.

The small correction hurt more than he had expected. He had kept the number in his head for years. Even that part had been wrong.

Kael placed both feet flat on the ground.

“The bridge ahead had been taken. We tried the ridge track. The wagons couldn't make the turn together.”

Nera watched him.

He could see the old road now, distinct from the one before them.

***

Eight years earlier, his right glove had a hole beside the thumb.

Kael remembered worrying it with his nail while the captain argued with a scout. It was an absurd thing to remember. There had been smoke above the trees. An arrow stood upright in the road where it had struck a packed rut. The glove had been wet since morning and was rubbing the skin raw.

The scout said there were men above the cut.

The captain sent two soldiers forward.

One came back.

Behind Kael, a wagon wheel caught in a drainage groove. The driver whipped the horse. A woman shouted at him to stop, that he was turning the wheel against the boy's leg.

Kael ran back.

Jem was trapped between the rim and a low stone. Kael did not know his name then. He knew the shape of his boot, the way the leather folded above the ankle. The woman had both hands around the wheel. Nera, younger and with her hair coming loose from a red tie, was pulling at the shaft.

“Lift,” she said.

He put his shoulder under the wagon.

The driver stopped whipping the horse.

Together they moved it enough. The boy came free with a noise that barely sounded human. Blood soaked his stocking.

His mother knelt beside him. She had a narrow face and a missing button at the throat of her coat. She pushed Kael's hand away when he tried to move the leg.

“Get me a straight piece.”

He broke a lath from the wagon side.

The first arrows came while she was binding it.

One struck the driver. Another went through the flour sheet. The horse reared. Someone dragged the wounded driver away from its feet.

“Back,” the captain shouted.

Kael looked at the track.

Two soldiers were falling behind the ridge. The others had begun to pull away from the wagons. The captain pointed towards the lower turn. If they stayed in the cut, they would be shot without room to use their weapons.

It was a lawful order. Kael had never needed to lie about that.

He lifted the boy.

For six steps he carried him towards the lower turn. He could still remember the weight, lighter than he had expected. Then a soldier caught his coat and shouted that they had to clear the choke point. Nera's mother was trying to lift a chest out of their way. A wheel blocked the path.

Kael set the boy beside a white boundary post.

“Wait here,” he said.

Nera stared at him.

“The lower path. I'll get the line down and come back.”

“Take him.”

“I'll come back.”

Her hand closed on his sleeve.

He pulled free.

That was the movement he had spent eight years leaving out.

Not the retreating soldiers. Not the captain shouting. His own arm, turning in the wet cloth until her fingers lost their hold.

He ran.

At the lower turn he looked back once. The white post was hidden by the wagon. He could see the red tie in Nera's hair.

Then the trees took the road.

***

Kael stopped speaking.

Vale's pen remained above the page.

Nera was looking at his sleeve.

“You said you would bring a stretcher,” she said.

“I don't remember that.”

“I do.”

He nodded.

The hollow around them was quiet except for the cart repair. Toma struck the rail once, then stopped, perhaps because he had heard the silence.

“What happened when you reached the post below?” Nera asked.

“We counted the soldiers.”

“Did you count us?”

“No.”

“Did you ask to go back?”

“Yes.”

“And?”

“The captain said the cut was lost. We were to hold the lower road until the next unit came.”

“How long?”

“Until dark.”

Nera's mouth tightened.

Kael had told that part before, if only to himself. It still had the protection of an order around it.

“The next morning,” he said, “they sent scouts by the southern slope. I wasn't one of them. They saw the broken wagons. Smoke. They said there was nobody moving on the track.”

“They didn't come down.”

“No.”

“You could have.”

“Yes.”

Vale put the pen down.

Kael could feel her attention now without looking at her. It was no longer the polite attention of a clerk helping someone arrange a difficult account.

“I thought you'd all been killed.”

“You thought.”

“Yes.”

“Did that make it easier?”

He made himself answer.

“Yes.”

Nera turned her face away.

He waited because she had waited eight years and he could bear the length of a few breaths.

“What did you do instead?”

“Marched west. Took another escort after the paymaster settled us. Bought a new pair of boots.”

She looked back.

The boots had come into his mouth without warning. He had forgotten them until he began to speak. Thick soles, good stitching, the luxury of dry feet. He remembered trying them on while the cobbler's wife complained that he was getting mud on the stool.

“I had time,” he said. “After the first night. After the first week. I could have gone looking.”

Nera's voice had gone flat.

“You didn't.”

“No.”

Vale picked up the pen.

“In the report,” she said, “what did you put?”

“That the position couldn't be held. That we withdrew under orders.”

“Which was true.”

“Yes.”

“What did you omit?”

“That I told you I'd return. That I never did, even after the withdrawal order no longer kept me from trying.”

She wrote slowly enough that he had to remain with each sentence.

***

Nera told him about the first night.

She kept her eyes on the stone while she spoke. Her mother had pulled Jem beneath the tipped wagon after the fighting moved away. A woman with the two hidden children had brought water. They had waited for a stretcher until the boy fell asleep, then carried him between them on a door torn from a roadside hut.

“That was the bridge I told you about,” she said. “Later that night. We tied the sleeves because we couldn't carry him and hold one another. The woman sang to keep us moving together. I still hear it when I carry somebody after dark.”

“My mother blamed the door,” Nera said.

Kael did not understand.

“It was too short. His foot kept falling off. She swore about the carpenter all the way down the bank.”

There was an odd tenderness in the irritation.

“Leth?” he asked.

Nera looked at him sharply.

“You said her name at the mill.”

“Yes.”

“I'm sorry.”

“Don't apologize for remembering her name.”

She rubbed her palms against her knees.

“Jem kept telling her she was saying the same thing. He had a way of making you wish he'd stop being right.”

A little smile came and went.

“He used to leave crumbs in my blanket. Said he was helping me find breakfast. I put a beetle in his boot.”

Kael listened.

“I've told that story more often than this one.”

She did not owe him a reason.

They had found shelter in a charcoal shed. By the second day, Jem's leg had swollen above the splint. Leth went for help along the river path and returned with a torn skirt, shaking so badly she could not pour the water she had brought. Someone had struck her while she was trying to get past a road levy. Nera did not know whose men they were. Vale left a space for their allegiance; Nera asked her to write *unknown*.

Jem died before dawn on the fourth day.

Her mother lived another two days.

Nera said those facts plainly. The plainness made Kael hear the time between them: the hours of keeping a fire, the effort of finding water, the possibility that every small task might be the thing that kept someone alive.

“Who helped you?”

“The woman with the children. Later, people from the charcoal camp.”

“Do you know where they—”

“No.”

He stopped.

Nera folded her hands.

“Don't go looking for them because you want to finish something.”

“I won't.”

“And their names don't go in this.”

Vale touched the edge of the paper.

“Yours?”

Nera considered.

“For the protected account. Mine. What I saw. You can keep my mother's and brother's names with it, if that part stays closed.”

Vale nodded.

“For the public account, he can say what he did.”

Kael looked at her.

“Yes.”

“If I speak, I'll choose it then.”

She stood.

Nera lifted her stool onto the cart. Kael took the other.

***

Mira found him by the stream, washing ink from his hand.

Vale had taken the two accounts to dry separately. Kael had signed his correction twice, then watched her label the remembered wording of the old report as his recollection, not an inspected original. A proper comparison could be made if the old filing was found. The absence of that page did not make his admission uncertain.

Mira crouched on the opposite bank.

“Nera told me the part she wanted me to know.”

Kael watched water move around a stone.

“All right.”

“You could have gone back.”

“Yes.”

She pulled a dead leaf apart between her fingers.

“I knew you left. I thought—”

She stopped.

“What?”

“That you'd tried afterwards.”

“So did most people.”

“Did you let me?”

He looked up.

“Yes.”

Her face changed.

The word had cost him less to say than it cost her to hear. He saw that and wanted to explain everything: the orders, the fear, the years in which the omission had become the shape of his life. Every explanation began to sound like a request for her to do some of the work of forgiving him.

Mira threw the leaf into the stream.

“I don't know what to do with you this morning.”

“You don't have to do anything.”

“Don't give me permission. I'm telling you.”

He nodded.

She looked tired. There was a burn on the back of her hand from a cooking pot, and a strand of hair had stuck to her lip. He wanted to reach across and move it. He kept his hands in the cold water.

“I love you,” she said, with obvious annoyance. “That hasn't made me less angry.”

He had no answer ready for the first part. The second he understood.

“I love you too.”

“I know.”

She stood.

“Rook needs help sitting up. Go and be useful without making it a penance.”

After she had gone, Kael remained at the stream until his fingers hurt. Then he dried them and went back.

Rook was awake, with the flour account on his chest.

“Found a discrepancy,” he said.

Kael eased him upright against the rolled blanket.

“Where?”

“The hired man counted the open sack by weight. Nera counted it by meals.”

“Which is right?”

“Both. That's what makes it annoying.”

Kael took the slate.

They worked through the difference while Rook breathed in small, careful measures. Across the fire, Nera fed Bracken a crust and told him he was getting fat. Vale wrapped the dry accounts in separate cloth, marked the outer packet for protected custody and tied it shut.

When she asked Kael to check the label, he read it all the way to the end.


# Chapter 27


## The House That Was Empty


Hearthward had forty-three windows and no smoke.

Merrow counted the windows while they climbed the last rise. He seemed to find comfort in a number that had not yet been disputed.

“The western range was added later,” he said. “The roof there is better.”

“Then why is it empty?” Nera asked.

“The last keeper died. The next appointment was deferred.”

“How long?”

“Four years.”

Elna looked at the house.

“That's a thorough deferral.”

It stood above an old orchard, broad and low, with a square yard enclosed on three sides. The fourth side opened towards the road. A long building at the back had once held stores; shorter wings contained rooms for travelers and people who worked the surrounding land. Most of the fruit trees had gone wild. Small hard apples lay beneath them, bruised by their own fall.

Kael stopped the cart outside the gate.

Rook had been quiet for the last hour. That meant the road had been bad enough to defeat even his complaints. Mira checked him before looking at the house.

“He needs a bed that stays still.”

“We'll find one,” Kael said.

“Find a floor first.”

The gate hung from one hinge. Merrow tried to lift the latch and brought a piece of wood away in his hand.

Nera put down her pack.

“No one unloads yet.”

She sent Toma around the outside with the hired worker. Kael took the main door. Vale stayed with the documents. Senn sat on a fallen stone between his guards, looking towards the upper windows.

Bracken sniffed the gate fragment in Merrow's hand, sneezed and went to explore the orchard.

The house smelled of damp plaster, cold ash and mice.

Kael stood inside the entrance until his eyes adjusted. Benches lined the walls. A long table occupied the center of the hall, one end propped on a stack of old tiles. The hearth was large enough to roast an ox, but a nest of twigs blocked the chimney above it.

Someone had left a cup upside down on the mantel.

He lifted it. A pale circle remained in the dust.

Four years, Merrow had said. The cup made the absence look shorter.

A door opened at the back.

Kael drew his knife before he saw Toma.

“Rear entrance is open,” the farmer said.

“I noticed.”

“There's a well. Needs checking. The orchard ditch runs away from it, which is something.”

“Something good?”

“For once.”

They tested the floor with a pole. The main beams held. Near the west wall, the boards gave under pressure and revealed a black space beneath.

Mira came through the doorway carrying a rolled blanket.

“No one sleeps there,” Kael told her.

She looked at the hole.

“Did you discover it with your feet?”

“Pole.”

“Good. You're learning expensive things cheaply.”

She put the blanket on the table and went to examine the hearth.

Kael watched her for a moment. Since the stream she had been neither distant nor easy with him. She still touched his arm when she passed. She did not always stay.

He let her work.

***

They found two dry rooms.

One had a cracked ceiling and a door that would not close. The other held a broken loom, three sacks of wool gone hard with damp and a little Hearthscale asleep inside a stone warming box.

Bracken discovered it before they did.

He planted his feet, lifted his plates and made a low, indignant noise.

The wild creature opened one eye.

“Your relatives are rude,” Rook said from the litter.

Bracken ignored him.

Nera pulled the warming box gently away from the loom. The wild Hearthscale rolled itself deeper into the corner, showing them the thick scales along its back. Nobody tried to touch it.

“First tenant,” Elna said. “We can work around it.”

They carried Rook into the other room. He cursed when the litter caught the doorframe, then apologized to the woman carrying his feet, then cursed again when they lowered him.

Mira put a cup of water beside him.

“Anything else?”

“A better body.”

“We'll ask at the next house.”

Kael went back for the documents.

Vale had laid a cloth on the cart and was checking each wrapper against her list. Nera's chest had survived the culvert, the Keep and the ridge road. The wood looked older than it had a week before.

“This one stays with me,” Vale said, lifting the protected correction.

Kael nodded.

“The copied command goes in a different room from your set. Edric's instruction with it.”

“Where?”

“Somewhere that doesn't drip.”

“That leaves two choices.”

“One has Rook.”

“He'll complain at a leak.”

“A useful alarm.”

She carried the packet inside. Kael took the chest. In his own oilskin, the token with Oren's painted crow rested beside the red cord. He felt its small hard edge while shifting the weight against his hip.

He had begun the road carrying objects because somebody had paid him to find them. Now he knew how easily the people who had touched them could be reduced to the objects they left.

He set the chest on the table rather than the floor.

Outside, Nera called for the roof ladder.

***

The ladder had two rotten rungs.

They replaced one with a length cut from the broken gate. For the other, Toma bound a sound stave between the rails, testing it with his whole weight before Kael climbed.

From the eaves he could see the orchard wall, the road and the shallow valley beyond. A raised track ran south towards Lantern Reach. It would take a loaded cart longer than a walker, and the low section near the stream would need marking before dark.

Nera climbed to the ladder's middle.

“Don't come higher,” he said.

“I wasn't.”

“Tile's loose above you.”

She moved her hand.

Kael shifted the tile and found the hole beneath it. Water had been entering along a split lath. The whole stretch would need Tor's eye and several people with sound wrists.

“How much can we do today?” Nera asked.

“Cover this. Clear the chimney. Get them through the night.”

“That'll do.”

He looked down at her.

“Will it?”

“For today.”

She passed him a folded piece of sailcloth from the ferry stores, already patched twice. Losing the cart blankets had narrowed every choice. The cloth could cover the roof or divide a sleeping room; it could not do both.

They chose the roof.

Kael weighted the edges with tiles while Nera held the ladder. Beneath them, Merrow began carrying fallen wood into the hall. He wore his good coat and was ruining it. Elna watched until he tried to carry too much at once, then took half without comment.

The hired worker offered to go back along the southern track with their message.

Nera drew the route on a scrap, leaving the uncertain low ground clearly marked.

“Take the high side. Tell Tor we need his judgment before his hands. Nessa needs to know how many mouths and what the stove can burn. Anwen should bring clean cloth if there's any to spare.”

“And Pell,” Toma said.

The worker looked at him.

“If it's safe,” Toma added. “Tell them where I am.”

Nera put the message in a waxed wrapper.

“Come back in company.”

He took bread for the road. Rook called from the doorway to ask how much.

“Enough,” Nera said.

“That isn't a unit.”

“It is today.”
