The worker left while there was still light to see the lower track.

***

At the well, Toma lowered a bucket and brought up water with a leaf floating on top.

Merrow looked disappointed.

“The cover's broken,” Toma said. “Water may still be good. We'll boil it until we're sure.”

“There used to be a keeper's payment for the cover.”

“Was there a keeper?”

Merrow set down his notebook.

“No.”

Toma tipped the water into the washing pail. He kept the cleaner second draw separate. His movements had a patience Kael associated with fields: there was no arguing a seed into growing faster, no useful place to put a temper when the ground would not take the plough.

“Pell hates boiled water,” he said.

Kael took the bucket.

“Does she?”

“Says it tastes of spoons.”

“What does that mean?”

“I haven't managed to find out.”

He smiled, then rubbed the little cap at his belt.

“She'll want to sleep where she can see the orchard.”

“We haven't got that room dry.”

“I know.”

They carried the water in without promising it.

At the hall table, Vale and Merrow had spread a plan of the house. Elna wanted a room where she could shut a door. Mira wanted the driest space for the medicines and anyone too ill to sleep near the entrance. Nera wanted room to turn a cart under cover. None of those spaces overlapped conveniently.

“We could hold the hearing here,” Kael said.

The argument stopped.

Vale looked at the hole in the floor.

“Where would they stand?”

“In the yard.”

“And the people who need to sit?”

“We'd bring benches.”

“From where?”

He had no answer.

Elna sat on the edge of the table.

“I'd like a bed before I explain my husband to a crowd.”

Mira pointed towards Rook's room.

“And a way to get him to the privy without carrying him past everyone eating.”

Merrow turned the house plan a little.

“There is an old covered passage. If we clear it—”

“Clear it first,” Nera said. “Then draw people in it.”

Kael looked at the documents.

They were ready enough to accuse. The house was not ready to hold the living people whose lives would have to continue afterwards.

“All right,” he said.

Vale gathered the papers and put them away before somebody spilled water over them.

***

Senn asked permission to inspect the rear window.

The cart driver looked at Kael.

“With her,” he said.

They went together. The captain tested the frame, looked into the orchard and came back with a strip of rotten wood in his hand.

“That can be lifted from outside.”

“Can it be secured?”

“A bar across the inner frame. There are sound brackets.”

Nera handed him a saw.

His guard untied one hand and stayed beside him.

He worked badly at first. The blade caught. He had spent years telling other men to build things he then inspected. The cart driver took the saw, showed him how to let its weight cut, and gave it back.

Kael watched until the captain found a rhythm.

No one called him captain.

Merrow stood near the hearth with an armful of wood. He had put his notebook away.

“He was good at the road once,” he said quietly.

“People keep telling me that.”

“It mattered.”

“Yes.”

Kael took half the wood.

“It still doesn't tell us where to put him.”

The steward looked towards the room they had chosen for guarded custody: dry, a single door, enough space for a bed and two chairs.

“No,” he said.

They stacked the fuel beside the hearth.

***

The first fire smoked them out.

They had cleared the nest but missed a stone lodged higher in the chimney. Everyone retreated into the yard, coughing. Rook shouted that if they meant to kill him they could at least be straightforward about it. Elna told him the smoke was free and he should appreciate the bargain.

Kael climbed onto the roof again.

When the stone finally came loose, it struck the hearth with a crash that sent Bracken running into the orchard. The wild Hearthscale did not emerge from its warming box.

The second fire drew.

They ate thin soup standing or perched on boxes. Nera cut the remaining bread so that the slices looked larger than they were. Merrow began to apologize for the state of the house and was given a bowl before he could make it formal.

Mira sat beside Kael on a low step.

For a while they watched Senn's guard put his portion through the open door. The captain thanked her. She stayed to eat her own, the key resting beside her bowl.

“You're leaving room for the stove tomorrow?” Mira asked.

“Two paces.”

“Three. Nessa swings a pot when she's annoyed.”

“I'll mark three.”

She leaned her shoulder against his while the soup cooled.

After supper, Vale checked the roof cloth from inside. Toma barred the rear window. Elna chose the corner she wanted and announced that the wall would be blue when anyone had time for paint.

Nera made the first work list.

Beds. Stove. Dry shelves. Covered passage. Well cover. A place to wash without standing in the road.

Kael added the number of boards they had found.

Before sleeping, he went to the entrance and tried the door. It dragged across the floor, then stuck halfway.

Merrow came with a wedge.

Together they lifted it clear of the swollen board. Kael held the weight while the steward shaved the edge with a borrowed knife. When they tried again, the door closed.

They opened it once more to let Toma bring in the last bucket.


# Part Four The Road Between Crowns



# Chapter 28


## The House of Many Names


The first thing Hearthward needed was a door that shut.

The carts from Lantern Reach arrived shortly after dawn, bringing Nessa, Anwen, Tor and Pell into a house that still offered little beyond shelter from the night. They had barely unloaded before Tor found Kael struggling with the door.

There were three doors on the lower floor. One had swollen against its frame, one opened into a pantry with no roof, and the third stood on a single hinge, lifting whenever the wind entered the passage behind the house. Kael put his shoulder to the swollen one and felt the boards yield.

"Stop," Tor said.

Kael stopped.

"You'll split the stile."

"Wasn't planning to."

"Wood doesn't give a shit what you plan. Lift it."

They raised the door together, which meant Kael lifted and Tor guided with his good hand. The left was bundled against his chest. He had taken to tucking the loose end of the sling into his belt; otherwise it caught on everything and made him furious.

A splinter worked beneath Kael's thumbnail. He held the weight while Tor felt the upper hinge.

"Stone under the corner. That flat one."

When the door finally shut, it made a sound out of proportion to the effort: a modest wooden click.

Pell applauded from the stairs.

"You could have helped," Toma told her.

"I was watching whether it would fall on him."

"Useful," Kael said.

"Don't encourage her."

The house had been built around a long room with a hearth at each end. Someone had added the upper floor later, cutting windows wherever the beams permitted rather than where a person might want to look out. Beyond it lay a yard, a disused washhouse and a strip of ground sloping toward a spring. Through the empty upper windows Kael could see a road marking the hillside. It would be possible to reach the place without passing beneath the Keep's walls, though the longer approach was stony and exposed.

The passage that had brought them from the Undercroft smelled of wet lime. Kael disliked it. He disliked having a refuge attached, however distantly, to a place whose keys Merrow had carried.

Nera had inspected both entrances before taking her boots off.

"We keep the outside road clear," she had said. "No one has to go through the Keep to leave."

That had settled one question. The rest stood in piles around them.

Nessa was turning a flour sack inside out over a table.

"Who decided we had thirty places?"

Vale looked up from a folded sheet. "I counted the floor."

"The floor doesn't eat."

"I counted spaces for beds."

"And where will they put their feet?"

Vale looked at the sheet again. Kael saw her add a small mark with rather more pressure than necessary.

Nessa pointed to the far hearth. "That flue draws. This one doesn't. We cook there, dry things here once Tor has looked at it. Nobody puts bedding beside either until we've had a fire through the night."

Tor opened his mouth.

"You can look with one hand," she said. "You can't climb."

"I know what I can't do."

"I know what you'll do when nobody's watching."

Pell's smile vanished into her collar.

Kael carried the worst of the broken furniture into the yard. He had expected to find a place ready for people. Instead he found wet boards, mouse filth, empty cupboards and labor waiting to be done by bodies already used past comfort. His shoulders ached when he bent. The deep weariness of the previous days had acquired small, separate injuries: a rubbed heel, a split knuckle, the splinter under his nail.

He set a rotten bench on the ground.

Mira came out with a bucket and poured dark water beneath the hedge.

"Anwen says you slept."

"Anwen was in another room."

"You were making enough noise."

"That could have been the chimney."

She leaned against the wall beside him. Her sleeves were rolled above the elbow, and there was a gray mark beneath her cheekbone where she had wiped her face with a dirty wrist. For a moment he wanted to put his mouth there. It was a foolishly precise desire. He had admitted worse things to her than that, yet this morning he could not find a way to touch her without asking what her silence meant.

She turned the bucket between her hands.

"There's a child inside with a rash. Nessa thinks it's from the wool. I'm not sure."

"Do you need something?"

"Clean light. Which seems a large request in this house."

He looked at the washhouse. Its southern wall still had glass in two of the windows.

"I'll empty that room."

"There's a leak."

"I'll find it."

She nodded. Then she touched his forearm, briefly, with the back of her fingers. He watched her go in.

By midday the leak had a name and an explanation. It was a gap where the last row of tiles met the wall, caused by a timber that had sagged after years of water. Kael could cover it. He could not repair it properly before night.

Tor examined the temporary support from below, calling instructions through the window.

"Move your foot."

Kael moved his foot.

"The other one."

"There are only two."

"And you're using both badly."

They fixed a strip of tarred canvas beneath the loose tiles and weighted its edge. When Kael came down, his hands black with old soot, Tor was sitting on an upturned pail. He had unbound the sling at the elbow. The wrist beneath the splints was swollen, the fingers a faint sickly yellow.

"Too tight?" Kael asked.

"No."

Tor did not look at him.

Kael fetched Mira. Tor swore when he saw her, but he gave her the arm.

***

The first arrivals came before the beds were built.

Two carts halted in the yard with the drivers arguing over which had damaged the track. A woman sat among folded blankets, keeping one hand on a pot so it would not strike the side. A small boy slept against her thigh. In the second cart an older man held his hat between his knees with the attention of someone carrying a bowl filled to its rim.

Siv walked beside them, her carrying pole balanced over an empty shoulder.

"They came up from the landing," she told Nera. "Asked which way. I said I'd show them."

"How many?"

"Seven."

"Anyone hurt?"

"The old man can't see much. That little one has been sick."

Bera stood beside the gate, studying the yard.

"You're going to need a ditch there," she said.

"We've only been here since yesterday," Kael said.

"Water won't wait for your anniversary."

She had a bundle of switches under her arm. Before he could offer to take it she shook it open, selected a straight piece and pushed it into the mud where the cartwheel had begun to sink.

"From here. Past that post."

The woman with the sleeping child asked Nera where she should put her name.

Nera pointed toward the house. "Inside first."

"Do you need the household paper?"

"For what?"

"The bed."

The question embarrassed them both. Kael saw the woman glance toward Merrow, standing under the eaves with a stack of unused forms. He looked down at them, then put them behind his back.

Nessa appeared in the doorway.

"Bring that pot. If it's sound, we can use it. If it leaks, leave it outside."

The woman climbed carefully from the cart. No one asked her what she had seen, whom she blamed, whether she would stand in an assembly or lay a hand upon a book. She carried the pot inside.

Vale had made a list of necessary information: number of people, bedding needed, food they could not eat, anyone who might arrive later. The line for names was optional. Merrow read the sheet twice.

"How will you distinguish two households with the same number?"

"Ask them," Vale said.

He considered that.

"Where are you keeping the list?"

"Where the kitchen can use it."

"That isn't—"

He stopped. Vale waited, her face composed.

"It will need to stay dry," he finished.

"Yes."

He found a slate weight for the corner.

Work spread through the house in awkward bursts. Toma and Kael carried the bed frames upstairs. Pell sorted pegs until she became bored, then sorted them into people: tall thin pegs were Senn, square ones were Tor, a cracked one with an improbable bend was Rook.

"Which one's me?" Kael asked.

She held up a short black peg.

"I'm taller than that."

"It's the expression."

He used it in the first bed.

Toma wanted a patch of ground below the spring. He said it while they were hauling the second frame through the turn in the stairs, as though the subject required no more preparation than a difficult corner.

"For beds?"

"Growing."

"It's almost winter."

"Then we'll know where the water lies. Can't choose a garden by looking at it in summer."

Pell, behind them, said she wanted a window.

"You can both have a window," Kael grunted. "If you stop talking until this is through."

Later, when the frames stood level, Toma showed her two possible places for their blankets. She chose the smaller alcove beside the eastern window, which had a view of the spring and no convenient place for anyone else's bed.

"That's cold," he said.

"I like it."

"You'll complain."

"I can like it and complain."

He scratched his beard. "Apparently."

Downstairs, Elna had taken possession of a narrow cupboard beside the stairs. Kael found her scraping old tallow from its shelf with a broken knife.

"What are you putting there?"

"My things."

He had been about to suggest medicines. She must have read it in his face.

"There's a whole house. You'll survive without this bit."

"I was only asking."

"So was everybody who took the last cupboard."

He left her to it.

***

The records went into the washhouse after the floor was scrubbed and the patch had survived a bucket of water poured over the roof.

Vale laid the command copy on a clean board. She did not unfold it. Beside it she placed the separate account of Edric's suppression, Bram's surviving figures and the protected statements. Each had its own wrapping, its own identifying mark. Kael could have recognized some of those packets in darkness now. He did not like how much depended on objects that a spark or a hand might destroy.

"We need them apart," he said.

"We need the copies apart," Vale answered. "I need the sources together long enough to check what we've made."

She moved a page toward him. The public summary named institutions, offices, dates where the evidence supported dates. It described promised sanctuary, closed retreat, killings, reuse of ash and iron. It named the present ration condition. Homes and family marks had been left out.

At the bottom Vale had written three questions.

"Those stay questions," she said. "I won't turn them into facts because the account looks unfinished."

Merrow stood near the window. He had offered his handwriting. Vale had accepted his labor and seated him where she could see the page.

Kael watched him copy the heading. The letters were small and evenly spaced. It made him angry that a man could write so well.

Anwen came in carrying two linen covers. She had washed them herself, and the hems were still damp.

"These were for the beds," she said. "There's more cloth coming."

"We can't promise that," Nessa said from the doorway.

Anwen looked at her. "Then I'll find something else for the beds."

"No. Use them. Just don't tell people things are coming until you see them on the road."

For a moment Anwen's mouth tightened. Then she laid the cloth on the table.

"You're right."

Nessa looked dissatisfied at having won so quickly and went back to the kitchen.

They settled the custody before supper. Vale would hold the checked copy nearest at hand. Anwen would keep a second packet in a different part of the building. Access to protected statements required the person who had given them, or the limits that person had expressly left. Merrow could make copies under another pair of eyes. He would not carry a key.

He put his hand flat on the table.

"That will slow things."

"Yes," Vale said.

He withdrew his hand.

Kael's corrected account lay among the protected packets. He knew which one without looking directly at it. The knowledge had weight. So did the knowledge that Nera could refuse to hear him read it again.

Outside, Bera was telling three people that a drain should go downhill even if they disagreed about everything else. The woman with the pot had begun washing lentils. Pell brought in the first bowl of pegs Kael had asked for and announced she had lost him.

"What?"

"Your peg. It went into the wall."

"Good place for it."

She considered the remaining shapes. "Rook's still here."

"Usually is."

By dusk they had nine beds raised, space cleared for the rest, a kitchen that could feed more people than it could seat, and one dry room in which Mira could see the color of a child's skin. It was a house with work still wrong in it. Smoke leaked from the unused hearth, and one upper window would not close. The yard was a trench.

Nessa served the first meal from the borrowed pot.

Kael took his bowl to the stairs. A girl he did not know was asleep against the new door, her cheek on a folded coat. He tried to step over her without waking her. She opened one eye.

"Is this where we're staying?"

"For tonight."

"And tomorrow?"

He looked into the room: steam, wet sleeves, Tor having his food cut without admitting it, Elna guarding a cupboard no one wanted, Anwen carrying a second bowl to the woman whose child had a rash.

"If you want," he said.

The girl shut her eye.

Kael sat two steps above her and ate.


# Chapter 29


## What Remains Private


Mira found Kael in the washhouse trying to make a spoon.

He had taken a split piece of applewood from the yard. The first spoon had broken across the bowl. The second looked like a small injured foot.

"Is that for someone you dislike?"

He turned it over.

"It's a spoon."

"I can see the argument for it."

She stood with one shoulder against the door. The house behind her was quiet except for someone coughing upstairs and the irregular knock of Tor checking the repaired flue. Kael had chosen this room because Vale's work was finished for the night and because he could sit here without anyone asking him to carry anything.

Mira held a rolled towel.

"You left this by the pump."

"I was coming back."

"I know."

She put it beside him. There had been a time when those words would have needed nothing around them. Now he heard the echo before she could decide whether she had meant one.

He laid the knife down.

"Was that—"

"No."

He waited.

"I brought your towel, Kael."

"All right."

She pulled out the other stool and sat. Her hair was loose, falling forward when she bent to inspect the spoon. He had wanted to see her with her hair down all day. He wished that wanting her did not make every small thing feel so dangerous.

"I haven't asked you what you're doing tomorrow," she said.

"Roof. Firewood. Nera wants someone on the outer road."

"I meant after."

"I don't know."

She watched his hands.

"I keep thinking about that report."

"So do I."

"That's a poor answer."

"It's the one I've got."

She took the broken spoon from the floor. Her thumb passed over the place where he had cut too deeply.

"When someone dies under my hands, I go back through what I did. Sometimes there's a thing I missed. Sometimes the thing I missed wouldn't have helped. I can spend a day trying to work out which. Then somebody comes with a burned hand and wants me to look at it."

Kael looked at the knife.

"What I did wasn't a mistake with a bandage."

"I didn't say it was."

"I know."

"No. You keep making the worst possible version of what I'm saying, then agreeing with it before I've finished."

He felt anger rise, quick and welcome because it was easier than the rest. He pressed his thumb against the rough wood.

"Go on, then."

Mira set the broken spoon on the bench.

"I don't know what to do with loving someone who could leave those people."

She had said it before, angrily. In his head he had kept the love and softened the anger, though she had offered him no reason to separate them. Now he sat among wrapped records and wood shavings, hearing both again.

She rubbed her palms against her knees.

"I didn't come here to give you that like a gift."

"I know."

"You don't have to keep saying that either."

He drew a breath.

"Sometimes I was afraid. Sometimes I was tired. After a while it was easier to be somewhere else. I made each day small enough that I didn't have to call it leaving them."

The room seemed colder after he said it.

Mira looked toward the window. He thought she might go. She had every right. He had begun to hate that sentence in his own head because it could become another way of doing nothing.

"I want you here," he said. "I don't know what that buys me."

"It doesn't buy you anything."

"No."

"But I want it too."

She said it looking straight at him. The relief hurt his throat.

He reached across the gap between the stools. She let him take her hand. There was a small blister along the side of her finger where the mortar handle had rubbed.

"I can't stay wherever you stay simply because you stay there," she said.

"I wasn't asking."

"I know. I'm telling you before either of us starts pretending we assumed the same thing."

She had patients still in Greyfen, tools at the mill, beds of useful plants which would need moving before the first hard frost. If Hearthward became a place where people lived, she would need a room with a door and a window, shelves of the right depth and someone else willing to wash the rags. She wanted two afternoons in a week when nobody came merely because they were bored or lonely.

"That last one won't happen," Kael said.

"No. But I want it."

"What do you want from me?"

She considered him.

"An answer when I ask where you're going. Help when you've said you'll help. And occasionally to put a thing down because I asked, without deciding I'm really asking you to become a better man."

He looked at the spoon.

"Does this count?"

"That should be put down for everyone's sake."

He laughed, and she did too, though hers caught on a tired breath. He leaned forward. She met him halfway. The kiss was brief and familiar enough to make him miss the others they had not had.

Afterward she took the knife.

"You're working against the grain."

"Tor's been telling you things."

"Tor tells everybody things."

She showed him where the bowl ought to begin.

***

The next evening Nessa permitted a celebration on the condition that nobody call it one while she was cooking.

"There isn't enough to celebrate," she said.

There was, however, a batch of small flat loaves made with the last of a sack that had torn during the journey. There were onions browned in a pan, dried apples cut thin, and a jug of something Rook said was cider if you respected its intentions.

They ate in the long room. The repaired chimney drew so strongly that smoke vanished up it with a startled hiss. Tor sat where he could look at the hearth without appearing to inspect his work.

"Needs another brace outside," he said.

Nessa pushed a loaf into his good hand.

Rook attempted to get comfortable on a bench. Each adjustment made his face change. He had developed a cautious laugh, all breath and no movement, which made his jokes sound worse.

"I miss the old chairs," he said.

"Which old chairs?" Elna asked.

"The ones I haven't sat on yet."

"You're free to stand."

"That's what I like about you. The range of remedies."

She brought him a folded blanket anyway.

Pell and the girl who had slept by the door had made a board game from a scored piece of slate. The rules changed whenever one of them was losing. Vale watched for several minutes, then offered a correction.

"You can't have moved that piece there. The corner was occupied."

Pell considered her gravely.

"It went under."

"Under what?"

"The other piece."

"That isn't one of the rules."

"It is now."

Vale looked at Kael. He shrugged.

"Local law."

She asked if she could play the next game.

Within ten minutes she had been defeated, challenged the result, and lost an appeal to a judge who was eating her second portion of bread. Merrow, who had been quietly counting the pieces, made the mistake of saying he thought Vale had a point.

They made him play after her.

For a while Kael could listen without looking for danger in every raised voice. The room had too many people in it and no one quite enough space. He could smell damp wool, onions, the sour cider and the clean sharpness of the wood Tor had split for kindling. Mira sat beside him, her knee touching his beneath the table.

At the other end Elna was showing Anwen a small object in her hand.

Kael recognized a button. It was horn, worn smooth, with one hole broken through to the edge.

"Harl's?" Anwen asked.

"He couldn't leave buttons alone. Picked at them when he was thinking."

Elna smiled before she remembered that she had meant to be angry.

"I used to find them in the bed. In the flour bin once. Only he knew why he'd been thinking in the flour bin."

"Looking for something to eat," Rook said.

"He knew better."

Her hand closed around the button.

Later Kael found her at the cupboard beneath the stairs. She was fitting the horn button into a small cloth bag, along with a comb and a folded piece of old blue fabric.

"You don't have to hide that," he said.

"I'm putting it away."

"Yes."

She pulled the drawstring tight.

"People hear a thing about a dead man and they want you to empty him out on the table. Everything. What he ate. What he said in bed. As if one of those things will settle the other."

Kael leaned against the stairpost. He could hear Merrow objecting to a move in the game.

"They won't ask me what he did at the gate," Elna said. "They ask what I think he felt."

"What do you tell them?"

"Depends how much they've annoyed me."

She put the bag on the shelf.

"I loved him. I made him kinder when I told it. Both are true. I'm tired of people looking relieved when I admit the second. It doesn't fix what they wanted fixed."

Kael said nothing.

"He never told me he carried a child out," she continued. "Never said he opened the gate a crack. I could invent something. People would like it."

"Some would."

"I'd like it."

Her face hardened, and he understood she was furious with herself for that, too.

"Keep the button," he said.

"I intend to."

From the room came a shout of laughter, followed by Rook asking everybody to be less funny until his ribs had mended.

Elna shut the cupboard.

***

The public account was ready the following afternoon.

Vale read it aloud in the washhouse. Rain touched the patched roof without entering. Merrow sat opposite her with the checked pages arranged in order. Anwen stood at the window, and Kael held the door while people came in and out according to how much time they had.

The account was shorter than he expected.

It said what the old records could support. Sanctuary promised. Gates shut. Retreat barred. People killed. Names removed or entered in a category that implied a journey had ended safely. Materials taken and used. It separated the old command from the later order to suppress it. It described the household condition attached to present grain.

There were gaps in it large enough for a man to hide inside.

"Can we say they knew?" Tor asked.

"Which they?" Vale said.

He was irritated by the question, then answered it. The discussion improved.

When she reached a passage identifying one of the family sources, Elna tapped the board.

"That narrows it to three houses."

Vale looked again.

"It does."

She struck the description through and wrote a different one. Merrow shifted, as though he wanted to point out how much less precise it made the page.

"Say it," Vale told him.

"It's harder to check."

"Then someone with permission can check the protected copy. This is the one that goes on the wall."

He bent over his page. "I'll leave space for the correction."

Nera arrived near the end. Her boots were muddy to the ankle. She had been walking the longer approach to the house with two of the drivers.

"Road's passable," she said. "Bad at the bend above the spring. We need gravel."

Vale showed her the section referring to Kael's later retreat. His own admission was there, separate from Greyfen's old crime. The family particulars were absent.

Nera read it twice.

"Leave my people out of the public page," she said.

"They are."

"I can see that. I'm saying it so nobody decides later that I only forgot to ask."

Kael felt every person in the room become careful around him. He would have preferred anger.

"I heard you," he said.

Nera put the page down.

Siv and Bera had left with the morning cart for Greyfen. They had come ahead to see the house and help make it usable; their own people were still at home. Now they could describe the rooms, fetch the bedding and ask each household to make its departure arrangements. The families Siv had guided up the road remained here, learning where Nessa kept the bowls.

"The drivers want to know whether the outer yard will hold two wagons if we have to turn one. Bera says your ditch is crooked."

"It follows the ground."

"That's what I told her."

"What did she say?"

"She said the ground could stand to improve itself."

Anwen laughed. It escaped her unexpectedly, a little too loud. She looked startled, then almost ashamed.

Elna handed her the cloth bag of spare buttons.

"Make yourself useful. Pell has lost three off that coat."

Anwen took the bag. On the table the public account dried beside its corrections. In the locked cupboard behind Vale, the protected pages remained more complicated than the version that could safely travel.

Kael helped Nera carry the bench outside. They needed it in the yard to mark how far a wagon could turn. His bad thumbnail caught on the wood. He hissed and shifted his grip.

"Splinter?" she asked.

"Yesterday."

"You should have taken it out."

"Everybody's full of advice."

"That's because you keep doing things badly."

She said it without warmth, but she did not drop her end.


# Chapter 30


## What We Promise


Nessa made Kael serve the noon meal with a ladle whose handle had been repaired three times.

"That's too much."

He tipped a little back.

"Now it's too little."

He looked at her.

"Don't look at me. Look at the bowl."

The trouble was that no two bowls were alike. Some were broad and shallow, some narrow enough to make a small portion appear generous. The people waiting could see the differences. A man with a wooden bowl accused Kael of favoring the woman ahead of him. She offered to exchange portions. That made him angrier.

Nessa took both bowls, poured them back into the pot and filled them again.

"There. Be suspicious of me. He's too slow."

Afterward she made the man help wash the bowls. He agreed readily enough once he had eaten. Kael watched them disputing the best way to dry wood and wondered how often a quarrel needed food rather than an answer.

They were rehearsing the house.

Nera had used that phrase at breakfast. It sounded absurd until they tried to hand a simple task to someone who had not done it before. The clean rags were in the wrong cupboard. The woman who knew which child reacted badly to milk had gone to the spring. Merrow's list contained a mark nobody else could read. The kitchen needed to know when a cart arrived, but the person watching the road thought his only duty was to stop trouble.

By the afternoon there were charcoal arrows on the walls and a bowl beside the door in which arrivals could leave a plain wooden counter for each person needing supper. Nessa distrusted the counters immediately.

"Somebody will take two."

"Then we'll have fed someone who was hungry twice," Mira said.

"We might also run out before the last lot get here."

They settled on a person beside the bowl. Merrow offered.

Nessa looked at him for so long that he began to explain.

"I can count."

"That much we know."

He sat by the door anyway.

Tor had made an alarm from a cracked iron pan. It hung beneath the covered step on a short length of cord. The agreed signal was three slow blows, a pause, then three more. Pell struck it nine times in succession during the first trial.

Everyone came.

"That's good," she said.

"You were supposed to stop after three," Toma told her.

"They were still coming."

A baby upstairs began to scream. Nessa emerged carrying a knife and a half-cut turnip.

"Who is dying?"

"No one," Nera said.

"Then someone had better explain this to the baby."

The second trial was quieter. Anwen went upstairs beforehand. Mira stood by the outside road, out of sight of the door, and failed to hear the pan at all when the wind came up.

They moved it.

Kael held the ladder while Tor directed Toma with a series of increasingly small gestures. Toma drove the peg too far. Tor shut his eyes.

"It'll hold," Toma said.

"That's the problem. Now I can't take it out."

"Why would you?"

"Because it's wrong."

Nera went around the corner and tested the sound. This time she raised a hand.

It was only a pan on a wall. It had taken five people half a day to put it where it worked.

***

The rota was harder.

They laid the names on the table in the long room, using stones to hold down the page. Someone had left flour on the boards. Vale brushed it away from her elbow.

"We can write one week," she said. "Then do it again."

"A week is a long time," Rook said.

"A night is a long time if everyone assumes someone else is awake."

He touched his ribs and conceded the point.

Nessa offered to keep the stores and the meal lists, provided someone else did the heavy lifting and she had one morning when she could leave without being chased down the road.

"Where will you go?" Pell asked.

"Somewhere nobody knows what I can cook."

Pell thought this an excellent plan.

Vale took the records. She asked for a second reader, a dry box and the right to tell people they would have to wait. Merrow offered to be the second reader. She said they could discuss that after the Assembly.

"I meant the copying," he said.

"I know what you meant."

Mira agreed to organize care, with Anwen and anyone willing to learn the ordinary work: water, linen, food, sitting beside a person who should not be alone. She would not promise a cure where she had none. She would not stay at Hearthward every day.

Anwen's fingers closed around a loose end of gray thread.

"And if keeping the ward needs us here?" she asked.

Conversation stopped, unevenly. People farther down the table kept talking until they noticed the silence near her.

They had carried enough fragments of the old arrangement to know that a living community might ask something different of the Hart. What they did not yet know was precisely what it would require. Vale had written that uncertainty beside the proposed duties.

"If it requires someone who can't leave," Mira said, "I haven't agreed to that."

"No," Anwen said quickly. "No, I didn't mean—"

She looked at the page. Kael saw her swallow.

"I can't take another binding."

Nessa leaned back. Her chair creaked.

"Then don't."

"It isn't that simple."

"For you, perhaps."

Anwen's face colored. She pulled at the thread until it snapped.

"I've been listening for that bell since winter," she said. "Even where there isn't one. I hear a cart chain and I think—"

She stopped. Her hands lay flat on the table. For a moment no one filled the silence.

Mira reached past her for the rota.

"You can take the afternoon linen. Or nothing this week."

"I want to work."

"Then take the linen."

Anwen nodded once.

Nera declined the proposed continuing duty at the crossing. She had been studying it as if the words were a fastening she could test with her thumb.

"I'll bring people through," she said. "I'll teach the crews what we know. I'm not staying beside that water all winter waiting for permission to take another load elsewhere."

"No one asked you to," Rook said.

"The line says permanent."

Vale crossed out the word.

"That was my word," she said.

Nera pushed the paper back. "Crossing and relief, for the work we're doing now. Ask again afterward."

Nessa tapped the place beside her own name.

"I'll take the counting if we ask for a shared arrangement. Goods in, goods out, who's short. I can stop doing it when I need to, and someone has to be ready before then."

Vale wrote it as she had said it.

Mira read over the page. "Care, on those terms."

"Records," Vale said. "The same."

Kael looked from one to the other. They had made an alternative with three ordinary answers. It could hold. He had half expected someone to refuse the last duty, allowing them all to say the choice had been taken away.

No one did.

They would still have to hear the Hart's terms. Nothing said at the table bound anyone to words they had not heard. But the people needed for a shared keeping were willing to consider it, and the house had begun to show that they could do the work.

Rook rubbed his thumb over the edge of his cup.

"What about people who stay where they are?"

"We're making room," Nera said. "We aren't emptying the village by order."

He nodded, too readily.

Kael noticed. He left it alone.

Senn's place on the page remained a question until Nera took it to him. He was lodged in the small room beyond the pantry, with a guard at the outer door and another sleeping when the first stood watch. The guards had their own names, lives and complaints; Kael had come to know them chiefly by which one whistled through his teeth.

Nera asked Senn whether he would speak at the Assembly.

"That question again?"

"It's still a question."

He looked past her toward the yard.

"Yes. About my own orders and acts."

"Not about anything you think will get you released."

A faint, unpleasant smile moved his mouth.

"You've made that clear."

"Good."

She asked separately what he wanted after the Assembly. His guards would take him to Greyfen for the hearing; if the departure went ahead, he could return to Hearthward with the households or remain in guarded custody in Greyfen.

He looked at the low ceiling, then at the guard.

"The same conditions?"

"Under guard either way. We'll need to settle food and shelter in whichever place you choose."

He was silent long enough that the whistling guard stopped whistling.

"I'll come back here," Senn said.

Nera arranged the outward journey for the day after the watch. The guards would accompany him to Greyfen while Kael, Vale and Merrow went to the Keep.

Nera wrote it down. She did not put his name among the keepers.

***

Kael took the first half of the night with Nera.

They went around the house together before the others slept. The outside road was clear. The yard ditch carried a thin stream past Bera's marker. A lantern stood at the turn, shielded so its light could be seen from the track without shining directly into a watcher's eyes.

Nera checked its hook.

"You checked that an hour ago," Kael said.

"An hour ago there wasn't wind."

He held the light while she tightened the leather.

Inside, the household settled by degrees. Someone wanted water. Someone else wanted silence. The little boy from the cart had lost a shoe and refused to sleep until it was found inside a folded blanket. Merrow counted the empty bowls, twice, and was told to stop helping.

Kael and Nera sat beneath the covered step. They had the repaired door behind them and the yard in front. Bracken emerged from under the wood stack, nose low, the small scales along his back catching the lantern light. He investigated Nera's boot.

"There's nothing in there for you."

He began to lick the mud anyway.

Kael nudged him away with the side of his foot. Bracken climbed onto the dry plank between them and folded himself into a lump.

For a while they said nothing. Nera had stretched one leg into the yard. She rubbed the muscle above her knee through the cloth.

"Hurts?" Kael asked.

"Some days."

He waited. She did not explain further.

At the change of watch he wanted to stay. He had been measuring himself by whether he could bear to leave a post, and the habit had begun to feel virtuous.

Nera stood.

"Wake the others."

"I can do another hour."

"They need to know the work."

"I know."

"Then wake them."

He went upstairs.

Toma came down with his hair crushed on one side. Anwen followed carrying a shawl and two cups. They listened while Nera showed them the road, the lamp, the pan and the places they had checked. Kael told Toma about a loose board that sounded like a footstep when the wind lifted it.

"Anything else?"

He almost said no. Then he remembered.

"The guard at the back door hasn't eaten. He said he had, but his bowl's still on the shelf."

Anwen took one of the cups inside.

Toma sat on the plank. Bracken opened an eye, judged him acceptable, and pushed his head against his thigh.

Nera touched Kael's sleeve to draw him away from the door.

They climbed the stairs. He looked back once. Toma had put both hands around the warm cup Anwen had brought him. She was showing him where to keep the spare wick.

The watch continued without them.

Upstairs Kael found his blanket beside Mira's. She shifted when he lay down, making room without waking fully. He took off his boots slowly so the buckles would not strike the bed frame. There was a small wooden spoon on the stool beside him, its bowl scraped smooth by someone more competent than he was.

He picked it up.

Mira opened one eye.

"Don't lose it."

He set it down and lay beside her.


# Chapter 31


## The Refusal Book


They left Hearthward before the lamps were out.

Merrow wore his old office coat. Vale had insisted on it. If they were stopped, she wanted the people stopping them to recognize the man who had signed the inspections, rather than discover afterward that he had been disguised as somebody's harmless uncle.

Kael carried a short iron bar wrapped in sacking. Merrow kept looking at it.

"You can't open the inner lock with that."

"Then we'll carry the box."

"It's bolted down."

"We'll carry the bench."

Merrow stopped looking.

The service road brought them below the Keep by late morning. Carts filled the lower court. A clerk had been stationed beside the scales with two guards and a board of fresh notices. Kael let Vale read before he went near it. He was learning that being angry at a page did not help him understand it.

"Household quantities to be reverified," she said. "Existing marks suspended until renewal."

"All of them?"

"That's what it says."

The queue had barely moved. A woman held a sack open for the clerk to see it was empty. He explained something to her. She shook the sack once, sharply, and the guard stepped closer.

Merrow went pale.

"He'll say it isn't refusal. Only a delay."

"You know that language," Vale said.

"I used it."

They passed through the store court. Merrow's coat still worked there, though less confidently than it once had. A porter greeted him and received such a careful reply that he looked after them.

The north kitchen had been abandoned when the stores expanded. Its chimney was blocked, and the old service passage carried a chill which seemed to have been trapped in the stones. Merrow unlocked the outer door. Kael stepped inside first.

A chopping bench stood beneath the window. Somebody had scrubbed its surface often enough to wear a hollow in the middle. The strongbox was bolted underneath it.

Merrow knelt. His small flat key opened one lock.

He tried the other with his fingers, as if it might have changed since he last saw it.

"Edric still has that key," he said.

"Then move."

Kael put the bar beneath the bench. The first effort accomplished nothing. The second tore a strip from the old wood. Vale held the lantern while Merrow watched the door.

Outside a cart rolled over the stones.

"Again," Vale said.

The bench gave a dry crack. Kael put his weight against it. Pain crossed his shoulder. He stopped long enough to shift the bar, then leaned until a leg came away and the iron box struck the floor.

The noise filled the kitchen.

Nobody spoke.

Footsteps paused outside. A man called Merrow's name.

Merrow wiped his hands on his coat and opened the door a hand's breadth.

"An inventory."

"Kitchen's empty."

"Which would make it a short one."

Kael had never heard that tone from him. It was a small, irritated voice which assumed obedience. For a moment it worked.

Then the man said, "Lord Edric wants the keys you kept off the office ring."

Merrow's fingers tightened on the door.

"My private keys?"

"His order came this morning. All the old store keys as well."

"Nobody told me."

"He said you'd say that."

Kael moved the bar from the bench to the box.

Vale shook her head once. Too loud.

The man pushed the door. Merrow stepped back, and a store guard entered with his hand on his belt. His eyes went to the broken bench, then to the strongbox.

"What is that?"

"An account," Vale said.

"I wasn't asking you."

"You'll have to."

The guard looked at Kael's sword.

Kael kept his hands where he could see them.

"No one's hurt."

"Then leave it that way."

The guard took a whistle from his belt. Merrow caught his wrist.

It was a poor attempt. The guard was younger and stronger. He twisted, drove Merrow against the wall and struck him once in the mouth. Kael crossed the space before the whistle reached the man's lips.

He trapped the arm against the door frame. The guard kicked backward, catching Kael above the knee. They lurched into the passage together.

"Let go."

"Put it down."

The guard tried to bite him.

Kael drove him against the wall harder than he intended. A shelf fell somewhere behind them. Vale took the whistle from the man's open hand. Merrow, blood on his chin, caught the outside of the door and held it steady while Kael brought the guard's arm behind him.

They used the sacking to bind his wrists. He spat at Merrow.

"You signed my brother's allotment."

Merrow flinched.

"I know."

"You came to our house."

"I know."

Kael sat the guard on the floor. His shoulder would hurt. He was breathing without difficulty. Vale checked the back of his head and was sworn at for her trouble.

"I'll leave the door unlocked," she said. "You can shout when we're gone."

"You think a book will feed them?"

"No."

She turned away from him.

The inner lock held. The box's old hinge did not. Kael worked until the metal distorted enough for him to lift one corner of the lid. Merrow steadied it with both hands, bleeding onto his cuff. At last Vale could get her fingers inside and draw out a narrow volume wrapped in faded cloth.

She opened it beneath the window.

Columns. Dates. Quantities. Household marks. Requests refused.

At the foot of one entry Merrow had written a second reason beneath the official one.

*Would not assent.*

Vale looked up.

"Why did you keep this?"

Merrow touched his swollen lip.

"If they asked who had decided—"

"You wanted to prove you weren't the only one."

"Yes."

"And sometimes you were the one."

He looked at the page.

"Yes."

She wrapped the book.

Kael fetched the guard a cup of water from a covered jar and set it where he could reach it with his bound hands. Then they left by the service passage, carrying neither the box nor the bench.

The guard began shouting before they reached the court.

They had to run the last part.

***

By the time they came back to Greyfen, Kael's shoulder had stiffened and Merrow's mouth was almost twice its ordinary size.

They had slept in an empty cart shelter on the road. Vale had spent an hour checking entries by lantern light before Kael persuaded her to put the book away. It was evidence of particular refusals, dates and signatures. It did not explain what every hungry person had suffered. It did not tell them what the dead beneath the shrine had seen.

It would make Edric's proposed distinction between refusal and delay harder to maintain.

The village had filled while they were gone. People had come for the Assembly, for grain, to find relatives, to see whether the rumors about the Keep were true. A few had come because there might be a fight and they did not want to miss it.

They found one already beginning beside the old stable.

Senn stood against the wall with the ferryman in front of him. The man's bad knee had given way. He had one hand on a post and the other raised toward a group blocking the yard.

The broad-shouldered driver stood at Senn's other side. She held a cudgel low.

A young man in a patched coat had a paving stone in his hand.

"Let him look at me," he said.

Senn was looking. That seemed to make it worse.

"My grandfather went into the ash without a name because of you."

A woman behind him said something Kael could not catch. Someone else shouted that grain would be coming if they finished this properly.

Kael went into the gap between the stone and Senn.

The young man recognized him.

"You're keeping him safe."

"I'm keeping him here."

"Safe."

"Here."

He could hear how little difference the words made.

Senn opened his mouth.

"Don't," Kael said.

The man with the stone laughed once.

"Afraid of what he'll tell us?"

"No. Afraid he'll find something clever to say."

That unsettled the crowd more than an appeal would have. Rook, standing at the edge of it with one arm tight across his ribs, seized the interval.

"If you're going to throw it, move away from my window."

Several people turned.

"What?"

"My window. Behind him. It costs money. The stone doesn't stop when you're satisfied."

The young man looked at the window, which was not Rook's.

Rook kept his face perfectly grave.

Nera arrived from the well and asked the ferryman whether he could stand. She did not ask the crowd to be good. She moved the driver, made a space, and brought Senn into the stable with Kael behind them. The stone struck the door after it shut.

Inside it smelled of old hay.

Senn leaned against the partition.

"I could have answered him."

"Then answer tomorrow," Nera said. "If he wants to ask."

The ferryman sat on a stool, breathing through his teeth. She knelt to look at his knee. Kael heard the crowd slowly breaking into smaller arguments outside.

Rook came in through the side door.

"Whose window was it?" Kael asked.

"Tor's old store."

"He'll be pleased."

"He can invoice me for not breaking it."

Nera looked up. She did not smile.

Rook's expression changed.

"I didn't expect you to."

"I hadn't said anything."

"I know."

He sat on an overturned trough, carefully. His ribs had made him conscious of every place he might have to rest.

"I need to tell you something before you finish the departure lists," he said.

Nera stood.

"I'm staying."

Kael thought he meant until the Assembly. Then he saw the way Rook watched the door.

"In Greyfen?"

"Yes."

"After the ward?" Nera asked.

"If that's what happens."

"You understand what the road may become."

"I've been on roads without it."

"You've also been on this one with it."

Rook looked down at his hands.

"I have a room. I know which roof leaks. I know who's likely to pay me and who needs asking three times. Every time you talk about a new place, you make it sound as if we all get to arrive there clean."

"I haven't said that," Nera answered.

"No. I know. It's what I hear."

He picked at a loose stitch in his cuff.

"I don't want to be another man standing in somebody else's doorway with all his things in a sack."

For a moment Kael saw him without his usual motion: twenty-nine, frightened, wounded, still trying to turn a choice into a transaction he understood.

"What will you do?" Kael asked.

"Trade, if anyone will let me. Store things. Carry messages. There's still a village here."

"Some people won't trust your messages," Nera said.

"Then they'll check them."

She crossed to the doorway, looked at the thinning crowd and came back.

"You'll have a share of the grain. Same as the other households."

"I can pay."

"That isn't what I said."

Rook nodded.

"I can help load. Tell people where the carts are. With these ribs I'm better at telling than lifting."

"Yes," Nera said. "You are."

He accepted that, too.

***

They arranged the Assembly around the common space, with a clear lane from the grain carts to the road.

That was Nessa's condition.

"If people have to listen before they can get a sack, you've done it again with more chairs."

The grain already in the village was counted where everyone could see. The quantities were smaller than the people gathering had hoped. They would have to say that plainly.

Vale and Merrow worked beneath an awning, comparing his duplicate with the public notices and surviving receipts. Kael watched Merrow put his own initials beside three refusals. He did not excuse them. Nor did he suddenly become useful enough to make them disappear.

Edric sent word that he would attend under his own protection.

"Of course he will," Rook said. "He wants to see where the grain goes."

Senn would be brought out when invited, with his guards. His statement would be his choice. His custody would continue.

Anwen carried benches from the shrine until Mira made her stop and eat. Tor argued about the distance between the carts. Elna asked where people who did not want to speak could sit.

"Anywhere," Vale said.

"Then don't put us all at the back."

They moved another bench.

Kael stood in the lane as dusk gathered beneath the awning. He had imagined exposure as a moment when everyone saw the same thing at once. Instead there were wet stools, too little flour, a guard with a sore knee, a man who had chosen to stay and a book whose author had kept it partly to save himself.

Mira came beside him and pressed a cloth-wrapped heel of bread into his hand.

"Eat before you decide to make another speech."

"I haven't decided to make one."

"Good. Eat anyway."

He did.


# Chapter 32


## Crowns Without Mercy


Edric arrived while the grain was being weighed.

He came with four armed men and a clerk carrying a leather case. The crowd opened around them, reluctantly. He had dressed for the weather rather than for ceremony: a dark coat, good boots, gloves which he removed before approaching the table.

Kael stood beside the lane between the carts. He could smell the rain in Edric's wool.

"Whose order is this?" Edric asked.

Nessa did not stop pouring grain into the measure.

"The sacks were going damp."

"That wasn't my question."

"It was my problem."

He looked at the tally beneath her hand. "You haven't checked the household marks."

"No."

The word traveled farther than she had spoken it.

Edric turned toward Vale. She had placed three documents on the table, each weighted with a stone. The old command was wrapped and set aside. Today's dispute required no voice from thirty-two years ago.

"This is the current condition," she said. "This is the order suspending existing marks. This is the duplicate account of quantities refused."

Edric glanced at Merrow's swollen mouth before he looked at the book.

"You stole an official record."

"I kept a private duplicate," Merrow said.

"Of official records."

"Yes."

"You had no authority."

"No."

Edric waited for more. Merrow gave him none.

Vale turned the book to an entry she had marked. She showed the corresponding receipt. The quantity had been available. The household had met the ordinary distribution requirements. A second mark beside it recorded refusal of assent.

"There was food," she said.

"There are always limits."

"This refusal wasn't made because the store was empty."

Edric looked past her at the people around the carts.

"Do you imagine the wagons arrive because someone wishes for them? Patrols clear the roads. Stores are counted. Grain put aside in autumn is grain we can eat when the snow comes. If I release every obligation—"

"You can keep accounts," Nessa said. "I'm keeping one now."

"You know what I'm asking."

"Yes."

For the first time that morning he seemed uncertain which person he should answer. He could dismiss one objection, bargain with one opponent, explain the needs of the whole to one hungry household. The table gave him no single place to stand.

He approached the measure.

"Stop that until we've agreed a procedure."

Nessa held the scoop above it. She looked at Kael, then at the clerk with the leather case.

"Are you going to write down that he stopped it?"

The clerk did not answer.

Vale dipped her pen.

"I am."

Edric's nearest guard shifted his feet. It was a small movement, but Kael saw the people around him notice. The man was being asked to stand between a measure of grain and a woman with an empty sack. He had not drawn his sword.

Kael kept his own hands away from his.

"We're not asking you to forget the stores," he told Edric. "We're asking you to take that condition off them. In writing. The same order goes to the Keep and the mill. Quantities remain counted. Names for testimony aren't part of the count."

"And who enforces your order?"

"The people moving the grain will have a copy. So will the people receiving it."

"Copies don't escort wagons."

"No. Drivers do. Guards do. People who need wages, food and someone who will tell them plainly what they're being sent to do."

Edric's mouth tightened. "And if they refuse?"

"Then we know before we put a load on the road."

Behind Nessa, Tor rested his good hand on the side of a cart. Elna stood among the people waiting. Rook had brought receipts from three deliveries that had made it through without household assent, paid for by traders who wanted their customers alive. None of it could replace the whole store. Together it made Edric's assertion that there was only one workable arrangement less convincing.

Vale laid a fresh page on the table.

There was no grand declaration at its head. It said which grain would be released, how allocations would be recorded and where shortages had to be reported. It removed the condition from households that stayed as well as those that left. Disputes would be witnessed by people outside the office that made the decision. Two copies would travel by different hands.

Edric read every line.

"This gives you the power to open stores."

"Counted stores," Vale said. "With a record. It also gives people somewhere to complain when you close them."

"Somewhere other than me."

"Yes."

He looked toward the road. Kael could see him doing the calculation: the mill, the carts, the clerks, the faces of his own guards, the grain already in public view. There was danger in conceding. There was danger in being seen to withhold food after the means of release had been put beneath his hand.

"I won't have a mob deciding winter rations."

"Then help us make a record," Nessa said.

He took the pen.

The first order bore his name. The copies bore the names of those who had seen him sign and the limits he had accepted. He kept no power on the page to demand a household's account of the dead before it received its share. Merrow's office would be checked. So would Edric's. The men who carried the copies were named aloud.

Nessa emptied the measure into the waiting sack.

The woman receiving it had refused the renewal mark. Merrow found her in his duplicate. Vale drew a line through the pending refusal and wrote the amount delivered beside it, leaving the old entry readable.

Edric watched.

The woman tied the sack and left without approaching the benches.

Only then did Anwen ask whether anyone wanted to speak.

***

Kael had thought he might go first.

When the moment came, he let a woman ask how much grain would be left next week. He let a carter complain that the patrols had confiscated his good rope. He listened while Nessa explained that they had a quantity, a route and an order, and still needed fuel, wheels and people willing to travel.

The practical questions delayed him. He was grateful until he recognized the gratitude.

He stepped forward before he could make another reasonable excuse.

"I gave Vale an account of something I did eight years ago," he said.

A man near the back asked what that had to do with Greyfen.

"It isn't the same crime. I wasn't here then."

"Then why are we hearing it?"

"Because I've been asking people to answer for what they did."

Someone muttered. No one told them to be quiet.

Kael kept his eyes on a split in the tabletop.

"I escorted civilians on another road. We were ordered to retreat when we couldn't hold it. I obeyed. I left people I had promised to bring through."

He heard a child coughing in the crowd.

"My report said the road couldn't be held. That was true. It didn't say that I failed to go back afterward. There was time when I could have tried. I didn't."

The words did not expand to fill the square. They remained small. He had to say the last ones more loudly.

"I didn't go back."

Mira stood beyond the table with her arms folded. She did not help him.

"The families' names aren't mine to publish. My corrected account is held with the limits of the people whose lives it describes. You can ask about my acts. You can't demand their grief from me."

He stopped. He had more sentences ready, explaining fear and exhaustion. He let them remain unsaid.

The man who had challenged him scratched at his jaw.

"Are you asking us to forgive you?"

"No."

"Good."

Kael stepped aside.

For a time nobody else moved. Then Nera came forward.

"I was on that road," she said.

He looked up.

"I survived. Other people didn't. I'm not telling their lives here today."

She glanced toward the grain carts, making sure the lane was still open.

"I've seen him come back since. That doesn't change what happened then."

There was something more she might have said. Kael could feel the crowd wanting it: forgiveness, condemnation, a sentence they could carry home. Nera returned to her place beside the drivers.

He was left with what she had actually offered.

Senn asked for a stool.

The ferryman brought it. The driver remained beside him when he sat, and Kael saw the young man from the stable clench both hands against his knees.

Senn began with his age.

"I was twenty-two."

"Old enough," someone said.

"Yes."

He described his position, the line he had been ordered to hold and the people who tried to pass it. He could remember the shape of the road better than the faces. When he was uncertain, Vale made him stop and distinguish memory from what he had read afterward.

"You expect us to believe you don't remember?" the young man asked.

"I remember things I would rather not. That doesn't make the rest clear."

"Convenient."

Senn looked at him.

"It is."

A low sound moved through the listeners. Kael could not tell whether it was anger or surprise.

When Senn had finished, his hands were shaking. He put them beneath his thighs.

The ferryman asked whether he was ready to return to the stable.

"Am I dismissed?"

"You've said what you chose to say."

"That isn't what I asked."

"No," the driver said. "You're still in custody."

She helped him stand without loosening her hold.

Rook spoke from his bench because standing made him breathless. He explained the route copy he had given Merrow, the homes he had removed from it and the convoy that had been intercepted.

A trader asked whether he expected anyone to trust him again.

"I'd prefer it," Rook said. "I haven't found a way to require it."

Nobody laughed.

He swallowed and continued.

Anwen described the visit to the shrine, the recovered name, the thread, sending the travelers away while she sought a safer means of containment. She named Bram, Sella and Oren. When she came to the recovery patrol, Vale separated the known sequence from the uncertainty that remained. The wire had been added afterward. It did not prove who had caused the attack.

"You still sent them back onto the road," a woman said.

Anwen gripped the table.

"Yes."

No hidden hand emerged to take the weight from her.

Elna did not speak. Kael saw someone bend toward her with a question and watched her shake her head. The person sat back.

***

Edric listened until the shadows reached the grain wheels.

When he stood, several people shouted at once. He waited. He was good at waiting when others were made to feel disorderly.

"I found a record six years ago," he said. "I believed its release would destroy cooperation between the places that had to keep this region fed."

"You hid it," Vale said.

"I withheld it."

"You ordered it withheld."

"Yes."

She read the relevant line from his order. He did not dispute the hand.

"I had seen villages burn over less," he said.

"And you put the grain behind an answer you wanted," Nessa replied.

"I wanted the road kept safe."

The young man from the stable laughed harshly.

Edric turned toward him, then thought better of it. He looked instead at the public account.

"The old command wasn't mine."

"No," Vale said. "Your order was."

It was the simplest distinction of the day. He could not make it disappear by explaining the first.

They set the terms of the inquiry before he left: the records to be preserved, the store counts to be witnessed, the officials whose actions had to be answered for. Merrow remained among them. He did not inherit Edric's unchecked authority by helping expose it. Edric returned toward the Keep with his guards and a copy of the release order whose restrictions had been read aloud.

People argued as he went. Some thought he should have been seized. Others feared what would happen to the patrols. A man who had shouted for his arrest quietly asked Merrow whether his household could collect tomorrow's ration today.

The answer was yes, if the counted share was there.

The Assembly did not dissolve into agreement. It dissolved into work and grievances and people looking for whoever had moved their stool.

Kael helped fold the awning. His shoulder protested. Vale packed the old command separately from the Refusal Book and handed the second checked packet to Anwen. The human account was larger now. It remained an account made from partial witnesses, papers, surviving marks and the things people had finally chosen to say.

Beyond the village, the road to the Hart waited.

Mira found Kael winding the awning rope.

"How are you?"

He considered lying.

"Sick."

"Good. Sit down before you fall over."

He sat on an empty grain sack. She crouched beside him and put her hand at the back of his neck, where nobody watching could mistake it for an award.

At the cart, a clerk struck the old household condition from another form. The paper tore beneath his pen.

Nessa took it, turned it over and told him to use the other side.


# Chapter 33


## The Hart Remembers


The road narrowed beyond the last house.

Kael had expected it to grow strange by degrees. Instead he crossed a shallow run of water and found that he could no longer hear the village behind him. He looked back. The others were there: Anwen stepping from stone to stone, Nessa with her skirts gathered, Vale protecting a packet beneath her coat. Mira waited while Anwen found her footing.

They continued through trees whose lower branches had grown across the path. Nothing had kept this road convenient. That seemed almost reassuring.

The glade lay below a ridge of dark stone. Water ran through the grass in separate threads. At its center stood the Hart.

Kael stopped.

He had seen animals large enough to overturn a cart, bodies shaped by old violence into things that should not have moved. The Hart made none of those impressions. It was simply too present. The white of its coat held the dim light; its antlers divided the space above it into bare, irregular chambers. When it turned its head, a drop of water fell from one tine.

Nessa let out a slow breath.

"How long?"

The Hart looked at her.

"Since they asked the road to close."

The voice did not come from its mouth in any ordinary way. Kael heard it at the same distance as the animal's breathing. It was neither loud nor soft.

Anwen unfastened the outer wrapping of the command copy.

"We've brought what we recovered."

"I know what you have brought."

Vale's hand tightened on the packet.

"What can you know of it?"

"What was done here. What remains of that doing. I do not know every place your hands have carried those pages."

She nodded, as if it had answered a question about a difficult account.

Kael stepped closer. The grass bent beneath his boots.

"Then show us."

Anwen turned toward him. "Kael—"

"We came to hear."

The Hart lowered its head.

The glade remained around him. He could see the water and the ridge. He could also see the road as it had been.

A cartwheel stood at an angle in a rut. Someone had tied a strip of cloth around the damaged spoke, as though holding the broken pieces together might persuade them to become wood again. A man lay beneath the cart with his sleeves rolled back. He was asking for a tool. No one could find it.

Beyond him people waited with bundles, cages, a door taken from its hinges and laid across two sacks. A child carried a saucepan by the handle. The pan knocked against her knee whenever she walked.

Kael smelled hot wool and an onion crushed underfoot.

The promised gate was ahead.

People were still talking about where they would sleep.

He wanted the memory to move faster. He wanted the killing to arrive because he knew it was coming, and the ordinary delay was worse.

It did not obey him.

A woman argued over the ownership of a blanket. A tired man let a small boy put both feet on his boots while he walked him along the edge of the road. Someone called Avel. Several heads turned.

At the gate, a keeper counted stores.

Kael knew Harl from the surviving descriptions rather than recognition, and the distinction remained even here. The Hart showed a man at the gate doing what the accounts said had been done. Kael did not see the husband Elna had kept in her stories. He saw a hand pull a bar into place while someone on the other side was still asking whether there had been a mistake.

The shrine keepers had asked for concealment.

The promise had included no bloodshed.

He heard it. He could never have repeated the exact sound afterward, but he heard those words accepted. The road folded out of the attention of people who might have found it. People already upon it remained.

Then the soldiers closed the way behind them.

Halvern stood where the track narrowed. He turned toward the men under his command, and for an instant Kael thought refusal might be enough because it was so clear, so plainly a thing another person could hear.

It was not.

He saw the officer seized. He saw the people nearest him understand that the man who had promised them sanctuary could no longer give an order anyone would obey.

The killing was confused.

That was what Kael had least wanted to learn. He could have borne a single command followed by a single terrible act. Instead there were men doing different things at different times: one waiting, another striking too soon, another looking away and then stepping forward. A woman put her bundle down carefully before she ran. Someone tried to climb the closed gate and lost a shoe. The boy on the man's boots was suddenly standing alone.

Kael could not move.

He knew Senn was somewhere along the closed retreat. He could not make the witness arrange itself around the man he knew.

Hands gathered the bodies. Possessions changed owners. A ring would not come from a swollen finger. An argument broke out over a mule. Later came fire, and later the weighing.

A page received a neat category.

Returned.

Kael was on his knees.

Mira had one hand between his shoulders. He could hear Nessa retching behind him. The glade had never vanished. Water soaked through his trousers.

The Hart stood where it had stood before.

"You remember all of it," Vale said.

Her voice shook.

"Yes."

"Every person?"

"Yes."

She began to ask for names.

They stayed until the light changed.

Sometimes the Hart answered in words. Sometimes a particular detail came so sharply that Kael had to close his eyes. Vale wrote what she could. She checked the spellings the Hart gave her, distinguished what it said from what she thought she had seen, stopped when her hand would no longer form the letters.

There were more names than the afternoon could hold. More relationships than names. A person had a way of turning toward another person, a grievance about a borrowed bowl, a tune half learned. Nothing in Vale's pages could carry the whole weight of it.

At last Kael asked the question he had not intended to bring.

"Did people on another road wait for me?"

Mira's hand left his back.

The Hart looked at him.

"I was not on that road."

He swallowed.

"Eight years ago. An escort—"

"I was not there."

Nera had been there. The living woman who sometimes did not want to speak to him knew things this complete witness could not tell.

Kael lowered his head.

***

Anwen spread the human copies on a flat stone where the wet would not reach them.

"We can carry these out," she said. "Tell what we can establish. Keep asking."

Nessa wiped her mouth with the back of her hand.

"Who keeps this place?"

The Hart's ears moved toward the trees.

"The keeping remains while it is carried."

Kael heard the offer within that and felt an old, dangerous relief. A burden with a single bearer. A thing he could take on and be finished deciding.

"I could carry it."

Mira looked at him so sharply that he could not pretend he had spoken only to the Hart.

"What would it take?" she asked.

"My keeping would become his," the Hart said. "The road would hold. He would remain answerable to it."

"For how long?"

"While it is carried."

Kael imagined no more roads chosen for convenience, no more departures he could call temporary until they became permanent. He imagined being unable to leave. Part of him wanted that certainty.

Mira came closer.

"Is that what you want, or does it save you having to choose again?"

He hated the question for its accuracy.

"I don't know."

"Then don't answer quickly."

Anwen had been watching the Hart.

"We could release you."

"Yes."

"And keep the recovered names."

"What you have recovered remains with you."

She looked down at the pages. There would be no public triumph in that, no assurance that people beyond the small circle would learn what had been preserved. They would have to keep it alive in ordinary ways. She seemed to find both mercy and fear in the prospect.

Vale set her pen aside.

"Could we keep asking under different terms? Divide the work?"

Nessa exhaled. "We've already agreed to do half of it."

They gave the Hart the terms from the table at Hearthward. Mira for care, Nessa for stores and passage, Vale for the records. Each spoke for herself. They asked what happened if one withdrew, if somebody fell ill, if no one took a vacant place.

The Hart answered without promising that the vacancy would fill itself.

"The work would wait where the keeping failed."

"And people could still eat," Nessa said.

"Food is yours to give."

"Good. Because I'm not having that answer twice."

Vale asked whether a keeper could be replaced without her knowing.

"No."

"Whether anyone could be entered because they had not refused."

"No."

Mira looked at Kael.

"I would do this," she said. "If we chose it."

Nessa nodded. Vale did too.

There was no defect in the offer he could use as an excuse. It would require meetings, substitutes, days when a crossing closed because no one had freely taken the watch. It might work. They had begun learning how.

Kael walked to the edge of the glade.

His hand touched his sword hilt. Anger still lived beneath the horror: at what had been done, at what had been held here, at the animal whose complete memory made every human account seem small. He could imagine drawing the blade. He could imagine ending the witness because being unable to hear it was easier than deciding what to do with what it knew.

The Hart turned its head.

"If I kill you?"

Mira stepped toward him, then stopped.

"What is held would scatter," it said.

"Would that end the road?"

"The keeping would end. The broken memory would enter what receives it."

The land, Kael thought. The bodies that lived upon it. The next harvest, the next thing hunting beneath the trees.

His hand fell from the hilt.

He returned to the stone.

Siv's household request lay in the packet beside Bera's. They had asked to leave the failing shelter, to take their people to the prepared house. The requests had been read back to them. They had made their marks in front of people who knew what was being asked. Other names had separate answers. Rook's answer was no.

Kael had not brought those pages so a supernatural witness could overrule them.

"What happens if we take them out of the keeping?" he asked. "Open the boundary. Stop making this place depend on it."

Anwen went very still.

The Hart looked toward the stones beneath the ridge.

"The territory would lose its shelter."

"Could we come back?"

"The land would remain."

"To you."

The Hart did not move.

"I would withdraw with what I hold."

Vale's fingers tightened around her pen.

"Could we ask once more after that? If a name wasn't clear?"

"No."

The word reached him more sharply than the vision had.

No later correction from an undying source. No return to ask who had stood beside a person, whether a child had been carried, which voice belonged to a name. Human records would remain human: valuable, checked, incomplete.

Nessa looked at the requests.

"The house is ready."

"The route is ready," Kael said.

"Ready isn't safe."

"No."

Anwen sat on the edge of the stone. Her face had the exhausted look Kael remembered from the chapel.

"People will stay."

"Rook knows."

"Other people."

"We have to tell them plainly."

"And if they won't leave?"

"We don't take them."

Mira drew her coat around her.

She was quiet for a long time. Then she said, "I can do the work at Hearthward. I'll have to come back for people here."

"I know."

She gave him a tired, warning look.

"I heard you," he corrected.

That almost made her smile.

They did not decide by counting raised hands. Each had a different thing to surrender. Vale would lose access to answers she had spent years seeking. Anwen would release the last continuation of work she had inherited and then damaged. Nessa would leave a kitchen fitted to her hands. Mira would have to divide her days between a place she knew and one that would need too much from her.

Kael would lose the possibility that a complete account could somehow finish his work for him.

When they had said enough to understand the choice, he read the household requests aloud.

The Hart listened.

***

They did not pull the stones that evening.
