Nera needed the news. The households needed the departure time. The people staying needed to hear that the shelter would be gone before they watched the others leave.

Vale wrapped her new pages separately. She had written only what she could follow and verify. The packet seemed painfully small.

At the edge of the glade Kael looked back.

The Hart stood among the wet grass, holding more than any of them could take away.

"I haven't asked everything," he said.

"No," it answered.

He waited for reassurance. None came.

Mira took the lantern from him while he checked the path ahead. Behind them Anwen gathered the loose end of her gray thread and wound it around her fingers. Nessa asked how much time they had before the lower road became too dark to see.

They walked out together, carrying the pages they had.


# Chapter 34


## The Last Load


Siv read her request with a finger beneath the words.

Vale had written it. She read it back now without hurrying, stopping at the place where Siv had asked that her father be carried if he could not manage the upper road.

"He can walk some," Siv said. "He won't admit when he can't."

Her father sat beside the cart, a blanket over his knees.

"I can hear you."

"Then save me saying it twice."

Bera had brought her daughter and the two children to the waiting place before dawn. One child held a tin cup. The other held the first child's sleeve and seemed unwilling to exchange it for anything more useful. Bera's own request was shorter. She listened while Vale read it, then asked whether there would be somewhere to dry boots.

"There is," Vale said.

"Write that down. People forget the important bits."

Siv asked her father and sister once more whether they wanted to go. Her sister nodded; her father said he had asked for a bed, not a debate. Bera's daughter had packed before dawn.

They made seven between the two households. Nera counted them aloud and told them where they would wait for the last crossing. No name on the list meant a place in an oath. Each meant a person somebody had to see arrive.

Other departures had been settled separately. Rook's share was stacked in his room in Greyfen. He would help at the landing, then go home. Edric had returned to the Keep. The people who stayed had heard the warning about the ward and received the same release from the grain condition. Some had laughed at the danger. Some had refused to leave houses they had built. Kael could not tell which answer would be regretted first.

At the glade, Anwen carried the iron tools.

The Hart stood beneath the ridge. Kael had wondered whether it would be there. Now that it was, he wished there were a way to delay without calling the delay another choice.

He read the requests.

Siv had asked for a road and a bed for her people. Bera had asked for a place her grandchildren could reach. They had not asked him to bind anyone on their behalf.

Anwen knelt beside the first boundary stone. Its fastening was packed with soil. She scraped it clear, passing the loosened earth into the grass with her bare fingers. Kael set the iron beneath the clasp.

"Here?"

She tested the angle.

"Wait."

He waited.

She unwound the gray thread that had caught around the fastening. It came away in short rotten lengths.

"Now."

The clasp lifted.

A thin light went out beneath the stone. Kael had not noticed it until it was gone.

They worked their way along the boundary. The second fastening resisted. The third had almost eaten itself with corrosion. Anwen cut her thumb and bound it in a clean strip without comment.

When Kael loosened the last stone, the sound of water changed.

That was all. No wall fell. No crown burned above the trees. Somewhere beyond the glade a bird called and another answered from a place that had seemed empty.

The Hart turned.

Vale had told Kael she would think of questions after it was gone. He understood her now with a force that nearly made him call out.

There were names he had failed to hold. Faces which had passed through him and left only the knowledge that someone should remember them.

The Hart walked beneath the branches. Its white body remained visible for a few paces. Then the trunks divided the view, and it was gone.

Anwen stood beside the opened stone.

"Can you feel it?"

"No."

She looked at her bandaged thumb.

"Neither can I."

They gathered the tools. He did not put the fastening back.

***

At Lantern Reach the water was already touching the lower landing board.

The rain had been falling in the upper country for days. Here it came in bursts, leaving the air bright for an hour before the next gray curtain moved down the valley. The current carried branches with their leaves still attached. Kael watched one turn over and reveal a pale torn root.

The broad grain lighter had made two crossings that morning. Its guide rope stretched upstream between the new post on the Greyfen bank and the great willow opposite; the separate hauling line ran through the bank blocks. The river carried everything loose southward.

On the far bank, a train of people and carts waited to take the rising road toward Hearthward. Tor was there with Toma, keeping the wheels on the firm ground. Pell had gone with the earlier group. Mira and Nessa were at the refuge, where the first arrivals needed dry clothes and a meal. Vale's checked packets had traveled ahead under Anwen's agreed custody arrangements; one protected copy was already behind a locked door.

The complete witness had left with the Hart. The packets did not change that.

Nera stood by the near landing with the end of a rope in her hands.

"You're late."

"The last stone held."

"Did you finish?"

"Yes."

She looked once toward the road they had come from.

"Then we work with this."

Rook sat on a box beside the loading list. He had wrapped a broad strip of cloth around his chest. Bracken was under the box, attempting to keep every part of his small plated body out of the wet.

Kael crouched and coaxed him out with a warm corner of his cloak. Bracken climbed against him, claws finding painful purchase.

"Traitor," Rook said.

"He likes people who can lift him."

"I can lift him."

Nera pointed to the next load.

"Kael."

He put Bracken inside an empty basket lined with sacking. The animal objected, then discovered it was dry.

The load included Senn and his two guards. The ferryman had inspected the landing and the lines before taking his place. His bad knee made the step down awkward.

Senn offered him an arm.

The man looked at it.

"Hold the rail instead. You're in my way."

Senn held the rail.

Nera checked his answer once more, quietly enough that the crowd did not get another performance.

"You're choosing to go?"

"Yes."

"Your custody goes with you."

"Yes."

She marked the list.

The children from Bera's household watched him with open curiosity. One asked why a man his age needed people to tell him where to stand. Bera turned the child toward the baggage and told her to count cups.

They loaded blankets, tools, a small chest of medicines and a few sacks of grain. Four adults with freight: Kael, Senn and his two guards. Anwen would follow with Nera on the next crossing. Kael wanted to add another sack. The ferryman shook his head.

"Weight's wrong."

"It'll fit."

"That isn't what I said."

Kael set the sack down.

He had heard that sentence often enough lately to begin respecting it.

The crossing took longer than the first trial had. The current pushed against the ferry until the timbers creaked. Kael held the chest between his knees and kept his weight where the ferryman told him. The lighter was eight paces long; he had room to move, but each movement changed how it sat. From the landing Nera watched the guide rope instead of waving.

Halfway across, a piece of floating timber struck the upstream side.

The ferryman shouted. Kael looked where he pointed and saw another length coming, heavier, with a forked branch dragging beneath it.

"Push it clear."

Kael took the pole. The first touch nearly wrenched it from his hands. He let it slide, caught the end again and pressed at the angle the ferryman showed him.

The branch passed.

Senn had braced himself against the medicine chest. The driver kept a hand on his shoulder, holding him as much as guarding him.

When they reached the far landing, everyone wanted to stand at once.

"Sit down," the ferryman said.

They sat.

Kael carried the chest up the slope. Vale was leaving with the next uphill cart, and he handed her Bracken's basket. She wedged it securely beside her feet, beneath a dry cover, and took him toward Hearthward with a promise of another warm stone.

Tor pointed Kael toward a dry place beneath the awning. He could see the work of the earlier crews everywhere: the cleared turn, the staked edge, gravel spread where the wheels would cut in. Without it they would have been dragging each load out by hand.

"Water's eating the lower side," Tor said.

"I saw."

"No. Behind you."

Kael turned. Beneath the landing, the current had begun to take soil from under a supporting edge. A dark pocket opened and closed as the water surged.

Tor could not fix it with his left wrist bound against his chest. He could see precisely what needed doing. Kael watched the frustration work through him.

"Show us," he said.

Tor pointed.

They wedged timber where they could, shifted the unloading point and lightened the next return. The work bought room. It did not lower the water.

On the next crossing Anwen came with Nera and three people carrying light bundles. One woman was sick over the side and apologized repeatedly. Anwen moved her own coat out of the way and stayed beside her. Behind them Rook took charge of the waiting list on the Greyfen bank.

***

By midafternoon the outer road had become a line of small delays.

A cartwheel jammed against a stone. A child would not leave a dropped shoe. An old woman had to sit every hundred paces, and each stop brought people behind her to a halt. None of it looked like a great migration from inside the work. It looked like someone needing another pair of hands.

Kael walked the first steep stretch with a cart and returned to the landing before the last ferry journey.

The sky had darkened.

Across the river he could see Siv's carrying pole and Bera's pale headcloth. Rook raised a hand from beside the list. There were seven people still waiting to cross, not counting the workers who would remain on that bank.

Nera read the water.

"We bring them now. Belongings after if we can."

"Their bedding—"

"I said after."

The ferryman tested the line. He frowned.

Something upstream had shifted.

Kael heard it before he saw it: a scraping, woody crash, then the slow rotation of a whole section of bank. A tree came with it. The roots stood above the water, packed with earth.

The ferryman shouted for the line to be cleared.

There was no time to clear it completely.

The tree struck the water below the bend and rolled. A branch caught, held, and pulled. The ferry lurched against its fastenings. The saturated ground beneath the far willow shifted. Its great strop dragged against the exposed roots, and the upstream guide attachment tore free with a report like a snapped bone.

Kael dropped to his knees and caught the nearest rope.

It burned through his hands.

"Let it run!" Nera shouted.

He let it run. The rope whipped past him, struck the landing and vanished into the current.

The ferry swung downstream until the separate hauling line took its weight. For an instant Kael thought it would turn broadside. The ferryman threw himself toward the outer edge, Nera moving with him. Their weight steadied it enough to keep the low side from going under.

On the opposite bank, Rook was waving both arms.

Kael could not hear him.

Siv pulled her father back from the edge. Bera's daughter took one child under each arm. They climbed toward the old store shed as the lower landing board lifted beneath a rush of water.

Kael stood.

"Don't," Nera said.

He had not yet moved.

"You can't swim that."

"I can take the upper path."

"You can't reach them before dark by the upper path. The old footway's under already."

The facts came one after another. He hated each one and knew that hating it altered nothing.

They still had the ferry. They had the hauling line and enough rope in reserve to make a temporary return if they changed the approach. They had people who knew the landing. They had daylight, less of it with each minute.

The ferryman wiped water from his eyes.

"We can land below the shed. Won't take a cart."

"We don't need a cart," Nera said.

She turned to Kael.

"You go with him. Clear the landing. Bring the seven down in the order I give you. Nobody moves because somebody shouts louder."

He looked across the water.

Rook had stopped waving. He was helping Siv spread a blanket where her father could sit. Above them the store shed's dark doorway held shapes Kael could not distinguish.

Nera gripped his sleeve.

"Listen."

He turned back.

She gave him the order.

He repeated it until she was satisfied.


# Chapter 35


## The Man Who Came Back


The ferryman made Kael take off his pack.

"Leave the weight."

"My rope's in it."

"Take the rope."

Kael emptied what he needed onto the wet boards. He put the wrapped token inside his coat, close to his body. His bow went across his back. Nera watched him fasten the strap, then handed him a coil.

"Four on the first load. Bera's household. Three on the second. You don't add anything because it looks small."

He nodded.

"Say it."

He said it.

The empty lighter rode high and badly. Without its upstream guide it wanted to turn downriver. The ferryman worked the pole while the men on the eastern bank paid out the hauling line. Kael held the reserve rope clear of their feet.

Water struck the side and came aboard.

"Ignore that," the ferryman said.

Kael ignored it.

They reached the western bank below the ruined landing. The soil there was firmer, held by an old retaining wall that had outlasted whatever building once stood above it. Kael jumped ashore with the rope and nearly went down on the slick stones.

Rook caught his sleeve.

"For once," he said breathlessly, "I'm going to recommend arriving with less enthusiasm."

Kael found the old guide post still firm. Together they brought the reserve line to it. The ferryman tested the new angle, swore, shifted it and tested again. Across the water Nera's crew took up the slack.

It would bring them in lower than the original landing. It would keep the ferry from turning if the line held.

Nobody said it would hold.

Bera was waiting with her family.

"Which child first?" Kael asked.

"This one," her daughter said.

The older child objected.

"Let her object aboard," Bera said.

They moved down the wall one at a time. Kael stood below them, taking hands. Bera's split boot caught between two stones. She jerked it free and almost struck him in the face.

"I'd apologize," she said, "but I'm busy."

Her daughter passed down the younger child. Kael felt the small arms lock around his neck.

"Don't climb me. I'm not a tree."

The child tightened her grip.

Bera peeled her fingers loose.

"That's a very poor tree, anyway."

They settled near the middle of the lighter. The children sat between the two women. Bera's daughter held both of them, and Bera held the side with her knotted hands.

Siv stood above them.

"We'll have him down when you come back."

Kael looked toward the shed. Her father sat in the doorway. Her sister had wrapped his feet in sacking to keep them dry.

"I'll come back."

Siv's face altered.

He wished he had said something more useful.

"Bring the board from under that broken cart," he added. "We can sit him on it and move him along the stones. Don't bring the chair."

Her father heard.

"It's a good chair."

"It's a bad boat," the ferryman said.

They pushed off.

With six bodies aboard, the lighter sat deeper. The current made a different sound against it. Kael could feel the strain through his boots.

The older child began to count.

"What are you counting?" Bera asked.

"Waves."

"Choose something that finishes."

The child counted the people instead. When she reached six, she began again.

Halfway across, the ferry checked hard against the new line. A floating branch caught beneath the bow and dragged there. The ferryman leaned on his pole. His knee buckled.

Kael caught him by the coat.

"Stay where you are," he told the others.

Nobody had moved.

He let the ferryman regain his footing, then worked the branch clear while the man held them against the pull. For a moment the pole bent enough to make Kael think it would break. The branch slid free.

Bera's daughter was crying without making a sound.

Kael kept his eyes on the eastern bank.

Nera stood below the awning, one boot in the water. She caught the line and drew them toward the new landing place with the others. Tor shouted where they should put their feet. He could do nothing with his broken wrist, and the fury in his voice made his instructions exceptionally clear.

The children went ashore first.

Nera counted them as they passed. Then the women. She did not put a mark beside Bera's name until the older woman stood above the water.

Bera turned back.

"The chair?"

"No," Kael said.

"Good. He'd make us carry it forever."

Nera gave Kael a cup. Water, with a little honey dissolved in it.

"Drink."

"We should—"

"Drink it while he resets the line."

He drank.

They went back.

***

The western bank had changed during the crossing.

Water now ran behind the old store shed. The door hung open. Rook stood beside the retaining wall with a long pole angled toward the trees.

Kael saw the Stalker only when it moved.

It had come down through the brush above the shed, following the noise and the smell of animals and food. Its body stayed low, the long forelimbs placing themselves with an appalling delicacy. One side of its head was scarred pale. It watched Siv's father rather than the people able to move away.

Siv had put herself in front of him.

"Down here," Kael called.

Her sister tried to lift the old man from the board. He cried out.

The creature's head turned.

Rook pushed the pole toward it. It struck the wood aside with such force that he folded around his ribs.

Kael drew his sword.

He did not charge. There was nowhere useful to charge. The shed wall was on his right, water below him, the family above the stones. If he drove the creature uphill, it might circle behind them. He needed it looking at him.

He struck the blade against the iron lip of a discarded pot.

The Stalker flinched.

Kael stepped toward the shed, keeping its wall at his side.

"Come on."

Its mouth opened. The sound it made was brief and raw.

Rook tried to raise the pole again.

"Get them down," Kael said.

The Stalker came.

It covered the space in two movements, one low, one rising. Kael cut at the reaching limb and felt the blade meet bone. The force knocked his arm across his body. He struck the wall with his shoulder.

The creature landed beside him.

He turned. A claw caught the back of his calf.

Pain opened so suddenly that his leg failed before he understood it had been cut. He dropped to one knee. The next reaching hand struck the wall above his head.

He drove the sword upward with both hands.

The Stalker recoiled, twisting against the blade. Kael pulled free and fell sideways. Its weight struck the loose edge of the bank. Earth slid beneath it.

For an instant it hung there, one forelimb hooked over a stone.

Kael could have struck again. Instead he crawled toward the family.

Behind him the stone came away. The creature disappeared into the brown water.

He heard it once, farther downriver.

Then he heard Siv telling her father to stop arguing.

Rook and her sister had moved the board to the head of the retaining wall. The old man gripped its edges. His legs shook. Kael tried to stand and felt warmth run into his boot.

Rook saw it.

"That's bad."

"It'll wait."

"It doesn't look as if it knows."

The ferryman had wedged the lighter against the lower stones. He could not leave it. Across the river, Nera shouted something that the water took apart.

Kael put a hand beneath the board.

Siv slapped it away.

"Sit down."

"I can help."

"You can fall on him. Sit."

Her anger was so immediate that he obeyed.

Rook took one end with Siv's sister. Siv steadied her father's shoulders and moved backward down the stones. They did it badly, slowly, with stops and curses. The board caught. The old man accused them of trying to tip him in. His daughters told him, together, that they would have done it years ago if that were their intention.

Kael laughed once. It hurt enough to stop him.

They got the board to the ferry.

Siv's father stepped aboard with the ferryman holding him and his daughters behind him. Kael followed last. His wounded leg dragged.

His bow caught.

He turned. The strap had snagged beneath a broken projection of timber at the wall, pulled tight as the lighter moved away. He reached for the buckle and nearly fell.

"Leave it," the ferryman said.

Kael tried once more.

The boat shifted. Siv's father cried out. Kael drew his knife and cut the strap.

The bow fell between the lighter and the stones. He saw the polished curve vanish in the current.

Rook crouched at the edge, holding the line with both hands.

"Go."

"Your way back—"

"Upper village road. On this side. Two drivers are waiting. Go before you ask me to carry you."

Kael looked at him.

Rook's face was gray with pain. His mouth twitched.

"I know where I live."

They pushed off.

***

Kael sat on the wet boards with his leg straight in front of him.

Siv bound the cut with a strip torn from the bottom of her skirt. She pulled until he swore.

"Good," she said. "Still awake."

Her father watched with grave interest.

"That wants stitching."

"Thank you," Kael said.

"I had one like it."

"I believe you."

"On my hand."

Siv tightened the cloth again.

The ferry moved through the current. Kael could no longer tell how much of the shudder came from the line and how much from his own body. He watched the eastern bank without trusting the distance. Once he thought they were nearly there and found there was still a broad strip of water between them.

The ferryman told him to keep talking.

"I've nothing to say."

"Then complain."

"My bow's gone."

"Good start."

"It was a good bow."

"Expensive?"

"Yes."

"Then keep going."

Kael complained about the bow. Its balance, the hours he had spent repairing the grip, the price of replacing it. Siv's sister began to laugh. She looked ashamed and laughed harder.

When they reached the eastern landing, Kael tried to rise before the line was secured.

Nera put a hand on his chest and pushed him back down.

"Stay."

"Siv—"

"I can see her."

"Her father—"

"I can see all three."

She counted them off the ferry. Three people who had been on the wrong side of the water were on the right one now.

Then she came back for him.

Toma took him beneath the arms. Tor helped support his legs with his good hand, swearing at anybody who asked whether he should. Anwen met them above the landing. She cut away Kael's soaked trouser leg.

The wound was longer than he had thought.

He looked away.

"Tell me," he said.

"It's deep," she answered. "Keep still."

She pressed a clean pad against it. He heard his own breath turn ugly.

Nera knelt beside him.

"Seven," she said.

He looked at her.

"Seven across. Bera's people and Siv's."

For a moment he could do nothing with the number. Then he nodded.

They had lost the chair, a bedding chest, two sacks of grain and much of the last load of tools. Rook would stay on the old side with the goods they could shelter there. The ward would not return to cover the road behind him.

The complete witness had gone. No one had fetched it back.

The people in front of Kael were cold, wet and alive.

***

They could not reach Hearthward that night.

Nera refused to move the slowest people along the upper bend in darkness. They took shelter in a roadside store where the roof still held. Toma and the drivers brought the carts in as close as they could. Tor directed a brace beneath one of the roof beams and allowed someone else to hammer it.

Anwen cleaned Kael's leg with boiled water.

He gripped the edge of the bench.

"Don't apologize," he said.

"I wasn't going to."

She worked in the light of two lamps. Siv sat beside him, mending the place she had torn her skirt. Her father had been given the least uncomfortable corner and was complaining about the absence of his chair.

Bera told him there were no chairs worth discussing in the whole building.

The children slept.

By morning Kael could not put weight on the leg. They placed him in a cart with the remaining bedding and lashed a board beside the wheel so he could keep it raised. He hated the arrangement immediately.

Nera inspected the fastening.

"Complain to the ferryman. He's had practice."

The road to Hearthward was slow. At the bend above the spring, the wheels found the gravel they had laid days earlier. People took the turn one at a time. No one had to invent a safe line while carrying an old man.

Pell was waiting at the house.

She ran toward Toma, then stopped when she saw Kael in the cart.

"That's stupid," she said.

"I agree."

Mira came through the doorway with her sleeves already rolled.

Nera stood beside the arrival table and read the names and numbers as people entered. Bera went in with her daughter and both children. Siv followed with her father between her and her sister. The old man asked where he should sit.

Nessa pointed.

He considered the chair she had found.

"It'll do."

Only when they were all inside did Nera draw the final mark.

Mira climbed onto the cart beside Kael.

"How bad?"

"I've been advised not to answer that."

"Sound advice."

She looked at the bandage, then at Anwen.

Together they moved him into the room with the repaired window.


# Chapter 36


## The Relief Handover


Mira took twelve stitches in Kael's leg.

He counted the first six because it gave him something to do. After that he lost count and asked Anwen, who told him to stop asking questions whose answers would not help.

The room smelled of boiled cloth and the bitter wash Mira used on the wound. Beyond the window someone was unloading a cart. Each time a wheel struck the stone lip of the yard, Kael's body tightened.

"That has nothing to do with your leg," Mira said.

"I know."

She paused with the needle.

He shut his mouth.

When she was finished, she sat back on her heels. There was blood on one sleeve. Her hair had escaped its binding and stuck to the damp skin at her temple.

"You were lucky."

"It doesn't feel like it."

"The worst of the cut missed what you need to stand. There's torn muscle. You'll need time, and I don't know how much. If it heats or you start shaking, you tell someone."

He looked at the bandage.

"What can I do?"

"Today?"

"Yes."

"Lie there."

"Tomorrow?"

She looked so tired that he regretted the joke before it became one.

"I mean it," she said.

He reached for her hand. She let him hold it until someone called from the doorway.

Siv's father was asking whether the chair could be moved away from the draft.

Mira closed her eyes.

Anwen went to find him another place.

***

Nera completed the arrival count before she ate.

Seven from the two households. All living. One older man who needed help on the stairs, one child with a cough, wet clothes, lost bedding, the father's chair gone. She wrote the losses beneath the arrivals where they could not be mistaken for missing people.

Then the other departures: who had reached Hearthward, who had stopped on the road, who remained by choice in Greyfen. Senn in the small guarded room by the yard. His guards paid through the end of the week, the ferryman's knee to be looked at. Rook on the western bank, taking the village road with two drivers. A message still needed to confirm they were home.

She left that line open.

The urge to finish it was almost physical.

She put the pen down.

Siv came through the doorway carrying two buckets. Nera took one without asking. They brought water to the kitchen, where Nessa was separating what could be cooked that evening from what had to last.

"How many?"

Nera told her.

"Again. Slowly."

She repeated the count.

Nessa made three small piles of counters on the table, frowned, then combined two of them.

"All right. Tell them it'll be late."

Siv laughed.

"They're here. They can wait an hour."

Nera looked toward the room where Kael lay. She could see only his boot beyond the door.

The sight brought back Jem's shoes.

Not the day he died. A summer before it, when he had stolen her better pair because they made a louder sound on the road. He had stuffed the toes with cloth and strutted past Leth's washing as if he had been appointed to an important office.

Her mother had made him finish the washing in them.

He had been furious. Nera had laughed so hard she could not help him when he tried to pull them off.

She stood with the bucket handle biting into her fingers.

Siv touched her elbow.

"Put it down."

Nera did.

There were days when memory arrived wearing the shape of useful work. There were days it simply stood in the doorway and stopped her.

She washed her face at the pump. The cold water hurt her teeth. When she came back, Nessa had saved her a piece of bread with the browned end she preferred.

"Sit."

Nera sat.

***

By the third day Kael was allowed to work at a table, provided someone brought the table to him.

He suspected this concession had been made for Mira's peace rather than his recovery.

Vale arrived with a board across two trestles, Merrow with ink, and Nera with the departure sheets. They set them within reach of the bed.

"Read it back," Nera said.

He read the names and numbers. She checked each against the count made at the landing and the count at the door.

There were discrepancies in the goods. A sack recorded as meal had contained bran. One family's blankets had been entered among another's. A box of iron fittings remained on the wrong bank, if it had not been washed away. They could settle some errors. Others stayed marked uncertain.

"You'll need those fittings," Kael said.

"I know."

"The ferry—"

"Needs work. Tor's making a list."

From the next room came Tor's voice.

"Somebody has to."

Kael smiled.

A driver had brought word from Greyfen that morning. Rook and the two men had reached the village. He had put the remaining goods under cover and sent an itemized account of what he believed belonged to whom. At the bottom he had charged nothing for storage.

Nera read that line twice, then placed the sheet with the goods records.

She did not write forgiven beside it.

Merrow waited while Vale checked his copy of the new ration order. At the Assembly he had surrendered the authority to make a refusal disappear beneath an official phrase. Here he had been given the unglamorous task of making every visible entry readable to someone who had not worked in his office.

He had difficulty with it.

"This abbreviation is standard."

"Where?" Nera asked.

"In the stores."

"We're in a house."

He wrote the words out.

His cut lip had begun to heal. It pulled when he spoke. Kael watched him press a damp cloth to it, careful not to let blood touch the page. His care for paper was older than his care for the consequences of what he put on it.

It might become useful. Nobody at the table treated that as a settled fact.

When they finished, Vale separated the arrival record from the protected statements. Merrow reached for both bundles, then stopped and took only the one she offered.

The command copy, the suppression order and the Refusal Book would be available to the inquiry under the custody they had agreed. So would Senn's account. No one could now ask the Hart to settle a doubtful word.

Kael thought of the glade.

"Did you remember another question?" Vale asked.

"Yes."

"So did I."

She tied the packet.

***

Senn asked to see the yard.

The driver opened the door and stood beside him. He came out slowly, blinking at the light. The ferryman remained seated on a bench with his leg stretched in front of him.

Kael watched from the washhouse window.

For a few minutes Senn did nothing. He looked at the wagons, the ditch, the stairs people had built from loose boards to make unloading easier. Bera crossed the yard with her broom. She moved around him without speaking.

"Could I have a task?" he asked the driver.

She considered it.

"You could carry water."

The ferryman said, "Not with your hands free and me sitting here."

Senn waited.

They found a pail small enough to be carried between the house and the pump with the driver beside him. It was useful work and humiliatingly slow. Nobody called it restitution for thirty-two years.

Nessa gave him the same stew as everyone else.

Later a man came to the door asking whether Senn would answer a question about his grandfather. The driver asked Senn. He said yes. They sat where others could see them, and Vale brought a clean page.

Kael could not hear the question from his bed.

He left the window open.

***

Nera brought the handover to the evening meal.

There were three routes on the board: the damaged ferry and the road back to Greyfen, the supply journey to the mill, and a smaller run to the outlying shelters where people had not joined either the Assembly or the departure. The first two had crews. She was taking the third.

"When?" Kael asked.

"Five days, if the road dries enough."

"You're tired."

"Yes."

"You could wait."

"I might."

She ate a spoonful of stew.

He looked at the route. There were places on it he knew. Places he could have offered to walk with her if he had not been sitting with his leg on a stool.

She saw him measuring the distance.

"You can't come."

"I hadn't asked."

"Your face had."

He looked down.

She set her spoon beside the bowl.

"I've got two drivers. One knows the upper road better than either of us. Siv wants to take the first run to the mill once her father's settled. She'll need a second carrier."

"Ask Toma."

"I have."

"Right."

For a while they ate. The noise around them was ordinary and dense: arguments about salt, somebody coughing, a child laughing so hard he dropped a piece of bread. The house still leaked near the pantry. A bucket had been placed beneath the drip, and each drop made a distinct sound.

Nera turned the handover sheet toward him.

"You're down for the first stores check tomorrow. Sitting."

"I can do that."

"With Merrow. Vale checks after."

Kael imagined the morning. Merrow's abbreviations. The smell of damp grain. His leg aching on the stool. It was an unheroic prospect, and for once he did not need to make it larger.

"I'll be there."

Nera looked at him, then at the page.

"Good."

When the others began clearing bowls, she stayed.

Kael kept his hands around his cup.

"Do you want company?"

"Tonight," she said.

"Tonight."

She moved the spare stool closer to the door so they could watch the yard without being in the way. He shifted his bad leg with both hands. It was clumsy, and she waited until he had finished before sitting.

They talked about the ferry.

Then about the ferryman, who had offered detailed criticism of everyone else's ropework while refusing to rest his knee. Then about Rook's bill for nothing, which Nera distrusted so strongly that Kael laughed.

She laughed too, once.

When she left, it was because she wanted to sleep. She took her own bowl to the kitchen.

***

Mira found Kael still by the door.

"You were supposed to call someone when you wanted to move."

"I haven't moved."

"That was beautifully literal."

She fetched Toma to help him back to the washhouse. Pell carried his cup with an importance that made everyone else step aside.

When they were alone, Mira sat on the edge of the bed.

"The room upstairs is free."

"The cold one?"

"It has a door. Two shelves. A window that shuts when you kick the frame."

"Luxury."

"I was thinking I could keep my private things there. The medicines stay here. I need some part of a day when every object I touch isn't somebody's treatment."

Kael nodded.

"Would you want me there?"

"Sometimes."

He smiled.

"Sometimes sounds good."

"And when you're fit, you'll have to find work that doesn't depend on falling into rivers."

"I was in a boat."

"I've heard the distinction."

He reached for her. She leaned against him, careful of his leg.

They stayed that way while the house settled. From the long room came Elna's voice, singing a verse Kael had never heard and which Nessa loudly claimed had not been part of the original song. Tor laughed, then complained about his wrist. Anwen went upstairs with a basket of clean linen. The iron pan by the door hung still.

Mira lifted her head.

"I need to check the old man's chest."

"Siv's father?"

"Yes."

"He's going to ask about the chair."

"He already has."

She kissed him and stood.

Kael picked up the little wooden spoon from beside his bowl. There was still a rough place beneath its handle. He rubbed it with his thumb while he listened to Mira cross the yard.

Tomorrow there were stores to count. He set the spoon where he would find it in the morning.


# Epilogue


## The Road Between Crowns


Six months after the crossing, Kael could walk to the spring without a stick if nobody had put a cart in the way.

On cold mornings the scar along his calf drew tight. On other mornings he forgot it until he tried to turn too quickly. Mira had stopped asking whether it hurt. She could tell by the way he put his boot on.

He sat on the edge of their bed and worked his heel into the leather.

"You could loosen it."

"Then it slips."

"There's a position between crushed and loose."

"I've heard of it."

She watched him for a moment, then returned to fastening her own boot. Above her shelf hung a drawing of salt-growing plants. She had arranged three weeks on the coast after the summer planting, paid a deposit with money she had put aside herself, and found people willing to share her work while she was gone.

Today they were going to Greyfen with Rook's cart. The journey would take most of the day, and they would stay overnight before coming back.

Downstairs, Nessa had left bread beneath an upturned bowl. She had also left a note saying it was for the journey, not for everyone who passed the table. Somebody had drawn a mouse beside the words.

Kael knew Pell's hand.

Outside, the garden below the spring had begun to green. Toma knelt between two beds, moving stones which seemed to return whenever he looked away. Pell was meant to be helping. She was conducting an examination of a Gallows Weaver's web beneath the eaves.

"Don't put your face in it," he called.

"I'm looking."

"Look from farther away."

"Then I can't see."

Kael took the smaller bag from Mira.

Rook waited by the cart with a list.

"You've put on weight," he said.

"The leg?"

"The luggage."

"Mira's going to work."

"That usually means the luggage improves on the return journey. Empty jars are lighter."

His ribs had healed. He still touched them when he laughed too hard, as if checking that the repair remained in place. He had brought oil, cloth, seed and two household messages from Greyfen. Nera had checked the seals on the messages before they were delivered. He had waited without making a joke.

Now he looked at Kael's limp.

"I've put a cushion in."

"That'll cost me."

"It already has."

They set off after breakfast. Bracken rode in a basket between the bags, warmed by a stone wrapped in cloth. He was larger than he had been in autumn. Copper showed through the ash-dark plates along his back when the light caught him.

Rook claimed the animal had become too expensive to transport.

Bracken slept through the discussion.

***

The ferry had a new guide line and a notice stating its permitted load.

Tor had dictated the notice while someone else painted it. Beneath the words was a small picture of a sinking boat, added after the ferryman said most of the people who needed the warning would not read it.

The far-bank willow remained, scarred where the old attachment had torn. The new fastening stood farther back on firmer ground. Every rain meant another inspection.

Kael stepped from the cart before they loaded it. He could make the crossing sitting, but he preferred to see the line.

The ferryman looked at his leg.

"You still complaining about the bow?"

"I've bought another."

"That's unfortunate. I liked the old complaint."

It was a plain crossing. Kael watched the water pass beneath them and felt his body prepare for a danger which did not arrive. He let it prepare. He had work to do on the other side whether his pulse believed the ferry or not.

Greyfen smelled of wet earth and chimney smoke.

The ward had not risen again. Nothing hid the road from those who meant to find it. People had cut back the brush near the approaches, repaired the gates and paid for watches whose hours had to be filled by someone awake. There had been losses in winter: stolen animals, a ruined store roof, journeys delayed when the patrols could not cover every road. People complained about the new arrangements as freely as they had complained about the old.

Rook's room was still his.

His storeroom had acquired better shelves and a chair he refused to let customers use while bargaining. He said sitting made them ambitious.

Mira left for a house near the well. There were people she visited each time she came, and a few who waited too long between visits because they disliked owing a healer anything. She had learned to accept eggs, mended cloth and occasionally nothing, while refusing the claim that care gave anybody a right to her whole day.

Kael went to the graves.

He carried the token in its old wrapping. The red cord had faded. The black crow remained, with the restored name visible beneath the paint.

AVEL.

Bram Kett. Sella Vey. Oren.

He spoke the three travelers' names quietly. They had become familiar in public accounts, attached to the beginning of a story people told with different emphases. Here he tried to remember the particular things that did not fit a heading: the cart ledger's battered corners, the red-thread feathers, a child taking the trouble to restore a name someone else had covered.

Rook came to stand beside him.

"Someone tried to sell me a left glove last week," he said.

Kael looked at him.

"Just one. As if I might have learned nothing from Bram."

"Thirty-seven."

"He said thirty-seven. I never counted."

"You counted what he owed you."

"Of course."

Rook put his hands in his coat pockets.

"He sold them as polishing cloths. Looked so pleased when I finally worked it out."

Kael smiled.

For a while they stood without talking.

The token would remain protected, available to the people responsible for its care. Kael had brought a different crow for the grave: a small wooden one Pell had carved, blunt at the beak, painted black by Mira after Pell grew impatient with the drying. Tor had made the little bracket that held it.

Kael fixed it beside the names.

The child was dead. No voice rose through the stone. The crow sat crookedly until Rook crouched and adjusted the bracket.

"There."

It still looked like something a child had made.

Kael preferred it that way.

***

They returned to Hearthward two days later with less medicine, more empty jars and a packet of checked store accounts.

Edric remained at the Keep. The inquiry had separated what he had done from the old command he had hidden. He had lost the sole power to put a household condition between people and grain. His arguments about roads, guards and winter stores still had to be answered because some of them described real needs. They were no longer accepted merely because he made them beneath his seal.

Merrow's handwriting appeared throughout the accounts. So did the names of the people who had checked his entries. There were corrections. He had stopped scratching out the old words until nothing could be read beneath them.

At Hearthward, Vale took the packet from Kael and immediately found a quantity she wanted explained.

"Tomorrow," Mira said.

Vale looked offended.

"Tomorrow," Kael agreed.

She considered them, then put the packet on the closed shelf.

Pell called her to a game.

Vale asked whether pieces were still allowed to travel underneath one another.

"Only the small ones."

"That wasn't the rule before."

"They've grown."

Vale sat down.

Tor was in the yard fitting a new chair for Siv's father. He could use his left hand now, though the wrist remained stiff and sometimes swelled after work. He held a joint in place while Toma drove the peg.

"Less."

Toma stopped.

"Now more."

"Make up your mind."

"It's a matter of degree."

"Everything is, with you."

Siv's father tried the chair before the last peg was trimmed.

"Back's too straight."

Tor looked at him.

"Your old one went in the river. You can fetch it if you prefer."

The old man settled more deeply.

"This will do."

Siv arrived from the mill road with another carrier. She had taken the regular run for the spring, leaving water at the shelters that could not easily reach the supply carts. Her father complained about her absence, then asked when she was going again so he could arrange a game with the ferryman.

Bera had new boots.

She showed them to Kael without waiting to be asked. They were sound, plain and already dirty.

"Still need a better ditch," she said.

"It works."

"So do I. We can both improve."

Her grandchildren ran past them toward the garden. She shouted after them to leave Toma's seedlings alone.

Inside, Elna was lending Anwen a needle and making sure she understood that lending was a temporary arrangement. Harl's button remained in the cupboard beneath the stairs. Elna had given a fuller account of what she knew to the inquiry. She had kept the blue cloth, the comb and the ordinary memories that no questioner needed to own.

Anwen had spent the afternoon showing two people how to bind a sprained hand. She no longer took every sick person into her own keeping. Sometimes she still woke at a sound in the night and went to the door before she knew what she expected to find there.

She had told Mira about it.

That had helped more than Kael would once have understood.

Senn lived in guarded custody beyond the yard. His hearings had begun. He had answered some questions, failed to answer others, disputed a memory, corrected his own statement. The inquiry had found no reason to release him because he had cooperated. People who had lost families were not required to sit with him.

Kael passed the window and saw him repairing a torn grain sack while the driver read aloud from a page.

He kept walking.

***

Nera was leaving in the morning.

She had taken the longer spring route for herself. There were loads to collect, shelter crews to pay, two roads she wanted to compare and a market where she intended to buy a coat without somebody explaining that the old one still worked.

"What's wrong with the old one?" Rook asked.

"I dislike it."

"An excellent purchasing position."

She had arranged the handover before supper. Another driver held the local routes. The ferryman knew who would inspect the lines. Siv had the mill run. No one waited for Nera to remain at the center simply because she had once been necessary there.

Kael helped load the light bags. His leg allowed that much.

"How long?" he asked.

"Three weeks. Perhaps four if the upper road's bad."

"I'll keep your place at the table."

"Don't. Someone might want to sit there."

He smiled.

She checked the strap beneath his hand, gave it a firm pull and moved to the next.

Later they ate outside because the evening was warm enough. Nessa had taken the afternoon off and returned in a clean dress, furious to discover that someone had used her good knife to cut cord. She accepted the first bowl served to her and made no attempt to stand when the second pot needed carrying.

Mira sat beside Kael. Beneath the table she put a foot against his good one.

The small wooden spoon was in his bowl. Its rough place had worn smooth.

At the gate, a cart arrived after most of the dishes had been cleared. Rook recognized the driver and stood to ask what had delayed him. A broken strap, then a detour around a fallen tree. There were two tired passengers, a bundle of bedding and a pot whose lid had been tied on with string.

Nera began to rise.

Siv touched her shoulder and went instead.

Kael finished his last mouthful. He set down the bowl and took his stick from beneath the bench.

By the time he reached the cart, Mira was at the other side. They lifted the bedding between them. It was heavier than it looked, badly balanced, the string cutting into his fingers.

"Ready?" she asked.

He shifted his grip.

"Yes."

They carried it through the door together.
